CPT Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Return to supply to gather the missing tube before proceeding further): confirming the requisition and assembling every needed supply before approaching the patient prevents an interrupted draw and a second unnecessary puncture. Leaving mid-procedure to fetch equipment increases patient anxiety and the risk of a compromised specimen. Complete preparation is a foundational step taught before any patient contact begins. A well-stocked tray also reduces the chance of substituting an incorrect tube additive under time pressure.
  2. The correct answer is B (Immediately before initial patient contact and glove donning at the bedside): gloves are put on at the point of care, right before touching the patient, to maintain barrier protection throughout the entire interaction. Donning gloves too early risks contaminating them on other surfaces before the draw even begins. This timing also supports infection control standards used across Canadian healthcare settings. Waiting until after skin contact defeats the purpose of the barrier entirely.
  3. The correct answer is D (An artery has been located, so a different site must be selected): a palpable pulse signals arterial rather than venous tissue, and arterial puncture during a routine venipuncture carries real risk of excess bleeding and nerve involvement. Veins feel soft, round, and resilient without any rhythmic beating under the fingertip. Recognizing this distinction during palpation, before the needle is inserted, is a core safety skill. Choosing an alternate site avoids the complications associated with an accidental arterial stick.
  4. The correct answer is C (The full tourniquet application should be released and reapplied within one minute): prolonged tourniquet time causes hemoconcentration and can shift the local concentration of certain analytes, skewing laboratory results. If site selection and preparation take longer than a minute, the tourniquet should be released and reapplied just before puncture. This practice protects sample integrity without sacrificing the ability to locate a good vein. Ignoring elapsed time risks reporting inaccurate values back to the ordering clinician.
  5. The correct answer is B (Alcohol must fully air dry to avoid stinging and possible sample hemolysis): puncturing before the antiseptic has dried can carry residual alcohol into the specimen, contributing to hemolysis and causing the patient a sharper stinging sensation. Allowing the site to air dry on its own, without fanning or blowing, preserves both antisepsis and comfort. This differs from the more rigorous protocol used for blood culture collection. Rushing this step undermines the accuracy of the resulting laboratory sample.
  6. The correct answer is A (Bevel up, shallow angle near fifteen degrees, vein anchored below the site): a superficial vein calls for a shallower insertion angle so the needle enters cleanly without passing through the back wall of the vessel. Bevel-up orientation allows smooth penetration and even blood flow into the tube. Anchoring the vein with the non-dominant hand below the puncture site keeps it from rolling during insertion. Together these elements reduce the chance of a missed or through-and-through stick.
  7. The correct answer is A (Later tubes are typically manufactured with a somewhat reduced vacuum strength by design): each additive tube is manufactured to draw a specific fill volume, and slight variation in fill rate between tubes further along a multi-tube sequence is a normal, expected part of the evacuated tube system. This alone does not indicate a problem requiring the needle to be repositioned or the site rechecked. A blocked needle would typically cause flow to stop rather than merely slow, and a loosened tourniquet would slow flow into every remaining tube equally rather than just the second one. Steady, if slightly slower, flow into the second tube is consistent with proper technique.
  8. The correct answer is C (Release the tourniquet promptly, ideally before the last tube finishes filling): the tourniquet should never remain on for longer than about a minute, and here it has clearly exceeded that window, so it must be released without further delay. Prompt release reduces the risk of hemoconcentration affecting results and lessens patient discomfort from prolonged constriction. Releasing it while the last tube still fills, when possible, is standard practice. Delaying release until after bandaging compounds the error rather than correcting it.
  9. The correct answer is A (Direct disposal into a sharps container without any recapping attempt): a used needle must go straight into an approved sharps container the instant it is withdrawn, with no intermediate step. Recapping, even with a one-handed technique, is never acceptable practice because it reintroduces a preventable needlestick risk. Setting the needle down anywhere other than the sharps container also creates unnecessary hazard for staff and patients. Immediate disposal is one of the most consistently enforced safety rules in the entire procedure.
  10. The correct answer is D (Avoid heavy lifting with that arm for a short period and watch for swelling): patients should be advised to keep the bandaged arm relatively still and to watch for signs of a developing hematoma such as swelling or discolouration. Removing the bandage too quickly risks reopening the puncture site before clotting is complete. Massaging the area can actually worsen bruising rather than reduce it. Clear, simple post-draw instructions help the patient recognize a genuine complication if one develops.
  11. The correct answer is B (At the patient's bedside, immediately after collection, before leaving the room): every tube must be labeled at the point of collection while the phlebotomist and patient are still together, never carried unlabeled to another location. This practice prevents mix-ups between specimens drawn from different patients during a shift. Delaying labeling introduces a real risk of misidentification that can have serious downstream consequences. Bedside labeling is treated as a non-negotiable patient safety step in Canadian phlebotomy practice.
  12. The correct answer is C (A complete label requires collection time and collector identification too): a fully compliant specimen label includes patient identifiers, the exact collection date and time, and an indication of who performed the draw. Missing the time or collector initials can affect result interpretation and accountability if a question later arises about the specimen. Waiting for the laboratory to flag the omission introduces avoidable delay and rework. Completing every required field at the bedside prevents the specimen from being questioned or rejected later.
  13. The correct answer is D (A hematoma from blood leaking into the surrounding tissue at the site): firm, tender swelling with discolouration developing soon after a draw is the classic presentation of a hematoma, typically caused by insufficient pressure or premature removal of the tourniquet before withdrawal. Applying adequate direct pressure immediately after needle removal is the main preventive measure. This differs from infection, which usually develops more slowly and often includes warmth or drainage. Recognizing the hematoma pattern helps staff reassure the patient and monitor appropriately.
  14. The correct answer is A (Withdraw the needle immediately rather than redirecting or probing further): shooting or electric-like pain is a warning sign of possible nerve contact, and continuing to probe or redirect blindly significantly raises the risk of lasting nerve injury. The needle should be removed at once, pressure applied, and an alternate site considered if another attempt is warranted. Never fishing for a vein once nerve involvement is suspected is a fundamental safety rule. Prompt withdrawal protects the patient from a preventable and potentially serious complication.
  15. The correct answer is B (A likely vasovagal response, so stop the draw and lower the patient's head): pallor, lightheadedness, and sweating during venipuncture are classic signs of a vasovagal reaction, and the priority is patient safety over completing the collection. Stopping the draw, having the patient lower their head or lie down, and monitoring closely helps prevent a fall or loss of consciousness. Continuing the draw despite these warning signs disregards the patient's wellbeing. Recognizing this pattern quickly allows the phlebotomist to intervene before the reaction worsens.
  16. The correct answer is D (Stop and involve another qualified staff member rather than trying again): standard practice limits any one phlebotomist to two attempts before escalating to another qualified colleague, regardless of which arm might be tried next. This limit protects the patient from repeated tissue trauma and unnecessary distress. A fresh set of eyes and hands often succeeds where repeated attempts by the same person have not. Respecting the two-attempt rule reflects both patient-centred care and professional accountability.
  17. The correct answer is A (Confirm the discrepancy and verify the correct patient before proceeding): any mismatch between the requisition and the intended patient must be resolved before proceeding, since drawing blood from the wrong individual is a serious identification error. Verifying identity is part of the preparation phase that occurs before equipment is finalized and before any patient contact happens. Assuming either source is correct without checking introduces unacceptable risk. Careful verification at this early stage prevents a cascade of downstream errors.
  18. The correct answer is D (At the point of care, right before contact with the patient begins): gloves should be applied at the actual point of care, immediately before touching the patient, so that hand hygiene performed beforehand is not undone by contact with door handles or other surfaces. Donning gloves too early, such as before hand hygiene, defeats the purpose of clean hands entirely. Waiting until after the vein is located leaves the patient unprotected during initial contact. This sequencing keeps infection control consistent throughout the encounter.
  19. The correct answer is B (A sclerosed or thrombosed vein that should be avoided for puncture): a hard, cord-like vessel that fails to compress or rebound signals scarring or clotting from repeated prior use, and attempting to draw from it is likely to fail and may harm the vessel further. Healthy veins feel round, soft, and resilient, springing back promptly after gentle pressure. Selecting an alternate, more resilient vein improves the chance of a successful first attempt. Recognizing this tactile difference is a core palpation skill developed with practice.
  20. The correct answer is C (Approximately seven to ten centimetres above the intended puncture site): this distance provides enough venous engorgement for palpation and puncture while keeping the tourniquet clear of the actual working area. Placing it directly over the site or too close interferes with cleansing and needle insertion. Placing it excessively far away, near the shoulder, reduces its effectiveness at engorging the target vein. This standard spacing balances visibility of the vein with a clean, unobstructed puncture site.
  21. The correct answer is D (Fanning may reintroduce contaminants onto the freshly cleansed puncture site): waving anything, including a gauze pad, over a cleansed site risks depositing airborne particles or touching the area with a non-sterile surface, undoing the antisepsis just performed. Air drying should occur passively, without fanning or blotting the skin. This principle applies to routine venipuncture, though blood culture collection follows an even more rigorous protocol. Allowing natural air drying protects both specimen quality and patient safety.
  22. The correct answer is A (A somewhat steeper angle within the general fifteen to thirty degree range): a deeper vein generally requires an angle toward the higher end of the standard fifteen to thirty degree range so the needle can reach the vessel without excessive lateral movement under the skin. The bevel remains up throughout, since that orientation supports smooth blood flow regardless of depth. Flattening the angle further would actually make reaching a deeper vein harder, not easier. Adjusting angle to vein depth is a skill refined through supervised practice.
  23. The correct answer is B (It allows a lower, more flexible insertion angle and steadier control near small veins): the short, flexible tubing and small-gauge needle of a winged set make it easier to achieve a shallow angle and stable positioning in fragile or hard-to-access veins. This does not remove the ongoing need to follow the correct order of draw or to use a tourniquet appropriately. It also does not automatically prevent hemolysis, which still depends on technique such as tube mixing and needle gauge. The mechanical flexibility is the genuine benefit this device offers.
  24. The correct answer is A (The correct order of draw must still be followed during tube transfer): whether tubes fill directly from a holder or receive blood transferred from a syringe, additive carryover and order-of-draw principles apply equally, so tubes must be filled in the proper sequence to protect sample integrity. Tourniquet timing, drying time, and bedside labeling remain unchanged regardless of which collection device is used. Believing the syringe method exempts the phlebotomist from these standards is a common and avoidable misunderstanding. Consistent technique across methods keeps results reliable.
  25. The correct answer is D (The tourniquet should be released before the needle is withdrawn from the vein): releasing the tourniquet before withdrawing the needle reduces venous pressure at the site, helping limit bleeding and bruising once the needle comes out. Withdrawing the needle first while the tourniquet remains tight can push blood into surrounding tissue and increase hematoma risk. Leaving the tourniquet on through bandaging is never appropriate regardless of patient preference. This sequencing is a small but meaningful part of minimizing post-draw complications.
  26. The correct answer is D (Apply firm, direct pressure to the site with clean gauze right away): immediate direct pressure over the puncture site is essential to promote clotting and reduce the risk of hematoma formation before any bandage is applied. Waiting passively without applying pressure leaves the site vulnerable to continued bleeding into surrounding tissue. Reapplying alcohol at this stage serves no purpose and can irritate the fresh puncture. Sharp arm bending is discouraged since it can disrupt clot formation rather than help it.
  27. The correct answer is C (Bedside labeling prevents mix-ups and keeps each specimen tied to the correct patient): labeling immediately at the point of collection, while the patient can still be verified, is what prevents specimens from being confused with those of another patient collected later in the shift. This is a required safety step rather than a personal preference or something reserved for busy days. It does not replace identity verification, which still occurs earlier in the encounter as its own distinct step. Bedside labeling is one of the most tightly enforced rules in the entire procedure.
  28. The correct answer is B (It allows accountability and follow-up if a question about the draw arises later): recording who collected a specimen creates a clear line of accountability if a discrepancy, complication, or question about technique needs to be investigated afterward. This requirement applies to all patients, not a specific age group, and does not substitute for recording the collection date and time, which remain separate required fields. Treating collector initials as unnecessary formality overlooks their real role in traceability. A complete label protects both the patient and the phlebotomist.
  29. The correct answer is D (Insufficient direct pressure applied immediately after needle withdrawal): swelling and bruising limited to the puncture site without signs of infection points to a hematoma, most commonly caused by inadequate pressure or premature bending of the arm before the site has clotted. The collection method, order of draw, and alcohol drying time do not directly cause this particular complication. Emphasizing firm, sustained pressure after withdrawal is the main way to prevent this outcome in future draws. Recognizing the likely cause helps guide patient reassurance and any needed follow-up.
  30. The correct answer is A (This brief pain pattern is a typical, expected sensation and the draw may continue): a short, sharp pinch at the moment of insertion that resolves into mild pressure is a normal and expected sensation, quite distinct from the sustained shooting or electric-like pain associated with nerve contact. Recognizing this difference allows the phlebotomist to proceed confidently rather than unnecessarily withdrawing the needle. Arterial puncture and prolonged tourniquet time present with different signs entirely. Distinguishing normal discomfort from a genuine warning sign is an important clinical judgment skill.
  31. The correct answer is C (The draw may be reassessed and resumed or rescheduled based on how the patient tolerates it): once a vasovagal episode has settled and the patient appears stable, the phlebotomist uses clinical judgment about whether to continue, pause, or reschedule the collection depending on how the patient is coping. Not every episode requires an emergency department visit, though ongoing symptoms or loss of consciousness would warrant escalation. Simply trusting a verbal report without brief continued observation skips an important safety step. Increasing tourniquet pressure has no relationship to managing a vasovagal reaction and would not be appropriate.
  32. The correct answer is B (It limits repeated tissue trauma and gives the patient a better chance of success): capping attempts at two per phlebotomist protects the patient from unnecessary repeated needle sticks and bruising while bringing in a colleague whose different technique or perspective may succeed. This rule applies broadly to patients of any age, not one specific group, and is not contingent on the patient asking for someone else. Framing it as mere paperwork misses its real purpose of reducing harm. Following this escalation step reflects genuinely patient-centred practice.
  33. The correct answer is A (It ensures the correct tubes and additives are on hand, avoiding a second interruption): matching the requisition against available tube types before starting confirms that every needed additive is on hand, preventing a mid-procedure interruption to retrieve a missing item. This check applies equally to inpatient and outpatient settings, not just one. It also does not replace identity verification, which remains a separate required step performed at the bedside. Thorough preparation reduces both delays and the chance of collecting into an incorrect tube.
  34. The correct answer is C (At the point when direct physical contact with the patient begins): gloves go on right at the point where physical contact with the patient starts, such as palpating for a vein, keeping the barrier protection meaningful and timely. Applying gloves before hand hygiene would be backward, since hands must be clean first. Waiting until after the site is already cleansed exposes the patient to ungloved contact during palpation. Treating the timing as merely convenient overlooks its role in consistent infection control.
  35. The correct answer is B (The soft, springy site, since it reflects a healthy, usable vein without sclerosis): a vein that feels soft and springs back after gentle pressure is the appropriate choice, while the firm, cord-like vessel likely reflects sclerosis or thrombosis from prior use and should be avoided. Firmness in this context is a warning sign rather than a sign of strength. Comparing nearby sites within the same arm during palpation is a normal and encouraged part of site selection. Choosing the healthier vessel improves the likelihood of a successful, complication-free draw.
  36. The correct answer is C (It can interfere with proper site cleansing and needle insertion technique): placing the tourniquet too close to the puncture site crowds the working area, making it harder to cleanse thoroughly and to position the needle at a proper angle without contacting the tourniquet material. Maintaining the recommended seven to ten centimetre distance keeps the site accessible and clean. It does not by itself guarantee hemoconcentration, which relates more to how long the tourniquet stays on. This spacing issue is a practical technique problem rather than a vein-visibility one.
  37. The correct answer is C (It risks dragging outer contaminants back toward the intended puncture point): cleansing should always move from the centre outward so that microorganisms are pushed away from the eventual puncture site rather than back toward it. Working from the outside in reverses this logic and can reintroduce contamination exactly where it matters most. This is a matter of correct antiseptic technique rather than simple personal preference or cost. Following the centre-outward pattern consistently supports a genuinely clean puncture site.
  38. The correct answer is A (A downward bevel can reduce smooth blood flow and increase insertion discomfort): the bevel-up orientation is standard because it allows the needle opening to enter the vessel wall smoothly and supports even blood flow into the tube. Inverting this orientation can make insertion feel less smooth and may compromise flow once the vessel is entered. This principle applies broadly across straight needle and winged set collections alike, not to one device only. Maintaining bevel-up as the default helps ensure a technically sound puncture.
  39. The correct answer is C (Inversion mixes additive with blood, supporting proper clot formation or anticoagulation): gently inverting each tube the recommended number of times distributes the internal additive throughout the blood sample, allowing anticoagulants to work evenly or clot activators to function as intended. Skipping this step can lead to clots forming in anticoagulated tubes or uneven results. It applies to the relevant additive tubes generally, not one specific test category, and does not substitute for following the correct order of draw. Proper mixing is a small mechanical detail with a real effect on specimen quality.
  40. The correct answer is B (Keep the arm extended while applying firm, direct pressure with the gauze): keeping the arm extended while applying firm, direct pressure allows even compression over the puncture site, whereas bending the elbow can trap blood and increase the chance of hematoma formation rather than prevent it. Releasing pressure too early, once bleeding briefly slows, risks reopening the site. A bandage is appropriate only after adequate manual pressure has already controlled bleeding, not as an immediate substitute for it. Correcting the patient's positioning here helps prevent a common and avoidable complication.
  41. The correct answer is D (Whether a labeling mistake such as a wrong name occurred at bedside): a final label-to-requisition check is specifically aimed at catching a transcription or identification error, such as a misspelled name or wrong date of birth, before the specimen leaves the patient's presence. Order of draw, alcohol drying, and tourniquet timing are technique issues that occurred earlier and are not what this particular verification step addresses. Catching a labeling error while the patient is still available allows immediate correction rather than a rejected specimen later. This last check is a simple but valuable safety net.
  42. The correct answer is A (Mild bruising alone is typically less concerning than firm, painful swelling): superficial discolouration without pain, significant swelling, or warmth is a common and usually minor after-effect of venipuncture, distinct from a true hematoma with firm, tender swelling that suggests ongoing bleeding into tissue. Recognizing this difference helps staff reassure patients appropriately without underreacting to a genuine complication. Treating every bit of bruising as equally serious can create unnecessary alarm. Careful assessment of the specific signs present guides the right level of follow-up.
  43. The correct answer is D (Explain to the patient what is happening and select a fresh, well-palpated site): before a second attempt, the phlebotomist should communicate clearly with the patient and carefully palpate a new site rather than assuming the prior assessment still applies to a different arm. Reusing equipment from a failed attempt raises contamination and safety concerns and should not occur. Skipping re-palpation increases the chance of a second unsuccessful stick. Doubling tourniquet pressure does not improve vein prominence safely and simply adds discomfort without benefit.
  44. The correct answer is B (Anchoring the vein firmly below the site with the non-dominant hand): a mobile or rolling vein is stabilized by anchoring it below the intended puncture point using the non-dominant hand, applying gentle traction on the skin to hold the vessel in place during insertion. Increasing the angle or changing bevel orientation does not address the underlying mobility issue. Releasing the tourniquet before the vein is entered would reduce engorgement and make the vein harder to access, not easier. Proper anchoring technique is the direct solution to this common challenge.
  45. The correct answer is C (It reduces venous pressure at the site, helping limit post-draw bruising): releasing the tourniquet first lowers the pressure within the vein before the needle is removed, reducing the amount of blood that can escape into surrounding tissue and lowering the risk of a hematoma. This sequence is not primarily about saving time, and it applies regardless of whether a straight needle or a winged set is used. Framing the order as arbitrary habit overlooks its real physiological rationale. Explaining this reasoning to patients can also help them understand why the timing matters.
  46. The correct answer is A (Preparation and identification steps precede direct patient contact and technique steps): the overall procedure follows a logical sequence in which administrative and preparatory steps, such as reviewing the requisition and verifying identity, occur before hands-on technique steps like gloving, tourniquet application, and cleansing begin. Treating the order as interchangeable ignores the safety reasoning behind each stage. Gloving before identity confirmation, or applying a tourniquet before reviewing the requisition, reverses the intended flow of the procedure. Teaching this structured sequence helps a new colleague build safe, consistent habits from the start.
  47. The correct answer is B (Bending the arm afterward risks disrupting clot formation and is not recommended): keeping the arm extended, rather than bent, after bandaging allows the clot at the puncture site to stabilize without added pressure or movement that could disturb it. This is why bent-arm holding is discouraged even earlier during pressure application, not just after bandaging. It is not a step reserved only for when a hematoma has already occurred, since the goal is prevention rather than reaction. Clear, correct post-draw instructions like this help patients avoid an avoidable complication after they leave.
  48. The correct answer is B (Cross-check the requisition against two patient identifiers at bedside): patient safety protocols in Canadian healthcare require verifying at least two independent identifiers, such as full name and date of birth, directly with the patient or their wristband. A bed number or ward position alone is not a reliable identifier since patients can be moved or share similar names. This confirmation must happen at the bedside immediately before the draw, not delegated to a third party. Skipping this step risks a preventable misidentification error with serious downstream consequences.
  49. The correct answer is A (Immediately before any physical contact with the patient begins): hand hygiene should be performed right before touching the patient, and gloves should be donned right before contact with the patient, their bedding, or the puncture site. Performing hand hygiene too early during supply gathering allows recontamination before the patient encounter even starts. Gloving before palpation and tourniquet application protects both patient and phlebotomist from bloodborne exposure. This sequencing is a core infection control expectation in every Canadian clinical setting.
  50. The correct answer is C (A sclerosed or thrombosed vein that should be avoided entirely): a hardened, cord-like vessel without the resilient bounce-back of a healthy vein indicates scarring or clotting from repeated punctures or disease, and drawing from it risks a failed collection or inaccurate results. A usable vein should feel round, firm, and springy under gentle pressure. Warming or reapplying the tourniquet will not change the structural nature of a sclerosed vessel. The phlebotomist should select an alternate site rather than attempt puncture there.
  51. The correct answer is D (Select a different site since this vessel is likely an artery): a pulsating vessel indicates an artery, which carries higher pressure blood and a greater risk of hematoma, excessive bleeding, and pain if punctured for routine venipuncture. Arteries are anatomically distinguishable from veins by this pulsation and should never be used for standard blood collection. The phlebotomist must choose an alternate, clearly venous site instead. No adjustment to angle or antisepsis corrects the fundamental problem of the wrong vessel type.
  52. The correct answer is D (Too close may affect site selection; use 7 to 10 cm): the tourniquet should generally be applied about 7 to 10 cm above the intended puncture site so it engorges the vein enough to aid selection and puncture without interfering with the cleansed area or the phlebotomist's grip. Placing it too close crowds the working space and can complicate palpation and needle insertion. The distance is a practical spacing guideline, not primarily a contamination or visibility concern. Adjusting to the recommended range improves both site assessment and procedural safety.
  53. The correct answer is B (Hemoconcentration causing falsely elevated analyte results): prolonged tourniquet application beyond about one minute causes fluid to shift out of the vessel into surrounding tissue, concentrating cells and large molecules in the blood that remains, which skews results for tests like proteins, enzymes, and cell counts. This is a well-documented preanalytical error rather than a hemolysis or contamination issue. The phlebotomist should release the tourniquet, allow the arm a brief rest, and reapply it once a site has actually been chosen. This protects the accuracy of the eventual specimen.
  54. The correct answer is A (Allow the site to air dry completely before proceeding): the antiseptic needs to air dry fully to achieve its bactericidal effect and to avoid stinging the patient or causing hemolysis when the needle drags residual alcohol into the sample. Blotting with gauze can recontaminate the site and fanning does not guarantee complete evaporation. Reapplying alcohol simply restarts the drying requirement without solving the underlying wait. Patience at this step protects both specimen quality and patient comfort.
  55. The correct answer is C (The blood culture site requires a more rigorous antisepsis protocol): because blood cultures are highly sensitive to contamination and a false positive can lead to unnecessary antibiotic treatment, facilities require a more stringent skin preparation than the standard single alcohol swab used for routine draws. The specific extended steps belong to specimen collection protocols covered elsewhere, but the phlebotomist must recognize that routine and culture antisepsis are not interchangeable. Using only the routine method for a culture draw increases contamination risk. Recognizing this distinction is a core competency for accurate test ordering support.
  56. The correct answer is C (Bevel up at roughly 15 to 30 degrees depending on depth): standard venipuncture technique orients the needle bevel upward and enters the skin at a shallow angle, generally between 15 and 30 degrees, adjusted slightly for how deep the target vein sits beneath the surface. A steeper angle risks passing through the back wall of the vein, while bevel-down or sideways orientation is not standard practice for routine draws. Proper angle and orientation reduce trauma to the vessel and improve first-attempt success. This technique is taught consistently across Canadian phlebotomy training programs.
  57. The correct answer is D (Anchor the vein below the site by pulling the skin taut): anchoring the vein by pulling the skin taut a few centimetres below the intended puncture site with the non-dominant hand prevents the vein from rolling or sliding away as the needle is inserted, which is especially important in patients with mobile or fragile veins. This technique directly supports first-attempt success and reduces the need for probing. The non-dominant hand should not be occupied elsewhere during this critical moment. Proper anchoring is a fundamental hands-on skill assessed in phlebotomy competency evaluations.
  58. The correct answer is B (The needle bevel may have shifted against the vein wall): a sudden slowing or stopping of flow that occurs before a tube is properly filled often indicates the needle bevel has partially exited the lumen or is resting against the vessel wall, obstructing flow through mechanical positioning rather than vacuum failure. A gentle repositioning of the needle angle, without probing blindly, can often restore flow. Vacuum exhaustion would typically occur after the expected volume has already been collected, not before. Recognizing this distinction helps the phlebotomist troubleshoot without needing to remove and reattempt.
  59. The correct answer is A (It allows for a smaller gauge and reduced hand movement): winged sets are typically available in smaller gauges and their flexible tubing allows the tube holder to be manipulated with minimal movement transmitted to the needle, making them well suited to small or fragile hand and wrist veins. Standard antisepsis and the correct order of draw still fully apply regardless of the device used. The insertion angle for a butterfly set remains shallow, similar to a straight needle, not steeper. Choosing the right device for vein fragility improves patient comfort and specimen quality.
  60. The correct answer is A (Release the tourniquet, then withdraw the needle): the tourniquet should be released before the needle is withdrawn, ideally once the last tube is filling or just after, so that venous pressure normalizes and the risk of hematoma formation from a still-pressurized vessel is reduced. Withdrawing the needle first while the tourniquet remains in place can cause blood to leak into surrounding tissue as pressure is suddenly released near an open puncture. Leaving the tourniquet on through gauze application unnecessarily prolongs venous congestion. This sequencing is a standard safety step taught in every venipuncture procedure.
  61. The correct answer is C (Dispose of it directly into an approved sharps container): the needle must be disposed of immediately and directly into a puncture-resistant sharps container without being set down, handed to anyone, or recapped, since recapping is the leading cause of accidental needlestick injuries. Gauze and pressure should already be prepared beforehand so this disposal step happens without delay. Setting the needle on a tray, even briefly, creates unnecessary exposure risk to the phlebotomist and others. Immediate, direct disposal is a non-negotiable safety standard in Canadian workplaces.
  62. The correct answer is D (Keep the arm extended and apply steady direct pressure): patients should be instructed to keep the arm relatively straight and maintain firm, direct pressure over the gauze for a sufficient period, since bending the elbow can actually increase the chance of bleeding into surrounding tissue and hematoma formation. Massaging the site can worsen bruising rather than prevent it. Removing gauze too soon risks reopening the puncture before adequate clotting has occurred. Clear post-draw instructions reduce complications after the patient leaves the collection area.
  63. The correct answer is B (Labels must always be applied at the bedside, not elsewhere): every tube must be labeled immediately at the patient's bedside, before leaving the immediate collection area, to eliminate the risk of mislabeling or mixing up specimens from different patients. Walking away with unlabeled tubes, even briefly, introduces a serious patient identification hazard regardless of the specimen type. This requirement applies to all tube types, not just coagulation or any single test category. Bedside labeling is one of the most heavily emphasized safety rules in phlebotomy practice.
  64. The correct answer is B (Patient full name, unique identifier, date and time of draw): a complete specimen label must include the patient's full name, a unique identifier such as a health card or medical record number, and the exact date and time the specimen was collected, along with the collector's initials per facility policy. A room number is not a reliable patient identifier since patients can be moved or transferred. Neither a physician's signature nor the facility's mailing address are required elements of specimen identification. Complete, accurate labeling is essential for specimen traceability and patient safety.
  65. The correct answer is D (Hematoma formation, often from incomplete pressure applied): bruising and localized swelling at a puncture site are classic signs of a hematoma, which occurs when blood leaks into the surrounding tissue, frequently because pressure was released too soon, was insufficient, or the needle partially penetrated the far wall of the vessel. Hemolysis and hemoconcentration are laboratory-level effects on the specimen itself, not visible bruising at the site. Syncope refers to a fainting episode, an unrelated complication. Recognizing hematoma signs helps guide appropriate follow-up care and prevention for future draws.
  66. The correct answer is A (Withdraw the needle immediately as this may indicate nerve involvement): sharp, shooting, or electric-like pain during venipuncture is a warning sign of possible nerve contact or injury, and the needle should be withdrawn right away rather than redirected or adjusted while still inserted. Continuing to probe or reposition the needle blindly significantly increases the risk of lasting nerve damage. This sensation is distinctly different from the usual brief pinch of needle entry and should never be dismissed as normal. Prompt withdrawal followed by appropriate documentation and site selection elsewhere protects the patient from further harm.
  67. The correct answer is C (Stop the draw, remove the tourniquet, and lower the patient's head): pallor, lightheadedness, and sweating are classic early signs of a vasovagal reaction or impending syncope, and the priority is to stop the procedure, release the tourniquet, and help the patient into a position with the head lowered or lying flat with legs elevated if possible. Continuing to draw tubes or having the patient stand and walk both risk a fall or worsening the episode. Simple distraction does not address the physiological cause of the reaction. Prompt recognition and positioning are essential to prevent injury from a fainting episode.
  68. The correct answer is C (A second attempt is allowed, then another staff member should be involved): standard practice limits an individual phlebotomist to no more than two attempts on a given patient before involving another qualified staff member, since repeated unsuccessful punctures increase patient discomfort, tissue trauma, and complication risk. This limit is a professional standard, not something determined solely by the patient's preference or requiring physician authorization for a routine second attempt. Recognizing when to escalate reflects good clinical judgment rather than a failure of skill. Following this rule protects patients from unnecessary repeated trauma.
  69. The correct answer is A (Before approaching the patient, based on the requisition): reviewing the requisition and gathering the correct tubes and supplies should happen before the phlebotomist approaches the patient, so that the encounter proceeds smoothly and efficiently once contact begins. Selecting tubes only after the tourniquet is applied or blood flow is confirmed introduces unnecessary delay and disorganization at a critical point in the procedure. Positioning the patient and gloving are steps that follow, not precede, this preparation stage. Thorough advance preparation is a foundational professional habit in phlebotomy practice.
  70. The correct answer is D (The resilient vein, since it indicates a healthy, patent lumen): a vein that feels round, firm, and springs back after gentle palpation indicates a healthy, open lumen well suited for venipuncture, while a flat or non-rebounding vessel may be compressed, collapsed, or otherwise unsuitable. Visibility alone is not sufficient for site selection since a visible vein can still be unhealthy on palpation. Flat veins are not inherently more stable and can actually be harder to access successfully. Palpation is considered at least as important as visual inspection when choosing the best site.
  71. The correct answer is B (Transfer blood into tubes promptly, respecting the order of draw): when a syringe is used, the collected blood must be transferred into the appropriate tubes promptly, using a safety transfer device, and still following the correct order of draw to avoid additive carryover between tests. Labeling requirements are unchanged and still fully apply regardless of collection device. Recapping any needle, syringe or otherwise, remains prohibited due to needlestick risk. Understanding syringe transfer technique is important since the mechanics differ meaningfully from a direct evacuated tube draw.
  72. The correct answer is D (Select a new, unaffected site away from bruised or compromised areas): areas with recent bruising or repeated prior punctures may have compromised vessel integrity and increased sensitivity, so the phlebotomist should assess the entire arm and choose a healthier, unaffected site whenever possible. Drawing directly through a bruise increases both patient discomfort and the risk of an unsuccessful or traumatic attempt. Ignoring the bruising or simply tightening the tourniquet does not address the underlying tissue condition. Careful visual and tactile assessment of the whole limb supports better site selection decisions.
  73. The correct answer is C (Steady the holder gently and continue, answering briefly): the phlebotomist should keep the needle and holder stable with a gentle, secure grip while briefly acknowledging the patient verbally, since sudden movements to remove a tube or withdraw the needle mid-flow risk vein trauma or an incomplete draw. Fully withdrawing the needle over a minor shift is an overreaction that would require restarting the entire procedure. Releasing the tourniquet prematurely interrupts blood flow needed to complete the remaining tubes. Calm, steady technique while managing patient interaction is an expected professional skill.
  74. The correct answer is B (Remove the adhesive bandage and use a non-adhesive alternative): once an adhesive allergy is disclosed, the phlebotomist should remove the standard bandage and replace it with a non-adhesive option such as gauze secured with a wrap, since continued adhesive exposure can trigger a skin reaction. Leaving the allergen in place or layering another bandage over it does not resolve the concern and may worsen irritation. Patient-reported allergies should always be taken seriously rather than minimized. Responding promptly to this information reflects attentive, patient-centred care after the draw is complete.
  75. The correct answer is A (The well-anchored vein, since it is less likely to slip aside): a vein that is well anchored in the surrounding tissue is less likely to move or roll away during needle insertion, improving the chances of a successful first attempt. A vessel that rolls easily is harder to stabilize even with proper anchoring technique and increases the risk of a missed or repeated puncture. Mobility of a vessel is not an advantage for venipuncture despite sometimes reflecting normal elasticity. Selecting the more stable vein is a straightforward, evidence-based preference in routine practice.
  76. The correct answer is A (Circular motion starting at the centre moving outward): antisepsis should be applied in a circular motion beginning at the intended puncture point and spiraling outward, which avoids reintroducing skin flora from the outer, less-cleaned area back toward the centre. Working from the outside inward, or using back-and-forth strokes, risks dragging contaminants toward the puncture site. A single light dab does not adequately cover or disinfect the full area needed. Correct antisepsis technique directly supports specimen and patient safety.
  77. The correct answer is B (Transfer into tubes respecting the correct order of draw): even when collection occurs via syringe rather than a direct evacuated tube draw, transferring blood into multiple tubes still requires following the correct order of draw to prevent additive carryover that could alter test results. Vigorous shaking is not appropriate handling for a syringe transfer and risks hemolysis. Choosing tubes by convenience rather than sequence defeats the purpose of the ordering rule. Warming the syringe is unnecessary and not a recognized part of proper specimen handling.
  78. The correct answer is D (Firm, prolonged palpation using the fingertip to trace the vein path): when a vein is not obvious on visual inspection, using the fingertip to apply firm, deliberate palpation and trace the likely path of the vessel beneath the skin often reveals a workable site that was not visually apparent. Inserting the needle at a landmark without confirming a palpable vein invites a blind, unsuccessful attempt. Vigorous rubbing is not a recognized or reliable technique for vein location. Repeated tourniquet cycling without palpation wastes time and does not substitute for proper technique.
  79. The correct answer is C (Avoid heavy lifting with that arm for a short period after the draw): patients are generally advised to avoid strenuous activity or heavy lifting with the draw arm for a short period afterward, since increased pressure and muscle engagement can promote bleeding into tissue and hematoma formation at the puncture site. A bandage alone does not prevent internal bleeding caused by physical strain. Immediately resuming heavy activity works against, rather than promotes, proper healing. Continuous ice application for hours is excessive and not a standard recommendation for a routine venipuncture site.
  80. The correct answer is C (Have the patient seated or reclined rather than standing): for a patient with a known history of vasovagal reactions, positioning them seated or reclined before the draw begins reduces injury risk if a fainting episode occurs and can also lower the likelihood of one happening in the first place. Omitting the tourniquet is not a recognized prevention strategy and would compromise vein engorgement. Rushing without explanation, or deliberately withholding information, can increase rather than reduce patient anxiety. Thoughtful positioning and clear communication together support a safer procedure for at-risk patients.
  81. The correct answer is D (Apply firm pressure with gauze over the puncture site): immediately after needle withdrawal and sharps disposal, the phlebotomist applies gauze with firm pressure to the puncture site to control bleeding and reduce hematoma risk before attention turns to any other task. Labeling, hand hygiene at the end, and tidying supplies all follow this critical patient care step rather than preceding it. Reconfirming identity is done earlier during requisition matching, not at this point in the sequence. Prioritizing site management immediately after withdrawal protects the patient first.
  82. The correct answer is A (It increases the risk of nerve injury or other tissue damage): moving the needle blindly beneath the skin in search of a vein, commonly called probing or fishing, significantly raises the risk of striking a nerve or damaging surrounding tissue, since the phlebotomist cannot see the needle's path once it is beneath the surface. This risk is a direct patient safety concern rather than a workflow or equipment issue. Turnaround time and tube vacuum are unrelated to the reason probing is discouraged. If the first attempt is unsuccessful, the needle should be withdrawn and a new attempt planned rather than redirected blindly.
  83. The correct answer is B (Confirming which tube types and quantities are required): reviewing the requisition allows the phlebotomist to confirm exactly which tube types, additives, and volumes are needed for the ordered tests, ensuring the correct supplies are gathered before the patient encounter begins. Questioning the clinical necessity of ordered tests falls outside the phlebotomist's scope of practice. Delaying tube selection until after site choice creates unnecessary disorganization during the procedure. Requisition review is strictly a preparatory step for accurate specimen collection, not a diagnostic or clinical decision-making one.
  84. The correct answer is B (The site may still warrant further assessment or an alternate choice): visibility alone does not confirm that a vein is suitable, since palpation for firmness and resilient bounce-back provides essential information about the vessel's true condition that appearance cannot. If a vein remains unconvincing on palpation despite tourniquet engorgement, the phlebotomist should continue assessing other sites rather than committing prematurely. Excessively tightening the tourniquet does not change the underlying character of the vessel and can cause patient discomfort. Antisepsis should only be applied once a definite site has been confidently selected.
  85. The correct answer is A (Which sites and techniques were already attempted and why): communicating which sites were tried, what was observed, and why the attempts were unsuccessful helps the second phlebotomist avoid repeating the same difficulties and choose a more promising approach. Starting entirely fresh without this information wastes valuable insight already gathered. Withholding procedural detail beyond the test list undermines the purpose of the handoff itself. Recommending a third attempt at the exact same failed site contradicts the reasoning for escalating to another staff member in the first place.
  86. The correct answer is D (To prevent cross-contamination of additives between tubes): the correct order of draw exists primarily to prevent additive carryover between tubes, since even trace amounts of one tube's additive transferring into a subsequent tube can alter test results for coagulation, chemistry, or other panels. It does not influence how many tubes are required for testing or simplify the labeling process itself. While a well-organized draw can be efficient, shortening tourniquet time is not the underlying rationale for the sequence. Respecting the order of draw is a foundational specimen integrity principle regardless of collection device used.
  87. The correct answer is C (Use the shallower end of the range, closer to 15 degrees): for a superficial vein sitting close to the skin surface, a shallower insertion angle near the lower end of the typical 15 to 30 degree range is appropriate to avoid passing through the vessel entirely and into deeper tissue. Angles well beyond the standard range are not part of routine venipuncture technique and increase risk of complications. Depth does influence angle choice, contrary to the claim that it does not matter. Adjusting angle to vein depth is a practical skill refined through supervised practice.
  88. The correct answer is D (Complete the final tube promptly, then release the tourniquet): since the tourniquet should already be nearing its one-minute limit by this point in the draw, completing the final tube promptly and releasing the tourniquet immediately afterward addresses both the patient's discomfort and the standard timing guideline together. Loosening the tourniquet mid-draw while flow continues can disrupt vacuum-driven filling and compromise the sample. Dismissing patient discomfort is inappropriate patient care. Repeated fist pumping is discouraged during collection since it can affect certain test results such as potassium.
  89. The correct answer is C (Carefully confirm the vessel is truly venous, not arterial, before use): when a candidate vein sits near a palpable pulse, the phlebotomist must carefully distinguish the intended venous vessel from the nearby artery to avoid accidental arterial puncture, which carries higher complication risk. Proximity to a pulse is a meaningful safety consideration, not something to disregard. Selecting a site specifically because of arterial proximity misunderstands the goal of venipuncture, which targets veins only. Loosening the tourniquet does not address the fundamental need to correctly identify the vessel type before puncture.
  90. The correct answer is A (Every tube has been fully and correctly labeled at bedside): before leaving the patient's room, the phlebotomist must confirm every collected tube has been completely and accurately labeled at the bedside, since this is the last opportunity to verify labeling against the actual patient before the specimens leave the immediate area. Re-signing the requisition is not a routine bedside requirement for standard draws. While identity was confirmed earlier, a final verbal repetition of birth date is not the specific final labeling check being described. Independent label review by a second staff member is not a standard requirement for routine, non-critical specimen types.
  91. The correct answer is B (The vein may still be usable since palpation quality is favourable): a vein that feels firm and resilient on palpation can still be a good candidate for venipuncture even if it is not strongly visible, since palpation often provides more reliable information about vessel suitability than visual appearance alone, particularly in patients with deeper veins. Dismissing a vein based on visibility alone, despite favourable palpation, overlooks an important assessment principle. Aggressive tapping is not a recommended technique and can cause unnecessary discomfort. Doubling tourniquets is not standard practice and introduces additional risk without proven benefit.
  92. The correct answer is A (Tourniquet, antisepsis, allow drying, then needle insertion): the standard sequence applies the tourniquet first to aid site selection, cleanses the chosen site with antiseptic, allows it to fully air dry, and only then proceeds with needle insertion. Applying antisepsis before the tourniquet risks cleansing an area that later turns out unsuitable once the vein is engorged. Inserting the needle before tourniquet application or antisepsis is unsafe and contrary to accepted technique. Skipping drying time undermines the antiseptic's effectiveness and can cause patient discomfort.
  93. The correct answer is A (The needle likely penetrated through the back wall of the vein): even with correct pressure applied afterward, a hematoma can still form if the needle passed through the back wall of the vein during insertion, allowing blood to leak into surrounding tissue from that internal injury site rather than from inadequate external pressure. Hydration status and gauze sterility are not typical direct causes of hematoma formation in this scenario. Antisepsis direction affects infection risk, not hematoma formation. Recognizing this cause helps the phlebotomist reflect on angle and depth technique for future draws rather than assuming a post-draw error occurred.
  94. The correct answer is B (Requisition review, identification, site selection, antisepsis, insertion, bedside labeling): the proper overall flow begins with requisition review and supply gathering, proceeds through patient identification, site selection with tourniquet and palpation, antisepsis with drying time, needle insertion and collection, and concludes with immediate bedside labeling before leaving the patient. Any sequence placing antisepsis before tourniquet-assisted site selection, or insertion before antisepsis, misorders these safety-critical steps. Labeling must never occur before the tubes are actually filled, since there would be nothing yet to label. Understanding the full correct sequence, not just isolated steps, is essential for safe and accurate specimen collection.
  95. The correct answer is B (It lets the phlebotomist remain continuously at the bedside once the tourniquet is on): gathering every supply beforehand means the phlebotomist never has to step away mid-procedure to fetch a missing tube or bandage. Leaving the bedside with the tourniquet still applied risks prolonged venous stasis and hemoconcentration, and it also breaks the continuous positive identification link between patient and specimen. Complete preparation supports both patient safety and specimen integrity in one uninterrupted step. This habit is taught early as a foundational workflow rule in Canadian phlebotomy practice.
  96. The correct answer is D (Hand hygiene is performed immediately before donning gloves at the patient's bedside): washing or sanitizing hands right before gloving prevents contamination of the glove exterior and protects the patient from organisms carried on unwashed hands. Gloves are never treated as a substitute for hand hygiene, and donning them without first cleaning hands defeats the purpose of the barrier entirely. This sequence is repeated before every single patient encounter, never assumed to carry over from an earlier task. It is a core infection control expectation across Canadian clinical settings.
  97. The correct answer is A (A healthy vein that is generally suitable for a standard venipuncture attempt): a vein that palpates as round, resilient, and bounces back after compression has intact walls and adequate lumen, which are exactly the hallmarks phlebotomists are trained to feel for during site selection. A sclerosed or thrombosed vessel instead feels hard, cord-like, and lacks that springy rebound entirely. This palpation step is precisely why site selection relies heavily on touch rather than sight alone. Choosing a vein with this feel improves the odds of a clean, single-attempt collection.
  98. The correct answer is C (Select a different site entirely because the pulsation suggests an underlying artery): a palpable pulse indicates an artery rather than a vein, and accidental arterial puncture carries a meaningfully higher risk of hematoma, excess bleeding, and patient injury. Phlebotomists are trained to abandon any site where pulsation is felt and to reassess the limb for a proper venous target elsewhere. Continuing with a pulsating vessel is never appropriate, regardless of how promising the blood flow might otherwise seem. Recognizing this tactile cue is a basic and essential safety check taught early in training.
  99. The correct answer is B (Roughly 7 to 10 centimetres above the site chosen for the puncture): this distance restricts venous return enough to distend the vein for easier palpation and access while still keeping the tourniquet clear of the antiseptic field once cleansing begins. Placing it too close risks interfering with the cleaned site, and placing it too far away meaningfully reduces its distending effect on the vein. This spacing is a standard measurement taught consistently across Canadian phlebotomy programs. Correct placement supports both successful site selection and overall patient comfort.
  100. The correct answer is D (Hemoconcentration may develop and skew several analyte concentrations in the collected sample): leaving a tourniquet on beyond about one minute causes fluid to shift out of the vessel while larger molecules and cells remain behind, artificially concentrating the eventual sample. This can distort results for proteins, cell counts, and other analytes that are sensitive to hemoconcentration effects. The recommended practice is to release the tourniquet and let circulation normalize before reapplying if more time is genuinely needed to locate a vein. Accurate laboratory results depend on respecting this one-minute guideline.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Working outward in expanding circles starting precisely from the centre point): starting exactly at the intended puncture site and moving progressively outward prevents carrying skin flora from the surrounding area back into the freshly cleaned zone. Moving inward toward the centre would instead reintroduce contamination exactly where the needle is about to enter the skin. This technique is standard for routine venipuncture antisepsis across Canadian laboratory practice. Proper direction of cleansing directly supports both specimen quality and patient safety during the draw.
  2. The correct answer is A (The site should be allowed to air dry fully before the needle enters): allowing alcohol to air dry naturally gives it time to achieve its full antiseptic effect and prevents residual alcohol from causing a stinging sensation or contributing to hemolysis of the resulting sample. Wiping the site with gauze would remove the antiseptic before it finishes working and could reintroduce contamination onto the skin. Fanning or blowing on the site is also discouraged since it can reintroduce airborne organisms nearby. Waiting for natural air drying is the correct and expected step.
  3. The correct answer is D (The blood culture site requires a noticeably more rigorous antiseptic protocol than a routine draw): because blood cultures are used to detect a true bloodstream infection, any skin flora introduced during collection can produce a false positive result, so a stricter cleansing procedure is applied before those bottles are filled. The specific steps of that enhanced protocol belong to specimen collection training beyond routine venipuncture technique itself. Recognizing that blood cultures demand extra care is an important distinction for working phlebotomists to internalize. Routine tubes drawn on the same patient still follow standard alcohol antisepsis.
  4. The correct answer is B (An angle at the shallow end of the standard 15 to 30 degree range): a vein sitting close to the surface requires a shallower approach so the needle enters the lumen without passing straight through the far wall or travelling too deep beneath the skin. Steeper angles are better reserved for deeper veins where more travel distance is genuinely needed to reach the vessel itself. The bevel should also be oriented upward regardless of the exact angle chosen within that overall range. Adjusting the angle to match vein depth improves with hands-on experience over time.
  5. The correct answer is A (It stabilizes the vein and reduces the chance that it rolls away from the needle): anchoring the vein by pulling the skin taut below the intended site keeps the vessel from shifting sideways as the needle approaches, which is a common cause of a missed or repeated attempt. This anchoring is done with the non-dominant hand while the dominant hand controls the needle and holder or syringe throughout insertion. Without anchoring, mobile veins in some patients are especially prone to rolling unpredictably. Good anchoring technique meaningfully improves the odds of first-attempt success.
  6. The correct answer is C (The tube's internal vacuum was not properly seated fully onto the needle): the evacuated tube system relies on the tube's vacuum pulling blood through the needle once the tube is fully pushed onto the internal needle inside the holder, so an incompletely seated tube will not draw blood even with otherwise correct needle placement in the vein. Reseating the tube firmly is the first troubleshooting step to try before assuming the needle itself has missed the vein. Checking needle position and angle comes next only if reseating fails to resolve the issue. This simple mechanical check saves an unnecessary second puncture in many cases.
  7. The correct answer is D (The blood is transferred gently using a dedicated transfer device into each tube): a blood transfer device attaches to the syringe and allows the tube's own vacuum to draw the sample in gently, avoiding the excess force and needlestick risk associated with injecting through the stopper or removing the cap entirely. Gentle transfer also reduces the risk of hemolysis compared with forceful manual injection into a tube. This method preserves the correct order of draw across multiple tubes filled from a single syringe collection. It is the accepted technique whenever a syringe draw is required instead of a direct evacuated tube collection.
  8. The correct answer is A (The connecting tubing itself contains a small amount of air that must be accounted for): the flexible tubing of a butterfly set holds a small volume of air before blood actually reaches the first tube, so when a citrate coagulation tube is drawn first in the sequence, a discard tube is used ahead of it to maintain the correct blood-to-additive ratio. This is a mechanical consideration specific to winged sets that does not apply to a straight needle and holder combination. Being aware of this tubing volume protects the accuracy of downstream coagulation testing results. The correct order of draw itself still governs which tube comes first overall.
  9. The correct answer is B (It should already have been released well before the needle is withdrawn): releasing the tourniquet before withdrawing the needle reduces pressure within the vein, which lowers the risk of hematoma formation and excess bleeding once the needle is finally removed. Withdrawing the needle while the tourniquet is still tight leaves the vessel under elevated pressure at the exact moment the puncture site is reopened. This particular sequencing is a deliberate safety step taught alongside the mechanics of the draw itself. It is separate from, and comes before, the tourniquet timing concern related to hemoconcentration.
  10. The correct answer is C (It should be activated for safety and placed directly into a sharps container): needles are disposed of immediately after withdrawal, with any safety mechanism engaged, straight into a puncture-resistant sharps container without setting them down or handing them to anyone else first. This single continuous action prevents accidental needlestick injury during the genuinely vulnerable moments right after use. Recapping a used needle is never performed under any circumstance in modern phlebotomy practice today. Immediate direct disposal is one of the most consistently emphasized safety rules taught throughout the field.
  11. The correct answer is A (Recapping is a leading, well documented cause of accidental needlestick injury among staff): attempting to guide a used needle back into its cap, even carefully, creates a genuine risk of the sharp tip missing the cap entirely and puncturing the hand instead. This risk exists regardless of technique or experience level, which is exactly why the practice is banned outright rather than merely discouraged. Immediate disposal straight into a sharps container eliminates this risk entirely and reliably. Needlestick prevention remains one of the most important occupational safety priorities in phlebotomy work.
  12. The correct answer is D (Advise the patient to keep the bandage on and avoid heavy lifting with that arm): giving clear post-draw instructions helps prevent bleeding, bruising, or hematoma formation after the patient has left the collection area entirely. Avoiding strenuous use of the arm and keeping the bandage on for a reasonable period both support proper healing of the puncture site. Skipping these instructions leaves the patient without guidance during the exact period when complications are most likely to appear. This step is a routine and expected part of completing every single venipuncture.
  13. The correct answer is C (Labels applied away from the patient risk being attached to the wrong specimen): every tube must be labeled at the patient's own bedside immediately after collection, while the patient is still physically present, to eliminate any chance of mixing up specimens between different patients. Carrying unlabeled tubes to another location, even briefly, breaks this positive identification chain and is considered an unacceptable practice under any circumstance. This rule exists specifically to prevent misidentified specimens, which can have serious downstream consequences for patient care overall. Bedside labeling is treated as a non-negotiable step in every collection performed.
  14. The correct answer is B (Patient full name, a unique identification number, date and time of collection, and collector identification): a complete label ties the specimen unambiguously to one specific patient and records exactly who collected it and precisely when, which supports both patient safety and specimen traceability throughout processing. Missing any one of these elements can make a specimen unacceptable to the laboratory and require an entirely new recollection. Room numbers alone are not reliable identifiers since patients can be moved or share similar room assignments over time. This full set of elements is required on every single tube collected without exception.
  15. The correct answer is D (A hematoma caused by blood leaking outward into the surrounding soft tissue): swelling and bluish discolouration developing shortly after a draw are classic signs that blood has escaped the vessel into surrounding tissue, often from incomplete vessel closure or pressure changes occurring during the procedure itself. Applying firm direct pressure promptly after needle withdrawal is the main way this particular complication is prevented from developing further. Hemoconcentration is a laboratory result effect rather than a visible tissue change occurring at the puncture site. Recognizing hematoma formation quickly allows appropriate pressure and reassurance to be given to the patient.
  16. The correct answer is A (Withdraw the needle immediately since this reaction may indicate possible nerve involvement): sudden sharp, shooting, or electric-like pain occurring during needle repositioning is a genuine warning sign of possible nerve contact, and the needle should be withdrawn right away rather than continuing to probe further beneath the skin. Blindly redirecting a needle in search of a vein, sometimes called fishing, significantly raises the risk of nerve injury and is never appropriate technique under any circumstance. Once withdrawn, the phlebotomist should reassess and consider a different site or another attempt by qualified staff instead. Prompt response to this symptom protects the patient from a potentially lasting injury.
  17. The correct answer is C (It meaningfully increases the risk of nerve injury along with other tissue damage): moving a needle blindly under the skin in multiple directions, often called fishing, risks contacting nearby nerves, arteries, or other structures close to the intended vein and can cause lasting injury to the patient. The recommended response when a vein is not located promptly is to withdraw fully and reassess rather than continuing to probe further beneath the skin. This risk exists even without an immediate pain complaint from the patient, since nerve proximity is not always announced clearly by symptoms right away. Avoiding this technique entirely is a core safety principle taught from the very start of training.
  18. The correct answer is B (A possible vasovagal reaction requiring the draw to stop and the patient positioned safely): pallor, lightheadedness, and sweating are recognized early signs of a vasovagal reaction, and the phlebotomist should stop the procedure right away, remove the tourniquet and needle if still in place, and help the patient into a safe position such as lying down with the legs elevated. Continuing the draw despite these clear warning signs risks a fall or loss of consciousness happening shortly after. Reassurance and close monitoring should continue until the patient's colour and symptoms genuinely improve. Recognizing this reaction early prevents it from escalating into a more serious event overall.
  19. The correct answer is D (Continue monitoring the patient until symptoms have fully resolved and moving is safe): a patient recovering from a vasovagal episode should be observed until colour, alertness, and overall stability have genuinely returned before any further movement or activity is attempted. Standing up too soon risks a recurrence of symptoms or an actual fall shortly afterward. Resuming the original collection attempt is not appropriate until the patient has stabilized fully and consents to continue with the process. Careful ongoing monitoring protects the patient and reflects appropriate clinical judgment following this kind of event.
  20. The correct answer is C (Discontinue further attempts and request that another qualified staff member try instead): the accepted standard limits any single phlebotomist to a maximum of two attempts before involving a different qualified staff member, since repeated unsuccessful attempts increase patient discomfort and overall complication risk considerably. Continuing beyond that limit personally is not appropriate even if the phlebotomist feels genuinely confident about a third potential site. Escalating to another trained person offers the patient a fresh assessment along with a different technique. This limit is a well established patient safety guideline throughout Canadian phlebotomy practice.
  21. The correct answer is A (It confirms the correct patient before any equipment is exposed or applied at all): verifying identity with two identifiers before any supplies touch the patient ensures that every subsequent step, from tourniquet application through to labeling, is happening with the correct person throughout. This early check does not replace bedside labeling, which is a separate and equally mandatory step performed after collection is complete. Skipping or delaying identification risks an error that could affect the entire remainder of the encounter. Two-identifier confirmation right at the very start is a foundational patient safety practice.
  22. The correct answer is B (Avoid this vessel entirely because it likely represents a sclerosed or thrombosed vein): a hard, cord-like vessel that does not rebound after compression has typically been damaged by prior punctures or clotting and will not reliably yield a usable blood flow during a draw. Attempting venipuncture on such a vessel often leads to a failed draw and unnecessary discomfort for the patient. A healthy vein instead feels round, resilient, and springs back promptly after gentle pressure. Recognizing this distinction by touch alone is central to appropriate and safe site selection.
  23. The correct answer is D (A vein can still be an appropriate choice based on touch even without clear visibility): phlebotomists are trained to rely primarily on palpation, feeling for a round, resilient vessel that rebounds after pressure, rather than depending solely on how visually prominent the vein appears. Many perfectly suitable veins are not strongly visible, particularly in patients with more subcutaneous tissue, yet still palpate very well overall. Rejecting a site purely for lack of visibility would eliminate many genuinely viable options unnecessarily during selection. This is exactly why touch, rather than sight, is emphasized as the primary selection tool.
  24. The correct answer is A (Release the tourniquet, allow circulation to normalize, then reapply before continuing the search): once a search approaches or exceeds the recommended one-minute guideline without success, releasing the tourniquet lets circulation return to normal before it is safely reapplied to continue with site selection. Leaving it on continuously instead risks hemoconcentration and unnecessary patient discomfort from prolonged constriction of the limb. This release-and-reapply approach is the recommended way to manage a search that genuinely takes longer than expected. It balances thorough site selection with proper respect for the tourniquet timing guideline.
  25. The correct answer is C (A stinging sensation for the patient and a possible effect on the resulting specimen): puncturing skin before alcohol has fully air dried can cause a noticeable stinging sensation for the patient and has been associated with an increased risk of hemolysis in the collected sample. This is exactly why phlebotomists wait for natural air drying rather than wiping the site down or proceeding immediately after cleansing. The tourniquet and choice of antiseptic agent are unrelated to this specific timing issue described here. Allowing full drying time is a simple step that protects both patient comfort and specimen quality overall.
  26. The correct answer is B (It should be adjusted toward the steeper end of the standard 15 to 30 degree range): a deeper vein requires a somewhat steeper angle within the accepted range so the needle travels far enough beneath the surface to reach the vessel without excessive lateral movement during insertion. Using a very shallow angle on a deep vein risks stopping short of the lumen entirely and missing it. Angles far beyond the standard range are avoided since they raise the risk of passing straight through the vein or striking deeper structures nearby. Judging depth accurately during palpation helps guide this particular angle decision each time.
  27. The correct answer is A (An upward bevel allows smoother entry into the vein and supports even blood flow): an upward-facing bevel creates a cleaner opening into the vein and helps blood flow steadily into the tube or syringe once the vessel has actually been entered. A downward-facing bevel can increase the chance of the needle opening pressing directly against the vessel wall and disrupting flow altogether. This orientation is checked as a routine part of preparing to insert the needle for any patient. Correct bevel positioning contributes meaningfully to a successful, single-attempt collection overall.
  28.
