The correct answer is D (The needle should be held steady and stable while the tubes are exchanged): once the vein has been entered successfully, the needle and holder should be kept as still as possible while tubes are removed and replaced in the correct order of draw, since unnecessary movement risks dislodging the needle from the vessel entirely. Advancing, withdrawing, or rotating the needle during this phase increases the chance of infiltration or a failed collection overall. A steady hand throughout each tube change is a key part of smooth, controlled technique. This stability supports both patient comfort and overall specimen quality throughout the draw.
  29. The correct answer is B (It prevents additive carryover between successive tubes from affecting downstream test results): collecting tubes in the established sequence limits the chance that additive from one tube contaminates the next tube drawn, which could otherwise interfere meaningfully with certain test results afterward. This concern applies to both straight needle and winged set collections equally, not just one particular method. Following the sequence is largely independent of how quickly the overall draw itself is completed. The full detailed sequence is covered in more depth elsewhere, but respecting some correct order remains essential regardless of the collection device used.
  30. The correct answer is C (It allows the additive to mix promptly with blood before clotting or settling begins): gentle inversion right after filling ensures anticoagulants or other additives distribute evenly through the sample before the blood has any chance to begin clotting or settling unevenly inside the tube. Delaying this mixing until later, such as after arrival at the laboratory, risks micro-clot formation or an otherwise unusable specimen entirely. Inversion is unrelated to confirming vacuum function or serving as a substitute for proper bedside labeling. Timely mixing is a simple step that protects specimen integrity right at the point of collection.
  31. The correct answer is D (The tourniquet is released first, and the needle is only withdrawn afterward): releasing the tourniquet before withdrawing the needle allows venous pressure to normalize so that when the puncture site is finally opened, meaningfully less blood escapes into the surrounding tissue. Withdrawing the needle first while the tourniquet is still constricting the arm leaves the vessel under higher pressure at the exact moment of removal. This particular sequence is a deliberate safety step rather than an arbitrary personal preference among practitioners. Following it consistently helps reduce the likelihood of hematoma formation after every draw.
  32. The correct answer is A (Post-puncture safety steps happen in one continuous motion without ever setting the needle down): withdrawing the needle, applying pressure, and disposing of the sharp are performed as one uninterrupted sequence so the used needle is never set down or handled unnecessarily anywhere along the way, which minimizes needlestick risk considerably. Capping the needle beforehand is never appropriate, and delaying disposal to attend to other tasks meaningfully increases exposure risk. Gauze is applied at the moment of withdrawal, not before it, since the needle must first be removed from the vessel entirely. This tight sequencing is a core safety expectation embedded in every venipuncture performed.
  33. The correct answer is B (That the site shows no active bleeding and no early signs of swelling): confirming that bleeding has genuinely stopped and that no swelling is developing before applying the bandage helps ensure a hematoma is not quietly forming beneath a covered site where it would be harder to notice afterward. Bandaging over a site that is still bleeding or beginning to swell can mask a developing complication rather than prevent one from occurring. This brief visual check takes only a moment but remains an important safeguard for the patient. It is a routine part of properly closing out every single venipuncture performed.
  34. The correct answer is C (It is the only point at which correct patient identity can be positively confirmed): labeling while still at the bedside, with the patient physically present, is the only moment that genuinely guarantees the tubes in hand belong to the patient just identified, since carrying unlabeled tubes elsewhere introduces the risk of mix-up with another patient's specimens entirely. This requirement exists specifically for patient safety rather than any administrative convenience or billing concern. It does not remove the need to reverify identity again for the very next patient encountered afterward. Bedside labeling is treated as an inseparable part of the collection procedure itself.
  35. The correct answer is D (The collection date and time along with clear identification of who collected it): a complete label records exactly when the specimen was drawn and precisely who drew it, which supports specimen stability assessments and overall accountability if a question later arises about the actual collection. Address and health card expiry date are not part of the standard label content required by policy. Manufacturer lot numbers are also not the phlebotomist's responsibility to record directly on the tube label. These four core elements together, name, identification number, date and time, and collector identity, make up a fully acceptable label.
  36. The correct answer is A (A hematoma that likely formed from insufficient post-draw pressure or vessel injury): a growing, tender, discoloured lump at a recent puncture site is consistent with a hematoma, which can result from inadequate pressure duration, vessel fragility, or the needle having passed through the far wall of the vein during collection. Patients experiencing this should be advised on appropriate self-care and told to seek further assessment if the swelling continues to increase or other concerning symptoms develop. Hemoconcentration is a laboratory measurement effect, not an actual physical lump forming under the skin. Prevention through adequate post-draw pressure remains the best way to reduce this particular complication.
  37. The correct answer is C (Stop the collection right away, attend to the patient, and position them safely): pallor, warmth, and dizziness occurring during a draw are early warning signs of a possible vasovagal reaction, and the priority is to stop collecting immediately and attend to the patient's safety rather than trying to finish the remaining tubes first. Continuing the procedure despite these clear symptoms risks the patient losing consciousness while the needle is still in place inside the vein. The needle should be withdrawn and the patient positioned safely, typically reclined with the legs elevated somewhat. Patient wellbeing always takes priority over completing a planned specimen collection.
  38. The correct answer is B (A maximum of two total attempts before another qualified staff member must be involved): standard practice limits one phlebotomist to two total attempts, after which a different qualified staff member should be brought in rather than allowing repeated attempts to continue on indefinitely. This limit protects the patient from unnecessary discomfort and reduces the cumulative risk of complications such as hematoma or nerve injury developing. Patient consent to continue trying does not override this well established limit on total attempts. Escalating appropriately after two attempts reflects genuinely sound professional judgment on the phlebotomist's part.
  39. The correct answer is D (Preparation and identification occur before antisepsis, needle insertion, and final labeling steps): the overall procedure follows a logical progression, beginning with requisition review and patient identification, then equipment preparation and hand hygiene, followed by site selection, tourniquet application, antisepsis, insertion, tube filling, withdrawal, and finally bedside labeling and bandaging at the end. Antisepsis is performed after tourniquet application and site selection, not before, since the vein must first be located through palpation. Labeling is one of the last steps, performed at the bedside only after collection is fully complete. Understanding this overall sequence helps a phlebotomist perform the entire procedure smoothly and safely.
  40. The correct answer is A (Applying pressure too early would interfere directly with the needle's removal from the vein): pressing gauze onto the site while the needle is still partially inside the vessel can drag the needle against the vein wall during withdrawal, increasing the risk of injury or an incomplete, jagged closure of the puncture. Waiting until the needle is fully withdrawn before applying pressure allows a clean removal followed immediately by proper hemostasis at the site. This timing detail is a small but genuinely meaningful part of smooth, controlled technique overall. It applies equally to all patients, not just a specific age group, since the underlying mechanical concern remains the same.
  41. The correct answer is B (Gently invert the tube three to four times to mix it with the anticoagulant): citrate tubes require prompt, gentle inversion to distribute the anticoagulant and prevent micro-clot formation that would invalidate coagulation testing. Vigorous shaking risks mechanical hemolysis, which also compromises results. Delaying or skipping mixing allows localized clotting even though the whole tube may still appear liquid. Proper technique protects the accuracy of PT and INR results drawn from that specimen.
  42. The correct answer is D (Hemolysis, since red cell rupture releases intracellular contents into serum): pink-red serum after centrifugation is the classic appearance of hemolysis, where ruptured red cells release potassium, LDH, and AST into the surrounding serum and falsely elevate those results. Lipemic serum appears cloudy and milky white rather than pink, and icteric serum appears deep amber rather than red-tinged. Because the true potassium level cannot be distinguished from this artifact, the specimen must be flagged and typically redrawn.
  43. The correct answer is A (The specimen is icteric, consistent with a genuinely elevated bilirubin level): deep amber serum is the icteric appearance, typically reflecting genuinely elevated bilirubin from liver dysfunction or biliary obstruction rather than a collection error. Unlike hemolysis or lipemia, icterus often reflects the patient's true clinical state, so the specimen may still be processed while the icterus index is noted on the report. The technologist correlates the visual finding with the ordered bilirubin result. Recognizing this appearance helps the technician avoid mistaking a real clinical finding for a specimen collection error.
  44. The correct answer is A (Using a needle bore too narrow, meaning too high a gauge number, for the vacuum draw applied): a narrow-bore needle, meaning a higher gauge number such as 23 to 25 gauge, combined with the vacuum pressure of an evacuated tube forces red cells through a fine lumen at high velocity, rupturing them and causing hemolysis even when the venipuncture itself seemed technically smooth. A well-anchored, visible vein and a short tourniquet time both reduce hemolysis risk rather than causing it. Following the correct order of draw also has no bearing on mechanical hemolysis from needle bore size. Selecting an appropriately sized needle for the vein and tube volume prevents this artifact.
  45. The correct answer is B (A lipemic specimen resulting from a recent, non-fasting meal intake): cloudy, milky-white serum is the lipemic appearance, typically caused by high circulating triglycerides after a recent meal rather than by a collection error. Because the patient was not fasting before this draw, the technician should document the timing and notify the requesting provider that a fasting redraw may be needed. Hemolyzed serum looks pink-red rather than milky, and icteric serum looks amber rather than cloudy. Recognizing lipemia helps distinguish a dietary factor from an actual pre-analytical mistake in collection.
  46. The correct answer is A (It separates serum from the cellular components using the gel layer): during centrifugation, the thixotropic gel in a barrier tube migrates to sit between the settled cells and the serum, physically separating them so serum can be safely pipetted for testing. This barrier also helps prevent analyte exchange between serum and cells if processing is briefly delayed. Centrifugation does not remove anticoagulant, since serum tubes contain none to begin with. Understanding this step explains why the tube must clot fully before it is centrifuged for release.
  47. The correct answer is D (The specimen was exposed to light, which can falsely lower bilirubin): bilirubin degrades on exposure to light, so specimens for bilirubin testing must be wrapped in foil or placed in an amber bag immediately after collection rather than left exposed under laboratory lighting. This is unrelated to warming, clotting, or mixing, all of which involve entirely different mechanisms of specimen error. The resulting falsely low bilirubin value could mislead clinical decision-making regarding a jaundiced patient. Prompt light protection preserves the specimen's true analyte concentration until testing occurs.
  48. The correct answer is C (The tube clotted before analysis and must be rejected for redraw): a solid mass with little or no visible serum or plasma layer indicates the specimen clotted, most often from inadequate or delayed mixing of an anticoagulated tube, and it cannot be tested because clot activity has consumed clotting factors and trapped cells. This is distinct from lipemia or icterus, which both still produce a measurable liquid layer despite their discoloration. A clotted anticoagulant tube is a standard rejection criterion in Canadian laboratory practice. The specimen must be redrawn rather than manipulated to force a usable result.
  49. The correct answer is A (Transport the tube on ice to the laboratory without any avoidable delay): ammonia is metabolically unstable at room temperature and continues to rise in the tube after collection, so specimens must be placed on ice immediately and transported to the laboratory without delay. Leaving the tube at room temperature or warming it would accelerate this false rise rather than prevent it. Light exposure is a concern for analytes like bilirubin, not for ammonia specifically. Rapid, cold transport is essential to obtain an accurate result for this particular test.
  50. The correct answer is B (It must be kept warm and transported promptly rather than placed on ice): cold agglutinins and cryoglobulins react in vitro when a specimen cools, causing red cells to clump and interfering with accurate results, so these specimens must be kept near body temperature and transported without delay rather than iced. This is the opposite handling requirement from analytes like ammonia or blood gases collected earlier in the same shift. Bedside centrifugation and light protection are not the relevant concerns for this particular test. Recognizing which analytes need warmth versus cold prevents a preventable pre-analytical error.
  51. The correct answer is C (The specimen appears free of hemolysis, lipemia, and icterus artifacts): pale, straw-yellow serum sitting as a clean layer above the packed cells is the expected normal appearance, showing no visible hemolysis, lipemia, or icterus that would interfere with electrolyte results. This appearance supports proceeding with routine chemistry testing without additional flags being raised. A pale colour is expected and does not itself indicate a problem, unlike the pink, cloudy, or amber discoloration seen in true artifact specimens. Recognizing a normal appearance is just as important as recognizing an abnormal one.
  52. The correct answer is D (It disrupts the required nine-to-one blood-to-anticoagulant ratio needed): citrate tubes are formulated for a precise nine-to-one ratio of blood to anticoagulant, and an underfilled tube shifts that ratio, leaving relative excess citrate that falsely prolongs coagulation results. The problem is excess anticoagulant relative to blood, not insufficient anticoagulant as the similar-sounding distractor suggests. Underfilling does not by itself cause spontaneous clotting or hemolysis on its own. Because the ratio cannot be corrected after the fact, quantity-not-sufficient citrate specimens must be rejected and redrawn properly.
  53. The correct answer is B (Reject the specimen and arrange for a completely new sample to be collected): a mismatched or mislabeled specimen must be rejected and redrawn rather than relabeled after leaving the patient's side, because there is no way to verify patient identity retroactively once the specimen has left bedside control. Relabeling from a requisition risks attaching the wrong identity to the wrong sample entirely. Testing an unverifiable specimen or asking a physician to guess which label is correct both introduce unacceptable identification risk. This strict redraw policy protects patient safety above simple convenience.
  54. The correct answer is A (Report the hemolysis and request a redraw rather than trust this value): hemolysis releases intracellular potassium into the serum, producing a falsely elevated result that does not reflect the patient's true status, so a hemolyzed specimen for potassium must be flagged and a new sample collected. Releasing an unreliable value could lead to inappropriate treatment for a false laboratory abnormality. Diluting the specimen does not remove the released potassium and would distort other analytes on the panel. Re-centrifuging cannot reverse cell rupture that already occurred, so redraw remains the only reliable option here.
  55. The correct answer is D (A documented, unbroken chain of custody with sealed, tamper-evident containers): forensic and legal specimens require a documented, unbroken chain of custody, with every person who handles the specimen signing, and sealed, tamper-evident containers used throughout transport and storage. This documentation trail protects the specimen's legal admissibility, unlike routine handling steps such as icing or light protection, which address analyte stability rather than legal defensibility. Any gap in the signature trail can render the result inadmissible in later proceedings. This is a distinct requirement layered on top of, not instead of, routine collection practice.
  56.   57. Silently proceeding hides information the physician genuinely needs for accurate interpretation of the panel. Falsifying the requisition to claim fasting status compliance is a serious documentation violation. Discarding the result without communication removes clinically useful information rather than simply contextualizing it.
  58. Falsely labeling a non-fasting draw as fasting misrepresents the specimen's true validity to the interpreting clinician. Refusing collection or silently canceling the order both withhold potentially needed clinical information from the care team. Transparent documentation lets the clinical team decide whether a repeat fasting draw is necessary.
  59. The correct answer is A (POCT devices use whole blood and different methodology than the central lab): point-of-care devices typically analyze whole blood using different technology and calibration than a central laboratory analyzer, which processes separated plasma or serum, so some numerical difference between the two is expected and not necessarily an error. POCT devices do not centrifuge specimens, since that step is exactly what they are designed to bypass for rapid results. Central lab testing does require processing steps such as centrifugation and separation before analysis. POCT results are used clinically, particularly for rapid decisions, but staff should understand their methodological limits.
  60. This is a clinically expected appearance in patients such as neonates with hyperbilirubinemia requiring monitoring. Distinguishing an expected clinical finding from an error avoids unnecessary specimen rejection in this scenario.
  61. The correct answer is D (A delta check, comparing a new result against the patient's own prior one): a delta check compares a patient's current result to their own previous result to detect an unexpected shift that may signal a pre-analytical error, such as a mislabeled or mishandled specimen, rather than a true clinical change. It is a patient-specific comparison rather than a chain-of-custody, calibrator, or inter-laboratory process entirely. A significant delta prompts staff to verify patient identity and specimen handling before releasing the result. Understanding this concept helps the technician appreciate why accurate collection and labeling matter well beyond the single draw.
  62. Vigorous shaking risks mechanical hemolysis and cell damage that can distort the resulting differential count. Skipping inversion leaves anticoagulant unevenly distributed, risking clotting in localized areas of the tube. Immersing the tube in ice water is unnecessary for a routine complete blood count and is not part of standard EDTA handling procedure.
  63. The correct answer is A (Reject the tube as clotted and request that an urgent redraw be performed): a heparin tube that has clotted, appearing as a solid mass with essentially no liquid layer, must be rejected because the additive failed to prevent coagulation, likely from inadequate mixing, and reliable plasma cannot be recovered from it. Extended centrifugation cannot separate a specimen that has already clotted throughout its entire volume. Reporting results with a clotting disclaimer is not acceptable since clotted specimens invalidate results rather than mildly affecting them. Manual filtration is not a recognized substitute for proper specimen collection and would risk further error.
  64. The correct answer is A (Mechanical hemolysis from the forceful, rapid agitation applied to red cells): vigorous shaking, rather than gentle inversion, subjects red cells to mechanical stress that ruptures them and causes hemolysis, which can falsely elevate analytes such as potassium and LDH. Excessive mixing does not cause under-mixing or micro-clots, which are actually the opposite problem entirely. Coagulation times are affected by clot formation or ratio errors, not by forceful mixing technique alone. Evaporation is not a realistic outcome of a brief mixing action and is not a recognized specimen-handling concern in this scenario.
  65. The correct answer is B (Eight to ten gentle inversions to distribute the heparin additive evenly): heparin tubes generally require about eight to ten gentle inversions to evenly distribute the anticoagulant throughout the blood and prevent clot formation before plasma can be tested. The lower three to four inversion count belongs to citrate tubes, not to heparin tubes like this one. Twenty or more inversions is excessive and increases the risk of mechanical hemolysis without adding any real benefit. Additives do not distribute on their own, so skipping inversion risks localized clotting even in a heparin tube.

  68. This inversion count differs from the eight to ten used for heparin or EDTA tubes drawn for other panels. Skipping inversion entirely can lead to uneven clotting and fibrin strands that interfere with serum separation. Excessive inversion beyond the recommended range offers no added benefit and increases the risk of hemolysis.
  69.   70. SST tubes contain no anticoagulant to dissolve, since they rely on clotting rather than anticoagulation to work. Centrifugation does not chemically warm the specimen or trigger any analyte reactions inside the tube. It also does not sterilize the tube, which is unnecessary for standard chemistry testing.

Cooling is not the standard protocol here despite being appropriate for other analytes like ammonia collected nearby. Vigorous shaking is never an appropriate mixing technique for any specimen type in this laboratory. Light protection is unrelated to cryoglobulin stability and would not correct this specific error.

  83. Analyzer recalibration is monitored through separate quality-control material rather than through patient-to-patient result comparisons.   86.   87. The correct answer is C (That the specimen was protected from light with foil or a dedicated amber bag): bilirubin specimens must be shielded from light throughout collection and transport, since light exposure degrades bilirubin and can falsely lower the measured result, potentially masking a clinically significant elevation in a newborn. Icing addresses temperature-sensitive analytes such as ammonia, not light sensitivity, and is not the relevant concern for bilirubin here. Warming applies to cold agglutinin testing, an entirely unrelated analyte and clinical scenario. Repeating centrifugation does not address light exposure and is not a standard step for confirming bilirubin stability.
  88. The correct answer is B (Incomplete centrifugation can leave the gel barrier improperly formed as a result): running a centrifuge cycle shorter than validated can leave the gel barrier incompletely formed and cells inadequately separated from serum, risking contamination of the serum layer and unreliable results. A shorter cycle does not produce a more concentrated or purer sample, since proper separation depends on the full validated time and speed being used. Centrifuge timing meaningfully affects serum quality and separation, contrary to the claim that it has no measurable effect. Standard centrifugation procedures apply broadly, including to SST tubes, not exclusively to anticoagulated specimens.
  89. The correct answer is B (Wrap the tube in foil or place it in an amber bag immediately after collection): both bilirubin and folate are light-sensitive analytes that degrade under light exposure, so a single tube collected for both tests should be wrapped in foil or placed in an amber bag immediately after the draw to protect its accuracy. Icing without light protection addresses temperature-sensitive analytes but leaves this tube's light-sensitive analytes unprotected from degradation. Leaving the tube exposed to normal lighting would allow ongoing degradation of both bilirubin and folate. Warming the specimen has no protective role for either analyte and is not indicated here at all.
  90. The correct answer is B (Document the recent meal and flag the result as potentially lipemic overall): when a lipid specimen appears lipemic and the patient reports recently eating, the technician should document the timing and flag the result so the provider understands the finding may reflect the recent meal rather than a true fasting lipid level. Discarding the specimen without documentation removes information useful for later clinical interpretation. Relabeling the specimen as fasting when it was not is a serious documentation integrity violation. Reporting the result as a normal fasting profile misrepresents the specimen's actual collection conditions to the provider.
  91. The correct answer is D (Immediate icing and prompt delivery to the laboratory for processing right away): lactic acid continues to be produced by ongoing cellular metabolism in the tube after collection, so like ammonia, the specimen must be iced immediately and delivered promptly to prevent a falsely elevated result. Lactic acid is not light-sensitive, so foil protection is not the relevant handling step for this particular analyte. Warming would accelerate rather than prevent metabolic changes, making it the wrong approach entirely. Lactic acid specimens are not stable at room temperature, so no special handling is not an accurate description of this test's true requirements.
  92. The correct answer is C (The blood-to-anticoagulant ratio is disrupted regardless of the mixing quality seen): an underfilled citrate tube disrupts the required nine-to-one blood-to-anticoagulant ratio no matter how thoroughly it was mixed, because the excess citrate relative to blood volume falsely prolongs coagulation results independent of mixing technique used. Good mixing distributes the anticoagulant evenly but cannot correct an incorrect overall ratio present in the tube. Underfilled citrate tubes are rejected based on volume alone, not only when a visible clot is also present. Ratio accuracy, not mixing quality alone, is the determining factor for whether a citrate specimen is valid.
  93. The correct answer is D (The near-total absence of a distinct liquid serum or plasma layer forming): the clotted tube's defining feature is the near-total absence of a separated liquid serum or plasma layer, since clot formation traps the liquid within a solid mass rather than allowing normal centrifugal separation. A pink-red tint describes the hemolyzed tube on the chart, not the clotted one being discussed. A cloudy, milky-white appearance describes the lipemic tube shown elsewhere on the chart. A deep amber colour describes the icteric tube rather than the clotted example being taught here.
  94. The correct answer is B (It provides visible proof if the container was opened outside the documented chain): a tamper-evident seal shows visible evidence, such as a broken seal or altered label, if the container was opened by anyone outside the documented chain of custody, supporting the legal integrity of the result. It has no effect on maintaining specimen temperature during transport, which would require appropriate icing or insulation instead. Alcohol stability is not primarily a light-sensitivity concern, so the seal does not serve that purpose here. This requirement is specific to forensic and legally significant specimens rather than a routine step applied to every specimen collected.
  95. The correct answer is A (Release it for testing since no visible pre-analytical artifact is present at all): a specimen matching the normal, pale, cleanly separated appearance shows no visible sign of hemolysis, lipemia, icterus, or clotting, so it can proceed to testing without further visual review needed. A second centrifugation cycle is unnecessary once a clean separation has already been achieved in the tube. Pale serum is the expected, reliable appearance rather than a reason for automatic rejection. Flagging a clearly normal specimen for hemolysis review would be an unnecessary step that delays result reporting without clinical justification.
  96. The correct answer is B (Gently invert the tube eight to ten times end over end): EDTA must contact the entire blood volume promptly to chelate calcium and prevent clotting, and eight to ten gentle inversions is the accepted range for lavender-top tubes. Vigorous shaking creates mechanical shear that ruptures red cells and causes hemolysis, which can falsely elevate potassium and other analytes. Leaving the tube unmixed risks micro-clots that will jam automated hematology analyzers and require a redraw. Tapping the tube does not distribute anticoagulant through the sample and offers no benefit.
  97. The correct answer is D (Reject the tube and recollect using a fresh, properly filled tube): coagulation testing depends on a precise nine to one ratio of blood to sodium citrate, and an underfilled tube dilutes the plasma relative to anticoagulant, producing a falsely prolonged clotting time. Diluting the sample further with saline would worsen the ratio error rather than fix it. Sending a known quantity-not-sufficient specimen forward shifts a preventable error onto the testing laboratory and risks an inaccurate coagulation result reaching the ordering physician. A correctly filled tube is the only reliable way to protect the integrity of this test.
  98. The correct answer is A (The tube was hemolyzed during or after collection): a pink or reddish tinge in the plasma after separation is the classic visual sign of hemolysis, where red cells have ruptured and released hemoglobin into the surrounding fluid. Common mechanical causes include a needle gauge that is too small for the vacuum draw, forcing blood through a syringe needle into the tube, or overly vigorous mixing. Heparin concentration issues would not produce this pink discoloration. A second spin will not remove already-released intracellular hemoglobin from the plasma.
  99. The correct answer is C (Wrap the tube in foil or place it in an amber transport bag right away): both bilirubin and folate are light-sensitive analytes that degrade with prolonged exposure to light, so protecting the specimen with foil or an amber bag immediately after draw preserves an accurate result. Icing the tube addresses cold-chain analytes like ammonia or lactic acid, not light protection, and is not the concern here. Leaving the tube uncovered in ordinary light allows ongoing photodegradation of both bilirubin and folate. Warming the specimen has no relevance to protecting a light-sensitive analyte.
  100. The correct answer is B (Place them on ice immediately and transport to the lab promptly): both ammonia and lactic acid are metabolically unstable analytes whose values rise quickly at room temperature due to ongoing cellular metabolism, so chilling the specimen on ice from the moment of collection slows this degradation. Room temperature transport would allow falsely elevated results to develop before the specimen ever reaches the analyzer. Foil protection addresses light sensitivity, which is not the issue for these two analytes. Warming the specimen would accelerate rather than slow the metabolic changes that cause falsely high results.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Keep it warm, close to body temperature, and transport it promptly): cold agglutinins are antibodies that bind red cells together at temperatures below body temperature, so a cooled specimen can show falsely elevated agglutination that does not reflect the patient's true titer. Icing or refrigerating this tube, the handling correct for analytes like ammonia, would actively induce the very reaction the test is trying to measure accurately. Prompt transport at a warm temperature prevents in-vitro agglutination from occurring before the sample reaches testing. Leaving it at ordinary room temperature for an extended period still risks cooling enough to trigger agglutination.
  2. The correct answer is D (Eight to ten gentle inversions, similar to the EDTA tube): grey-top tubes contain both an anticoagulant and a glycolysis inhibitor that must be distributed evenly throughout the blood, and eight to ten gentle inversions is the accepted range to accomplish this without causing hemolysis. Skipping mixing entirely risks clot formation and uneven preservative distribution, which can allow glucose to keep breaking down in parts of the sample. A single inversion or two is inadequate to fully mix the additive with the blood volume. Twenty or more inversions is excessive and increases the risk of mechanical hemolysis.
  3. The correct answer is C (It can cause hemoconcentration and contribute to hemolysis): prolonged tourniquet application restricts venous outflow while arterial inflow continues, concentrating larger cellular and protein components in the vein and increasing the mechanical stress on red cells as they are drawn, which can contribute to hemolysis. This is not a negligible effect, since hemoconcentration can shift results for proteins, cellular counts, and electrolytes. It does not guarantee clotting inside an anticoagulated tube, since clotting depends on additive mixing rather than tourniquet time alone. The effect is not limited only to coagulation testing, since hemoconcentration can influence multiple analyte categories.
  4. The correct answer is C (Hematoma sites commonly yield already-damaged, hemolysis-prone blood at the site): drawing from a hematoma pulls blood that has already leaked into surrounding tissue and mixed with damaged cells and fluid, which predisposes the sample to hemolysis before it even reaches the tube. Blaming centrifugation alone ignores the well-documented pre-analytical risk that hematoma sites pose during collection itself. Claiming hematoma blood is never associated with hemolysis contradicts established phlebotomy practice, which specifically warns against drawing from these sites. Standard tube additives are not a typical cause of hemolysis when used correctly.
  5. The correct answer is B (It does not require the same additive-mixing inversions as anticoagulated tubes): a plain red-top serum tube has no anticoagulant additive that needs distribution through the blood, since the sample is allowed to clot naturally rather than being kept in a liquid state, so it does not need the inversion count required for lavender, green, or grey tubes. Applying the same protocol as EDTA is unnecessary and not standard practice for this tube type. Vigorous shaking risks hemolysis regardless of additive status and offers no clotting benefit. While clotting time before centrifugation matters, there is no requirement to spin within thirty seconds specifically.
  6. The correct answer is D (It physically separates serum or plasma from the cellular layer): barrier gel has a density between that of the cells and the serum or plasma, so during centrifugation it migrates to sit exactly between the two layers, creating a stable physical barrier that prevents ongoing contact between cells and the liquid portion. This separation, not a chemical conversion, is what keeps analytes stable by stopping continued cellular metabolism and diffusion into the serum. The gel does not remove hemoglobin already released by hemolysis, since that hemoglobin remains dissolved in the liquid layer. Coagulation in a clot tube occurs before centrifugation, not because of the gel itself.
  7. The correct answer is A (Reject the specimen outright and require a fresh collection): an unlabeled tube can never be safely reconciled with a patient after it has left the bedside, since there is no reliable way to confirm identity without risking a serious misidentification error. Guessing through elimination or relying on someone's memory introduces exactly the kind of preventable error that patient safety protocols exist to prevent. Testing an unlabeled specimen and simply flagging the report still risks the wrong result reaching the wrong chart. Canadian practice requires rejection and redraw rather than any attempt to relabel after the fact.
  8. The correct answer is B (Treat it as a labeling discrepancy and recollect a properly labeled tube): any mismatch between the label and the patient identifiers, even one detail like a misspelled surname, is treated as a labeling error that compromises confidence in the specimen's identity and calls for rejection and redraw. Relying on the health number alone to override a visible discrepancy is not an accepted shortcut in Canadian labeling protocol. Altering a label after it has left the bedside is specifically prohibited, since it undermines the whole purpose of bedside labeling. Spelling mismatches are treated seriously precisely because they can indicate the wrong patient was drawn.
  9. The correct answer is C (A documented, unbroken chain of custody with signatures at each transfer): a forensic or legal specimen requires a formal chain of custody where every person who takes possession of the sample signs and dates a form, creating an unbroken documented trail that can withstand legal scrutiny. Treating it identically to a routine specimen with no extra documentation would leave the sample's integrity open to challenge in court. Relying on memory rather than a written record fails to meet the evidentiary standard these specimens require. Legal specimens generally require more, not less, documentation compared to routine testing.
  10. The correct answer is D (The chain of custody is compromised and the issue must be documented): a broken tamper-evident seal with no accompanying explanation breaks the unbroken custody trail that legal specimens depend on, and this gap must be documented immediately so the specimen's evidentiary value can be properly assessed. A broken seal is never treated as routine or expected in forensic handling, unlike ordinary transport of a clinical specimen. Proceeding with testing as if nothing happened ignores a serious integrity concern that could affect legal admissibility. Simply notifying the courier without formal documentation does not meet the standard required for a legal specimen.
  11. The correct answer is A (Document the actual collection time and flag the timing discrepancy): a trough level is only clinically meaningful when drawn at the correct point in the dosing cycle, so documenting the actual collection time and flagging the discrepancy allows the ordering clinician to correctly interpret or repeat the result. Treating trough timing as flexible ignores the pharmacokinetic reason the test is timed in the first place. Simply relabeling it as a peak level would be inaccurate, since a peak has its own specific timing requirement that this draw does not meet either. Silently discarding the specimen without communication leaves the care team unaware that a timing error occurred.
  12. The correct answer is C (Document that the patient is non-fasting and notify appropriate staff): recording the true fasting status and communicating it to the ordering team or laboratory allows the result to be interpreted correctly, since triglycerides in particular can be significantly elevated after a recent meal. Proceeding without any documentation risks the clinician acting on a result that looks abnormal only because of recent food intake. Simply canceling the draw without communication leaves the underlying scheduling problem unresolved and unaddressed. Relabeling the order type on one's own authority oversteps the phlebotomist's scope, since the ordering clinician determines how the specimen should be processed given the new information.
  13. The correct answer is A (POCT uses a small capillary or whole blood sample tested immediately at the bedside): point-of-care testing analyzes a small whole blood or capillary specimen directly at the bedside without the centrifugation, transport, or plasma separation steps a central laboratory sample requires, which is why POCT delivers a rapid result but can be more susceptible to certain interferences. Central lab testing typically does involve centrifugation to obtain plasma or serum before analysis on larger automated analyzers. Claiming both methods are processed identically ignores this fundamental workflow difference. A POCT result does not always require lab confirmation first, since it is designed to be used clinically on its own in many situations.
  14. The correct answer is B (A delta check comparing the new result to the patient's own prior result): a delta check flags a result that differs substantially from a patient's own previous value, which can point toward a pre-analytical error such as hemolysis, sample mislabeling, or improper collection rather than a genuine clinical change. This is distinct from a reference interval, which compares the result against a general population range rather than the patient's own history. It is also distinct from a critical value alert, which flags a result outside a dangerous threshold regardless of prior history. A delta flag does not by itself indicate an analyzer malfunction, though it does prompt investigation of the specimen and collection process.
  15. The correct answer is D (Coagulation testing depends on a precise nine to one ratio to be valid): the sodium citrate in a light blue tube is formulated to bind calcium in a fixed ratio to a full blood draw, and any underfill shifts that ratio, causing falsely prolonged clotting times that do not reflect the patient's true coagulation status. Other additive tubes also require inversion, so citrate is not unique in that respect. Draw order sequence affects contamination risk between additives but does not change the ratio requirement within the citrate tube itself. Citrate concentration is directly tied to accurate clotting results, not unrelated to them.
  16. The correct answer is A (Reject the specimen as clotted and request a fresh recollection): visible clots in an anticoagulated tube mean the additive failed to prevent coagulation, which alters cell counts and can obstruct analyzer sample lines, so the specimen must be rejected and a new sample collected. Attempting to filter out clots does not restore the integrity of the remaining cellular distribution in the tube. Assuming the analyzer will simply detect and compensate for clots is unreliable and risks reporting an inaccurate result or damaging equipment. Vigorous inversion after a clot has already formed will not reverse it and instead adds hemolysis risk on top of the existing problem.
  17. The correct answer is D (Red cells contain a much higher potassium concentration than plasma): intracellular potassium concentration is far greater than plasma potassium, so when red cells rupture during hemolysis they release this excess potassium directly into the surrounding fluid, producing a falsely elevated result. Hemolysis does not destroy potassium, it redistributes it from inside the cells into the plasma or serum being tested. Potassium is in fact one of the analytes most dramatically affected by hemolysis compared to many other electrolytes. While prolonged unrefrigerated storage can also raise potassium through ongoing cellular metabolism, hemolysis alone is sufficient to cause this falsely elevated effect.
  18. The correct answer is C (LDH and AST, both of which are present at high levels inside red cells): lactate dehydrogenase and aspartate aminotransferase are both enzymes found at high intracellular concentration, so when red cells rupture during hemolysis these enzymes leak into the plasma or serum and produce falsely elevated results, similar to what happens with potassium. Sodium and chloride are present at relatively similar concentrations inside and outside cells, so they are far less dramatically affected by hemolysis. Total protein and albumin are not characteristically decreased by hemolysis in this way. Calcium and magnesium do have some intracellular presence but are not the classic textbook examples of hemolysis-sensitive analytes the way LDH and AST are.
  19. The correct answer is B (An increased risk of mechanical hemolysis from excessive shear force): a smaller-gauge needle creates a narrower lumen, and when paired with the strong vacuum draw of a large collection tube, blood is forced through the narrow opening under greater shear stress, which can rupture red cells and cause hemolysis. Needle gauge is a well-established mechanical contributor to hemolysis risk, so claiming no effect is inaccurate. Clot formation is governed by additive mixing and timing, not primarily by needle gauge. A vacuum tube's draw volume is determined by its internal vacuum calibration, not by the needle gauge used to fill it.
  20. The correct answer is D (It commonly causes hemolysis from the forceful transfer through the needle): forcing blood from a syringe through a narrow needle under manual pressure subjects the red cells to significant shear stress, which is a well-recognized mechanical cause of hemolysis. Correct tube filling does not guarantee specimen quality if the transfer technique itself damages the cells. Claiming this technique improves quality misrepresents its actual risk, since it is generally discouraged in favor of using a transfer device. This risk is not limited to coagulation tubes, since hemolysis affects the accuracy of many chemistry and hematology analytes as well.
  21. The correct answer is B (The tube must be allowed to fully clot for the appropriate time period): an SST tube relies on natural clot formation before centrifugation, and spinning it too early can leave residual fibrin strands or incomplete separation that interferes with accurate testing and analyzer function. Icing an SST tube is not the standard requirement for a routine metabolic panel and instead applies to specific unstable analytes. Twenty inversions is excessive for a clot tube and not the relevant preparatory step here, since SST tubes typically require only a modest number of inversions to activate the clot activator. Diluting a specimen with saline is never an appropriate step before routine testing, since it would alter analyte concentrations.
  22. The correct answer is A (Prolonged contact between cells and serum can alter certain analyte results): when cells remain in prolonged contact with serum before separation, ongoing cellular metabolism and diffusion can shift the concentration of certain analytes such as glucose or potassium away from the true in-vivo value. Simply centrifuging eventually does not undo whatever pre-analytical drift already occurred during the extended delay. This concern applies to clot tubes as well as anticoagulated tubes, since separation timing matters broadly across specimen types. Delayed processing generally degrades rather than improves the stability of time-sensitive analytes.
  23. The correct answer is C (Heparin contamination from the line can falsely prolong clotting results): drawing a coagulation specimen through or near a heparinized line before an adequate discard volume has been removed risks contaminating the citrate tube with residual heparin, which falsely prolongs clotting time results regardless of how correctly the citrate tube itself was filled. Filling volume alone does not protect against this contamination source. Discard tube sequencing is specifically important for line draws because it clears the line of flush solution and residual anticoagulant before the diagnostic specimen is collected. This concern is specific to line draws and does not apply to routine capillary collection.
  24. The correct answer is B (Specimens should be transported within their established stability window for the ordered test): each analyte has a defined stability window during which the specimen can sit before results become unreliable, and transport must be planned so the specimen reaches the testing laboratory within that window. Freezing every specimen regardless of test type would damage samples for tests that require refrigerated or room-temperature handling instead. Stability windows apply broadly across specimen types, not only to point-of-care testing. Transport time is directly relevant to result accuracy, since analytes can degrade, metabolize, or shift in concentration the longer a specimen sits before processing.
  25. The correct answer is D (The glucose value would likely appear falsely decreased over time): red and white cells continue consuming glucose through glycolysis while they remain in contact with the plasma, so an unseparated specimen left at room temperature for hours will show a progressively lower glucose value than what was truly present at collection. This is the opposite of a falsely elevated result, which is why the direction of the error matters clinically. Glucose is one of the most well-known analytes affected by delayed separation, not an analyte that is unaffected. Sodium is comparatively stable under these conditions, so this delay is far more relevant to glucose specifically.
  26. The correct answer is C (Central lab processing typically involves centrifugation and larger batch analyzers): central laboratory testing generally requires collecting an appropriate tube, allowing clotting or mixing as needed, centrifuging to separate serum or plasma, and running the sample on a larger automated analyzer, a multi-step workflow point-of-care testing bypasses. Point-of-care testing typically uses a much smaller capillary or whole blood sample rather than a larger volume. Point-of-care results are generally available faster, not slower, than central lab results, which is the main advantage of bedside testing. Point-of-care devices often use specialized cartridges or strips rather than the standard tube types used for central lab collection.
  27. The correct answer is A (The delay could allow lactate concentration to rise before testing occurs): despite the glycolysis-inhibiting additive in a fluoride oxalate tube, lactate is still metabolically active enough that inadequate cooling during a prolonged delay can allow the value to drift upward before centrifugation and testing occur. The fluoride additive helps slow glycolysis but does not fully eliminate the need for prompt cooling on a genuinely unstable analyte like lactate. Cold-chain handling remains an important safeguard rather than an unnecessary extra step for this analyte. Lactate is well documented as sensitive to both temperature and processing delay, contrary to the claim that it is unaffected.
  28. The correct answer is D (It should be transported on ice promptly to slow ongoing cellular metabolism): blood gas values such as oxygen and carbon dioxide tension continue to shift as cells keep metabolizing after collection, so icing the specimen and transporting it promptly slows this metabolic activity and helps preserve accurate results. Leaving the specimen at room temperature for an extended period would allow this metabolic drift to worsen rather than stabilize the sample. Blood gas specimens do require distinct handling from routine chemistry tubes given their sensitivity to time and temperature. Light exposure is not the primary handling concern for a blood gas specimen, unlike bilirubin or folate.
  29. The correct answer is A (Cryoglobulins can precipitate out of solution if the specimen cools below body temperature): cryoglobulins are abnormal proteins that can reversibly precipitate when a specimen cools, so the tube must be kept warm and transported promptly to avoid an inaccurate result caused by premature precipitation. A routine complete blood count tube has no such temperature-sensitive precipitation risk and does not require warm handling. Claiming cryoglobulins are unaffected by temperature directly contradicts the defining property of this analyte. Applying identical icing procedures to both tubes would actively harm the cryoglobulin specimen by inducing the very precipitation the warm handling is meant to prevent.
  30. The correct answer is B (Green-top heparin generally needs eight to ten inversions, more than citrate's three to four): heparin tubes typically call for eight to ten gentle inversions to distribute the anticoagulant fully, while citrate tubes generally require only three to four inversions given the smaller additive volume relative to the fill line. Treating both tube types as requiring identical handling overlooks these established differences between additive systems. Citrate does not require more inversions than heparin, since the manufacturer-recommended range is actually lower for citrate tubes. Both tubes are anticoagulated specifically to prevent clotting, so neither is expected to clot naturally the way a plain serum tube does.
  31. The correct answer is C (Mild or in-vitro hemolysis can sometimes elevate potassium without an obviously visible tinge): a small degree of cell rupture can release enough intracellular potassium to meaningfully raise the result without producing hemolysis severe enough to be visually obvious, so a technologist may still consider a delta check or clinical correlation before assuming true hyperkalemia. Assuming visible hemolysis is always present whenever this occurs overstates how sensitive the human eye is to subtle cell damage. Dismissing the possibility of a pre-analytical cause entirely ignores a well-documented source of error for this specific analyte. Potassium is, in fact, one of the analytes most susceptible to pre-analytical handling issues, contrary to the claim that it is never affected.
  32. The correct answer is A (Note the light-protection breach and consider whether recollection is warranted): bilirubin is well established as light-sensitive, and an extended exposure to light without protection can degrade the analyte, so the laboratory should document this handling breach and evaluate whether the result is reliable enough to report or whether a new light-protected specimen should be collected. Fluorescent lighting does contribute meaningfully to bilirubin photodegradation over time, so testing without concern would be inappropriate. Freezing a specimen after the fact does not reverse degradation that has already occurred from light exposure. Light-related degradation of bilirubin typically causes a falsely decreased result, not a falsely elevated one, since the compound itself is broken down.
  33. The correct answer is C (A clear bag provides no meaningful protection from ongoing light exposure): a transparent specimen bag does nothing to block ambient or overhead light from continuing to degrade a light-sensitive analyte like folate during transport, which is why foil wrapping or an opaque amber bag is the recommended handling instead. Pneumatic tube transport itself does not provide light protection and can even expose specimens to lighting along various points of the route. Folate remains light-sensitive throughout handling, not only at the moment of collection, so ongoing protection is still necessary. Clear bags are specifically the wrong choice for light-sensitive specimens, which require an opaque covering instead.
  34. The correct answer is D (Both reflect pre-analytical errors that occurred before or during handling): clotting in an anticoagulated tube and hemolysis in a chemistry tube are both classic examples of pre-analytical errors, meaning problems that arise during collection, mixing, or transport rather than during the actual laboratory testing process itself. Blaming tube manufacturing defects for both issues is generally inaccurate, since most cases trace back to collection or handling technique rather than the tube itself. Both rejection reasons are, in fact, closely tied to collection technique, including mixing adequacy and mechanical stress during the draw. Neither clotted nor severely hemolyzed specimens can be corrected with a mathematical adjustment, which is exactly why they must be rejected rather than reported with a caveat.
  35. The correct answer is B (Gentle inversion mixes the additive adequately while vigorous shaking risks hemolysis): the recommended technique of slow, gentle end-over-end inversion is sufficient to distribute additive throughout the blood without subjecting red cells to the mechanical stress that vigorous shaking creates, which is why gentle inversion is the standard rather than shaking. Treating the two techniques as equivalent ignores the real difference in shear force each one applies to the specimen. Vigorous shaking is not required to activate standard tube additives, since gentle inversion accomplishes the same mixing goal safely. Mixing technique is, in fact, directly linked to hemolysis risk, which is the core reason gentle inversion is taught over shaking.
  36. The correct answer is C (The lactic acid could appear falsely elevated, potentially overstating illness severity): ongoing cellular metabolism in an unrefrigerated specimen continues to generate lactate over time, so a prolonged room-temperature delay can produce a falsely elevated result that may overstate the severity of a patient's condition and lead to inappropriate clinical decisions. Claiming no effect from delay ignores the well-documented time and temperature sensitivity of this analyte. The delay described specifically concerns the chemistry specimen for lactic acid, not the white blood cell count, which is a separate parameter typically drawn in a different tube. Icing, not room temperature storage, is the correct method to stabilize lactic acid during transport.
  37. The correct answer is B (Incomplete clotting can leave residual fibrin strands that interfere with testing): centrifuging before the blood has fully clotted can leave fibrin strands or a soft clot that interferes with proper serum separation and may clog analyzer sample probes or produce inaccurate results. Early centrifugation does not improve separation quality, since it works against the natural clotting process the SST tube depends on. Clotting time is directly tied to how cleanly a specimen separates, contrary to the claim that it has no bearing. This is not simply a matter of the tube taking longer to clot afterward, since premature spinning can leave the separation permanently compromised.
  38. The correct answer is A (Citrate needs three to four gentle inversions while EDTA needs eight to ten): manufacturer guidance generally recommends three to four gentle inversions for citrate tubes and a higher range of eight to ten for EDTA tubes, reflecting differences in additive volume and how thoroughly each needs to be distributed. Applying the same inversion count to both ignores these established manufacturer differences between tube types. Draw order affects contamination risk between tubes, not whether mixing itself is required, so both tubes still need to be inverted after collection. Citrate tubes do require mixing as well, since skipping it risks micro-clot formation just as it would in an unmixed EDTA tube.
  39. The correct answer is D (It protects the light-sensitive bilirubin molecule from photodegradation): wrapping a bilirubin specimen in foil blocks ambient and artificial light from breaking down the bilirubin molecule during handling and transport, preserving an accurate result. Foil wrapping has no bearing on temperature stability, which would instead be addressed through icing for a cold-chain-sensitive analyte. It also does not prevent mechanical hemolysis, which is a function of handling technique like needle gauge and mixing force rather than light exposure. Foil wrapping has no effect on clotting time, which depends on the tube's own additive or lack thereof.
  40. The correct answer is B (Some coagulation factors are less directly altered by mild hemolysis than intracellular electrolytes are): while significant hemolysis can still interfere with coagulation testing through platelet activation and other effects, mild hemolysis may have a less dramatic direct impact on clotting factor measurement than it does on an intracellular electrolyte like potassium, which is released in large quantities directly from ruptured cells. This does not mean hemolysis affects every analyte identically, since the degree and mechanism of interference genuinely varies by test. Potassium is very much affected by hemolysis, and this remains one of the most well-documented pre-analytical concerns in laboratory testing. Coagulation testing can still be affected by poor specimen quality, so it is not accurate to say it is entirely immune to these concerns.
  41. The correct answer is D (A documented custody trail should still be maintained per the study protocol): research studies with strict integrity requirements often specify their own chain of custody documentation to ensure specimen traceability and data validity, even when no legal proceeding is involved. Chain of custody is not exclusive to criminal or forensic specimens, since any context requiring a verifiable, unbroken handling record can call for the same documentation practice. Treating this research specimen with only routine handling would fail to meet the study's own protocol requirements. Documentation matters throughout the process, not only at the point the specimen eventually arrives at the lab.
  42. The correct answer is C (The legal specimen requires a signed custody trail the routine specimen does not): a legal or forensic specimen requires a formal, signed chain of custody documenting every transfer of possession to preserve its evidentiary value, a requirement that does not apply to a routine clinical chemistry specimen collected for patient care. Treating both specimens identically overlooks this important distinction in required handling. The routine specimen does not require more documentation than the legal one, since chain of custody is specifically the heightened standard reserved for legal specimens. Some documentation, such as collection time and identification, is still required for routine specimens even though it falls short of a full custody trail.
  43. The correct answer is A (EDTA carryover into the SST tube can interfere with certain chemistry results): the recommended order of draw exists specifically to prevent additive carryover between tubes, and EDTA reaching a subsequent chemistry tube can chelate calcium and interfere with certain assays, producing inaccurate results. Claiming no effect at all ignores the well-established purpose of the order of draw sequence. Order of draw is important for multiple tube types beyond coagulation testing, including chemistry and other additive-sensitive tests. An SST tube has no built-in mechanism to protect itself against additive carryover from a preceding tube, which is exactly why draw sequence matters.
  44. The correct answer is D (Clots indicate additive failure and can obstruct analyzer sampling regardless of test type): visible clotting in an anticoagulated tube signals that the additive did not adequately prevent coagulation, and beyond the immediate hematology concerns, clots or fibrin strands can physically obstruct sample probes on chemistry analyzers as well, making rejection appropriate regardless of the specific test ordered. Clotting is not a concern limited to hematology testing alone, since it can compromise multiple analyzer types. A clotted specimen cannot simply be centrifuged and treated as reliable, since the underlying cellular distribution and analyte concentrations may already be altered. Rejection remains necessary once a clot is identified, regardless of how far along in processing the specimen has traveled.
  45. The correct answer is C (A patient's hemoglobin drops sharply from a prior value with no clinical explanation, prompting investigation): a delta check specifically compares a patient's new result to their own previous result, and an unexplained sharp drop without a corresponding clinical event is exactly the kind of discrepancy this check is designed to flag as potentially reflecting a specimen or handling error. A result simply falling within the general reference interval reflects a population-based comparison, not a delta check against the patient's own history. A critical value alert is triggered by an absolute threshold rather than a comparison to the patient's prior result. A quality control flag concerns instrument performance on a control material, not a patient-specific historical comparison.
  46. The correct answer is B (Document the patient's actual fasting or non-fasting status regardless of order type): recording accurate fasting status supports correct interpretation of the result and provides a clear record even for a test ordered as random, since this information can matter for downstream clinical decision-making or later comparison to a fasting value. Limiting this documentation practice only to lipid panels ignores its broader value across other glucose-related and metabolic testing. Even a random order benefits from an accurate fasting status note rather than omitting it entirely. Waiting for the patient to volunteer the information rather than actively confirming it risks missing a detail relevant to result interpretation.
  47. The correct answer is A (Proper capillary technique, such as avoiding excessive squeezing that dilutes the sample with tissue fluid): excessive squeezing or milking of a capillary puncture site can introduce interstitial and tissue fluid into the sample, diluting the blood and producing a falsely low hemoglobin result on a point-of-care device. Point-of-care testing does have specific technique considerations that affect result accuracy, contrary to the claim that none apply. Central laboratory testing is not the only method susceptible to technique-related errors, since capillary collection carries its own well-documented pitfalls. Capillary sampling technique is directly linked to point-of-care hemoglobin accuracy, which is why proper method matters at the bedside.
  48. The correct answer is C (Documented administration time may not match when the infusion truly ended): a peak level depends on capturing the specimen at a specific point relative to when drug concentration is expected to be highest, and relying on a documented administration time rather than confirming the actual infusion completion risks drawing the specimen too early or too late relative to the true peak. Peak levels are, in fact, highly sensitive to precise collection timing, which is the entire reason peak and trough testing exists as a distinct practice. Assuming documented times are always accurate ignores a real and recurring source of pre-analytical timing error. Timing precision matters for infused medications specifically because the peak concentration point depends on knowing exactly when the infusion ended.
  49. The correct answer is D (Direct sunlight can raise the specimen's temperature and accelerate analyte degradation): beyond photodegradation of specifically light-sensitive analytes, direct sunlight can meaningfully raise a specimen's temperature, which can accelerate the breakdown or metabolic shift of various temperature-sensitive analytes even on a panel without an explicitly light-sensitive test. Claiming sunlight has no bearing on temperature ignores basic physical properties of direct light exposure. The concern extends beyond bilirubin specifically, since temperature elevation from sunlight can affect a range of analytes on a general chemistry panel. Windowsill storage is never an acceptable substitute for controlled cold-chain handling, since ambient sunlight conditions are neither monitored nor consistent.
  50. The correct answer is B (Post-draw handling errors like hemolysis or delayed processing can invalidate an otherwise well-collected specimen): even a technically flawless venipuncture can still yield an unreliable result if subsequent handling steps such as mixing, timing, temperature control, or light protection are not followed correctly, which is why post-draw handling is treated as an equally critical part of the overall process. Claiming post-draw handling has little influence contradicts the well-documented sources of pre-analytical error covered throughout specimen processing. Venipuncture technique alone does not guarantee accuracy if the specimen is subsequently hemolyzed, delayed, or improperly stored. Handling errors are not always automatically caught, since some, like subtle hemolysis or metabolic drift, can go undetected without specific attention such as a delta check or visual inspection.
  51. The correct answer is C (The red gold topped serum separator tube, the third tube shown in the chart): this tube contains a clot activator that speeds coagulation and a separator gel that forms a physical barrier between serum and cellular components during centrifugation. Chemistry, serology, and immunology testing rely on this clean serum layer for accurate results. The gel barrier is purely physical and does not chelate calcium the way an anticoagulant tube does. Most routine outpatient chemistry panels in Canadian labs use this tube as the default draw.
  52. The correct answer is A (The yellow topped blood culture bottle, the first tube in the sequence shown): blood culture bottles are drawn before any other tube to minimize contamination risk from skin flora or additive carryover. The broth inside supports aerobic and anaerobic organism growth for culture and sensitivity testing. Drawing this bottle first also protects the sample from any anticoagulant residue that could suppress bacterial growth. Canadian sepsis protocols emphasize strict order of draw for this exact reason.
  53. The correct answer is B (The blood to citrate ratio becomes skewed, leaving relatively excess anticoagulant in the sample): the sodium citrate tube depends on a precise 9 to 1 blood to citrate ratio to bind just enough calcium for accurate clotting time results. An underfilled tube leaves proportionally too much citrate relative to plasma, artificially prolonging PT and PTT results. This is why Canadian labs reject citrate tubes filled below the manufacturer's minimum line. No other tube in the chart depends this heavily on an exact fill ratio.
  54. The correct answer is D (The grey topped fluoride oxalate tube, the sixth tube in the sequence shown): sodium fluoride inhibits glycolysis, stopping red blood cells and any bacteria present from continuing to metabolize glucose after the draw. Potassium oxalate serves as the actual anticoagulant, since fluoride alone does not prevent clotting. This combination makes the tube the preferred choice for glucose and lactate testing when transport delays are expected. Without fluoride, a delayed sample would show a falsely low glucose result.
  55. The correct answer is A (The green topped heparin tube, the fourth tube in the sequence shown): heparin works by enhancing the activity of antithrombin III, which then inactivates thrombin and other clotting factors without removing calcium from the plasma. This mechanism differs fundamentally from citrate and EDTA, which prevent clotting by chelating calcium directly. Because heparinized plasma can be obtained quickly after a short centrifugation, it is well suited for stat chemistry requests. The distinction matters because a heparin tube cannot substitute for an EDTA tube when cell morphology needs to be preserved.
  56. The correct answer is C (The purple topped EDTA tube, the fifth tube in the sequence shown): EDTA chelates calcium irreversibly, which prevents clotting while best preserving the size, shape, and staining characteristics of blood cells for a CBC or manual differential. Heparin can distort white cell morphology on a stained smear, making it a poor substitute for hematology work. The irreversible nature of EDTA's calcium binding is also why the tube cannot be used for coagulation testing. Canadian hematology labs consistently specify EDTA as the required additive for complete blood counts.
  57. The correct answer is B (Sodium fluoride carryover can inhibit enzyme activity and glycolysis in the EDTA sample): the standard order of draw exists to prevent additive carryover between tubes, since even trace amounts of fluoride transferred on the needle or holder can inhibit enzymatic reactions and glycolysis in a subsequently drawn tube. This can distort results ranging from certain enzyme panels to glucose values in downstream tubes. Following the sequence shown, from blood culture through citrate, serum, heparin, EDTA, and finally fluoride, minimizes this cross contamination risk. Reordering tubes out of sequence is a recognized preanalytical error in Canadian phlebotomy practice.
  58. The correct answer is C (The light blue topped sodium citrate tube, the second tube in the sequence shown): sodium citrate reversibly binds calcium, a required cofactor in the coagulation cascade, which halts clotting long enough for PT, INR, and PTT testing to be performed accurately. Because the test depends on the coagulation pathway itself, only an anticoagulant that targets calcium without permanently altering the sample is appropriate. Serum tubes allow clotting to occur, which makes them unsuitable for any coagulation study. Canadian coagulation laboratories require the full 9 to 1 blood to citrate ratio for a valid result.
  59. This is a preanalytical safeguard specific to microbiology testing rather than a chemistry or hematology concern. The order of draw sequence shown exists precisely to protect specimen integrity across every tube type collected in one venipuncture. Potassium oxalate provides the anticoagulant action so the specimen remains liquid for analysis. Together these additives make the tube the standard choice whenever glucose or lactate stability over time is clinically important.   61. The red gold serum separator tube allows the sample to clot fully, so the recovered fluid is serum with clotting factors already consumed. This distinction matters because some chemistry assays are validated only for serum or only for plasma, not both interchangeably.   62. Because EDTA chelates calcium so completely, even a small carryover into a serum tube could interfere with chemistry results. Placing it fifth, ahead of only the fluoride tube, reflects its relatively high contamination risk to downstream draws. Memorizing this exact position is a common competency checkpoint in Canadian phlebotomy training programs.
  63. The correct answer is A (The gel forms only a physical barrier between serum and cells after centrifugation, with no calcium binding): the separator gel is inert and has a density between serum and cellular components, so it simply migrates to that interface during spinning to physically keep the two layers apart. Clotting in this tube happens through the clot activator coating before centrifugation, not through any calcium chelation mechanism. Sodium citrate, by contrast, actively binds calcium to prevent clotting altogether, which is a completely different chemical process. Confusing these two mechanisms is a common exam trap for new Canadian phlebotomy students.

Using the fluoride oxalate tube in place of a serum separator tube would suppress enzyme activity and produce falsely low or unreliable results. This is a recognized specimen rejection criterion in Canadian laboratories when the wrong tube colour reaches the enzyme bench. Selecting the correct tube from the sequence shown is therefore a critical preanalytical step.
  65. An underfilled citrate tube leaves relatively too much citrate in proportion to plasma, which prolongs coagulation results and is a common cause of specimen rejection. Other additive tubes in the chart use dry or spray-dried additives that scale more consistently with smaller fill volumes. Recognizing which tubes share a mechanism helps explain why citrate and EDTA cannot be substituted for each other despite both being anticoagulants. This is a preanalytical safeguard rather than an inventory or volume consideration. Canadian phlebotomy competency standards require strict adherence to this sequence for exactly this reason.
  71. Using a serum tube for coagulation testing would give no meaningful result, since clotting has already occurred and consumed the factors being measured.   72. Choosing the correct additive for hematology work is essential for both automated counts and manual smear review.
  73. The correct answer is C (It inhibits complement, phagocytes, and certain antibiotics that could otherwise suppress organism growth): SPS neutralizes several of the body's natural antimicrobial defenses present in blood, including complement proteins and phagocytic white cells, while also inactivating some antibiotic classes that might already be circulating in the patient. This allows any organisms present in the bloodstream to survive and multiply in the culture broth long enough to be detected. Its anticoagulant effect is a secondary benefit, but the primary reason it is used in blood culture bottles is this protective effect on organism viability.   74. This difference can affect total protein measurements and certain assays that are only validated for one specimen type.
