  75. The correct answer is D (The tube was underfilled, leaving excess citrate relative to the plasma volume collected): because sodium citrate depends on a fixed 9 to 1 blood to citrate ratio, an underfilled tube leaves proportionally more citrate binding available calcium than intended, which artificially prolongs clotting time results once the sample is tested. This is one of the most common preanalytical causes of a falsely prolonged PT or INR and is a leading reason coagulation specimens are rejected in Canadian laboratories. Draw order after EDTA could theoretically introduce trace calcium chelator contamination, but underfill is the far more common and directly explanatory cause here. Reviewing the fill line on the tube is a standard first step when investigating an unexpected coagulation result.
  76. The correct answer is C (Clotting releases potassium from platelets and cells, slightly raising the serum concentration measured): during the clotting process in a serum tube, platelets and other cells release intracellular potassium into the surrounding fluid, producing a serum result that is typically slightly higher than a plasma result from the same blood. Heparin plasma avoids this release because clotting never occurs, leaving intracellular potassium largely inside the cells until centrifugation separates them. This is a well recognized physiological difference rather than a laboratory error, and clinicians interpreting borderline potassium results should note which tube type was used. Canadian reference ranges for potassium are typically established separately for serum and plasma for this reason.
  77. This can prolong coagulation results in the same way an underfilled tube does, even though the tube technically reached its labeled fill line. Some Canadian laboratories require an adjusted citrate volume tube for patients with significantly elevated hematocrit for exactly this reason. Recognizing this edge case prevents a technologist from misattributing a prolonged result to a true bleeding disorder.
  78. Specialized trace element tubes are manufactured under strict quality control to minimize this contamination risk, regardless of whether the specific tube uses a plain, EDTA, or heparin additive. Choosing the wrong tube for this testing can produce clinically misleading results that suggest toxic exposure when none exists.   79. Sodium citrate used for routine coagulation testing is a different formulation optimized for calcium binding precision rather than long term cell preservation. Neither sodium fluoride nor lithium heparin is the standard additive chosen for this type of molecular and cellular testing in Canadian transfusion medicine and forensic laboratories.   80. Sodium citrate binds calcium reversibly, allowing the laboratory to add calcium back for controlled coagulation testing, while EDTA's chelation is effectively irreversible at the concentrations used, making it unsuitable for any test requiring calcium. Understanding this spectrum explains why each tube is matched to a specific category of testing rather than being interchangeable.   81. This is unrelated to the heparin additive itself, since heparin's mechanism involves antithrombin potentiation and does not interact with potassium.   82. Heparin tubes do not contain a clot activator or separator gel by definition, and they use a completely different mechanism than citrate tubes, so those checks belong to other tube types. Only specimens filled below this validated minimum line should be rejected, since they would leave a disproportionate amount of citrate relative to plasma. EDTA's chelation is so strong and effectively irreversible that adding calcium back would not restore the coagulation cascade to a testable state. Both additives do bind calcium, so the difference lies in reversibility rather than whether binding occurs at all. This distinction is a foundational concept tested on Canadian phlebotomy certification examinations regarding coagulation specimen requirements.
  91. Both citrate and EDTA specifically alter calcium availability as their core mechanism, which would compromise any assay dependent on native calcium levels. Selecting an additive based on its specific mechanism, not just its general anticoagulant category, is an important skill for Canadian laboratory professionals.
  92. The correct answer is A (Inadequate mixing after collection prevented the EDTA from evenly contacting the entire blood sample): EDTA anticoagulant must be thoroughly mixed with the entire blood volume immediately after collection, typically through gentle inversion, or localized clotting can occur in areas of the tube where the additive never made proper contact with the sample. EDTA does not degrade meaningfully within the short timeframe relevant to routine collection and processing.   93.   94. The correct answer is C (Lithium heparin, since it potentiates antithrombin III to continuously inactivate thrombin and other clotting factors): heparin's mechanism directly and continuously inactivates the very clotting factors a researcher is trying to measure, making heparinized plasma unsuitable for a study of native clotting factor activity. Sodium citrate is actually the industry-standard additive for coagulation and clotting factor testing precisely because its calcium chelation is reversible with recalcification, allowing the cascade to be studied under controlled conditions rather than permanently blocked. Sodium fluoride's role is glycolysis inhibition rather than any direct effect on clotting factors, and potassium oxalate is a calcium-chelating anticoagulant, not a factor-inactivating one. Selecting an additive whose mechanism does not permanently disable the pathway under study is a key consideration in specialized Canadian research laboratory settings.
  95. It also does not, on its own, indicate an underlying clotting factor deficiency and would not be interpreted that way without further clinical correlation. Recognizing and reporting fibrin strands before running an automated analyzer helps prevent instrument errors in Canadian laboratories.
  96. The correct answer is C (The blue tube contains a substance that temporarily pauses clotting so the lab can measure how blood clots): sodium citrate reversibly binds calcium, pausing the natural clotting process so the laboratory can later add calcium back under controlled conditions and time exactly how long the plasma takes to clot, which is the basis of tests like PT and INR. This is a simplified but accurate way to explain the mechanism to a patient without using technical terminology they may not understand. The blue tube's function has nothing to do with tube size, shipping preservation, or arbitrary colour matching to a requisition form.   97. Sodium fluoride, SPS, and clot activator contamination would not directly explain a calcium specific pattern, since none of those additives primarily target calcium. Investigating a shared tube lot as the common factor is a standard root cause analysis step when an unusual result pattern spans multiple unrelated patients. Canadian laboratories are required to report suspected tube manufacturing defects to the supplier and relevant regulatory bodies when this kind of pattern is confirmed.
  98. The correct answer is D (The CBC requires whole blood for cell counting, while the metabolic panel needs separated serum or plasma): automated hematology analyzers count and characterize intact whole blood cells directly from the EDTA tube, so centrifuging away the plasma would remove the very cells being measured. Chemistry analyzers, by contrast, measure dissolved analytes in serum or plasma, which requires centrifugation to separate the liquid portion from cells before testing can proceed. The clot activator in a serum tube works before centrifugation, not because of it, and EDTA is not chemically destabilized by standard centrifugation speeds. Understanding why some tubes are spun and others are not reflects a basic but important distinction in Canadian laboratory workflow.
  99. The correct answer is B (Manufacturer guidelines differ by additive and tube design to ensure adequate but not excessive mixing for each): recommended inversion counts are established by tube manufacturers based on validation studies specific to each additive and tube design, balancing the need for thorough additive distribution against the risk of hemolysis or excessive foaming from over-mixing. While citrate is a liquid additive and EDTA is often a spray-dried coating, the exact inversion count is a validated manufacturer specification rather than a simple volume comparison. Sodium citrate does not chemically degrade from standard gentle inversion mixing. Following the specific inversion guidance printed on each tube type, rather than applying one universal rule, is standard practice in Canadian phlebotomy procedure.
  100. The correct answer is C (Different additives work through distinct mechanisms that can each interfere with specific analytes if mismatched): whether an additive chelates calcium, potentiates antithrombin, inhibits glycolysis, or supports microbial growth, each mechanism can directly interfere with certain analytes or test methods if the wrong tube is used, even when the general specimen category of serum or plasma appears correct. This is why laboratories specify exact tube types on requisitions rather than allowing any serum tube or any plasma tube to be substituted interchangeably. Centrifugation speed and tube cost are minor practical considerations compared to the analytical accuracy risk posed by additive mismatch. Demonstrating this level of mechanistic understanding, rather than simple colour memorization, is the goal of comprehensive Canadian phlebotomy certification preparation.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (SPS inhibits complement, phagocytes, and some antibiotics so organisms survive and multiply): sodium polyanetholesulfonate is added to blood culture bottles specifically to neutralize the body's natural bactericidal defences and residual antibiotic activity. This lets any pathogens present in the bloodstream continue growing in the incubated broth rather than being destroyed before detection. Unlike coagulation-testing anticoagulants, SPS is not chosen for its effect on calcium or fibrin formation. This mechanism is unique among tube additives and is tested heavily on the CPTG exam.
  2. The correct answer is D (Reject the tube and recollect since underfilling distorts the citrate-to-blood ratio): sodium citrate tubes rely on a precise 9:1 ratio of blood to anticoagulant, and a short draw leaves a relatively excessive citrate concentration. That excess citrate binds more calcium than intended and falsely prolongs PT and PTT results. Diluting the tube with saline or extending centrifugation cannot correct a ratio problem that occurred at collection. A fresh tube filled to the marked line is the only acceptable fix.
  3. The correct answer is A (The clot activator and gel separation take longer than a heparin plasma tube allows): serum tubes require full clotting time, typically 30 minutes, before centrifugation, which delays turnaround for a true emergency. A green heparin tube produces plasma almost immediately after a short spin because no clotting step is needed. SST tubes are used constantly for routine chemistry, so restriction to serology is incorrect, and the gel is chemically inert rather than reactive with sodium. Time pressure, not chemistry, is the deciding factor here.
  4. The correct answer is C (Heparin potentiates antithrombin III, which then inactivates thrombin and other clotting factors): heparin works by dramatically enhancing the natural inhibitory action of antithrombin III against thrombin and several activated clotting factors. Because calcium itself is never bound or removed, the specimen still contains free ionized calcium after collection. This is the key distinction from citrate and EDTA, which prevent clotting purely by chelating calcium. This mechanism difference is a favourite exam trap between green and lavender or blue tubes.
  5. The correct answer is B (EDTA irreversibly chelates calcium and best preserves cell size and morphology for counting): ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid binds calcium so tightly that the chelation is essentially permanent, which prevents clotting while keeping formed elements intact for microscopic and automated evaluation. This preservation of cell shape and size is exactly why EDTA is the preferred additive for CBCs rather than heparin or citrate. Mild crenation after prolonged storage is a known limitation, not evidence the additive is unsuitable. EDTA is not chosen for optical compatibility or platelet activation, both of which would harm the count.
  6. The correct answer is A (Sodium fluoride inhibits glycolysis so red cells and bacteria cannot continue consuming glucose): sodium fluoride acts as an enzyme inhibitor that halts ongoing glucose metabolism inside the collected cells, while potassium oxalate or EDTA in the same tube provides the actual anticoagulant action. Without fluoride, glycolysis continues after collection and glucose falsely decreases the longer the sample sits before separation. This makes the grey tube the correct choice whenever transport or processing will be delayed. Fluoride does not oxidize glucose or add glucose to the specimen.
  7. The correct answer is D (The royal blue tube is manufactured with minimal trace metal contamination for accuracy): trace element tubes undergo a manufacturing process controlling for metal residue on the rubber stopper, interior tube wall, and additive itself, since even minute contamination would skew a lead result. A standard lavender tube is not manufactured to this stricter standard and could introduce falsely elevated trace metal readings. The additive inside a royal blue tube can vary between plain, EDTA, or heparin depending on the exact test ordered. Heat treatment and metal-repelling plastics are not the actual mechanism involved.
  8. The correct answer is C (ACD preserves white cell viability better than EDTA, which is important for cellular assays): acid citrate dextrose maintains lymphocyte and leukocyte viability over time better than EDTA does, which matters greatly for functional cellular tests such as HLA typing, paternity DNA testing, and certain blood bank compatibility studies. ACD is an anticoagulant, not a clot activator, so it does not improve any clot-based yield. Its mechanism is calcium chelation similar to other citrate compounds, not a pH-based effect on antigen exposure. This viability advantage is the specific reason it is chosen over EDTA for this category of testing.
  9. The correct answer is A (Premature centrifugation trapped incomplete clot formation, allowing fibrin to pass into the serum layer): SST tubes need the full recommended clotting interval, typically about 30 minutes, before spinning so that clot activator has time to complete the cascade fully. Spinning too early separates the specimen while fibrin formation is still underway, and residual fibrin strands then contaminate the serum aliquot and can clog automated analyzer probes. This is a procedural timing error rather than an expired additive or an incorrect centrifuge setting. Waiting the full clotting time before centrifugation prevents this outcome.
  10. The correct answer is B (Citrate binds calcium reversibly while EDTA binds calcium irreversibly for the specimen's duration): sodium citrate's calcium binding can be reversed by adding calcium back in a coagulation analyzer, which is essential since PT and PTT testing must recalcify the plasma to trigger clotting reactions for timing. EDTA's chelation is essentially permanent, which is precisely why it is unsuitable for coagulation testing but ideal for preserving cell counts. Neither additive destroys calcium or acts on thrombin or fibrinogen directly. This reversibility distinction is central to understanding why each tube serves a different test category.
  11. The correct answer is D (EDTA carryover into the citrate tube can falsely elevate the reported PT and PTT results): trace EDTA transferred on the needle or holder into a subsequently drawn citrate tube would chelate additional calcium beyond what citrate alone accounts for, prolonging clotting times inaccurately. Following the standardized order of draw prevents this cross-contamination between additives. Neither tube degrades meaningfully faster at room temperature in the short interval between draws, and vacuum strength is unrelated to draw sequence. This is a well-established CLSI order-of-draw principle tested regularly on Canadian phlebotomy exams.
  12. The correct answer is C (When the assay requires serum entirely free of any gel interference or additive contamination): certain specialized tests, including some drug levels, protein electrophoresis fractions, and specific serology assays, are validated against pure serum and can be affected by trace substances leaching from separator gel or clot activator. A plain red tube avoids that possibility entirely. Coagulation testing requires plasma from a citrate tube, not serum, ruling that option out. A stat electrolyte need favours a heparin plasma tube for speed, not a plain red tube, which still requires full clotting time.
  13. The correct answer is A (A high hematocrit reduces the plasma volume relative to a fixed citrate amount, over-anticoagulating the sample): the standard 9:1 blood-to-citrate ratio assumes a typical plasma fraction, so when red cell mass is unusually high the actual plasma volume drops while the citrate amount in the tube stays fixed. This creates a relative excess of citrate against the smaller plasma volume, chelating proportionally more calcium and falsely prolonging clotting times. Special adjusted-citrate tubes exist for these patients specifically to correct this imbalance. No chemical precipitate forms between citrate and red cells.
  14. The correct answer is D (Sodium heparin would falsely elevate the sodium result being measured on the same panel): because the additive itself contributes ions to the sample, sodium heparin would introduce extra sodium directly into the specimen and skew a sodium level upward. Lithium heparin avoids this specific interference since lithium is rarely the electrolyte being measured on a routine chemistry panel. Heparin does not bind calcium at all regardless of its salt form, and neither form inactivates antithrombin III, since heparin instead enhances antithrombin III activity. This ion-selection logic is a common exam scenario question.
  15. The correct answer is B (The gel is a physical barrier formed during centrifugation, not a chemical anticoagulant): the thixotropic gel in an SST tube has a density between cells and serum, so during centrifugation it migrates to sit as a physical wall separating the two layers after clotting has already fully occurred. It plays no role in preventing or promoting clot formation itself, since a clot activator handles that separately before spinning. This distinguishes SST gel fundamentally from citrate or EDTA, which act chemically on calcium before any centrifugation step. Confusing these two mechanisms is a frequent scoring trap on the CPTG exam.
  16. The correct answer is C (Heparin prevents clotting but does not stop ongoing cellular metabolism that raises ammonia levels): heparin's role is purely to prevent coagulation by potentiating antithrombin III, and it has no glycolysis-inhibiting or metabolism-halting property comparable to sodium fluoride. Because red and white cells remain metabolically active in a heparin tube, ammonia can continue rising after collection unless the specimen is kept cold to slow cellular activity. This is why ammonia specimens specifically require ice transport regardless of the anticoagulant chosen. Heparin does not denature at room temperature or generate ammonia itself.
  17. The correct answer is A (Fluoride alone does not anticoagulate the sample, so oxalate is needed to prevent clot formation): sodium fluoride's job in a grey-top tube is purely enzymatic inhibition of glycolysis, and it has essentially no meaningful anticoagulant property on its own. Potassium oxalate is included specifically to chelate calcium and keep the specimen as plasma, working alongside fluoride's separate metabolic role. This pairing lets the lab measure both glucose and lactate accurately from a single plasma specimen without ongoing consumption of the analyte. Oxalate does not affect pH or promote clotting.
  18. The correct answer is B (The transfer delay allowed clotting to begin in the syringe before citrate could act): citrate only prevents clotting once it is thoroughly mixed with the blood inside the collection tube, so any delay in transferring blood from a syringe into the tube gives the coagulation cascade time to begin before the additive can act. Prompt, gentle mixing immediately after collection is essential to prevent this from happening. Systemic heparin therapy in the patient does not overwhelm the tube's citrate, since the two anticoagulants act on entirely separate mechanisms. An expired tube is possible but far less likely than a procedural transfer delay as the direct cause here.
  19. The correct answer is D (Coagulation testing needs calcium re-addable through citrate, while a CBC needs preserved cell shape via EDTA): sodium citrate's reversible calcium binding allows the analyzer to recalcify the plasma and precisely time how quickly clotting reactions occur, which is the entire basis of PT and PTT testing. EDTA's irreversible calcium chelation instead protects red cell, white cell, and platelet morphology for accurate counting and sizing on a CBC. Both tubes contain anticoagulants, so the claim that light blue is additive-free is false, and there is no such provincial legal restriction on lavender tubes. This distinction reflects each additive's intended downstream use.
  20. The correct answer is C (Gold tube serum glucose would decline over time from ongoing glycolysis unless fluoride is present): without a glycolysis inhibitor, glucose in a gold-top specimen continues to be metabolized by red cells and any bacteria present, producing a falsely low result the longer the sample waits before separation. The grey tube's fluoride specifically prevents this decline, which is why glucose is often collected separately even though lipid panels run fine from serum. There is no requirement for a separate venipuncture site by law, and fluoride contamination of a lipid panel is not the actual reasoning behind this practice.
  21. The correct answer is B (Heparin plasma normally reads lower potassium than serum because it lacks platelet-release potassium): during clot formation in a serum tube, platelets release intracellular potassium into the surrounding fluid, which is why serum potassium characteristically runs slightly higher than a plasma value from the same patient. A heparin tube avoids this platelet-release effect entirely since no clot ever forms. Underfilling would affect anticoagulant sufficiency rather than lowering an analyte value, and standard heparin tubes contain no separator gel. EDTA contamination would more typically be associated with a falsely lower rather than higher potassium given EDTA's own effect on the assay.
  22. The correct answer is A (Reject the underfilled tubes since the citrate ratio cannot be corrected after collection): once blood and citrate have already combined in the wrong proportion, there is no reliable way to restore the intended 9:1 ratio after the fact, so the only accepted correction is recollection. Adding saline dilutes the specimen further rather than fixing the citrate imbalance and is never an accepted workaround. Reporting a flagged estimate would risk clinically dangerous errors in anticoagulation dosing decisions. This strict rejection criterion protects the integrity of PT and PTT results used for warfarin monitoring.
  23. The correct answer is C (Different bottles support different atmospheric conditions needed by different classes of organisms): aerobic and anaerobic bottles are formulated with different internal gas environments and culture media so that organisms requiring oxygen and those that cannot tolerate it both have a chance to grow and be identified. SPS itself performs the identical role in both bottle types, inhibiting complement, phagocytes, and residual antibiotics regardless of the atmosphere. SPS functions in both oxygen-present and oxygen-free conditions equally well, and anaerobic bottles do not substitute a different additive. Drawing both bottle types increases the chance of detecting whatever organism is actually present.
  24. The correct answer is D (Inversions distribute the additive evenly through the blood so anticoagulation occurs uniformly): gentle inversion mixes the dry or liquid additive coating the tube wall throughout the entire blood volume, which prevents localized clotting or microclot formation in areas where the additive has not yet made contact. Too few inversions risk incomplete anticoagulation, while too vigorous mixing risks hemolysis, so the correct count matters for both additive types even though their chemical mechanisms differ. The additives are already chemically active on contact with blood rather than requiring agitation to activate. Clotting time is influenced by the additive's presence, not directly by the inversion count alone.
  25. The correct answer is A (The clot activator may be less effective, delaying complete clot formation before centrifugation): additives, including clot activators, can degrade in effectiveness over time even when tubes are stored correctly, and using an expired tube risks incomplete or slower clotting that can lead to fibrin strands contaminating the serum after centrifugation. The gel itself is generally more stable than the clot activator but is still subject to the tube's overall shelf-life rating. Vacuum draw strength is a separate quality parameter not directly tied to additive expiration. Checking expiration dates before every draw is a basic but essential quality practice.
  26. The correct answer is B (A serum-based trace element test needs a clot activator or no additive, not an anticoagulant version meant for plasma): the specific test ordered determines whether serum or plasma is required, and royal blue tubes are manufactured in plain, EDTA, and heparin versions precisely to match that need while still maintaining low trace metal contamination. Using an anticoagulated version when serum is required would produce plasma instead, which is the wrong specimen type for that particular validated assay. Cost and patient age are not the deciding factors in selecting the correct royal blue tube variant. Matching additive to specimen type remains essential even within this specialized trace-element tube category.
  27. The correct answer is C (Systemic heparin in the patient's own blood will still prolong clotting times regardless of tube additive): the tube additive only controls what happens to the specimen after collection, but a patient's own circulating heparin from IV or infusion therapy is already present in the blood and will continue to prolong coagulation reactions when the plasma is tested. This is a patient physiology issue, not a tube malfunction, and citrate still binds calcium normally in this situation. Standard citrate concentration is not altered for heparinized patients, and vacuum draw performance is unrelated to the patient's medication. Clinicians interpreting these results must account for the patient's own anticoagulant therapy separately from the tube's additive.
  28. The correct answer is D (PST tubes combine a heparin anticoagulant with a separator gel, producing plasma rather than serum): a light green plasma separator tube pairs lithium heparin's anticoagulant action with the same type of gel barrier found in a gold SST tube, allowing rapid centrifugation without waiting for clotting since no clot needs to form. This combination makes PST tubes especially useful for stat chemistry testing where speed matters. Gold tubes instead rely on a clot activator and require the full clotting interval before spinning. The presence of gel is common to both tube types, so the difference lies in the additive underneath the gel, not the presence of gel itself.
  29. The correct answer is A (Inadequate mixing left fluoride unevenly distributed, allowing localized ongoing glycolysis in parts of the tube): even when a specimen is centrifuged promptly, if the fluoride additive was never mixed thoroughly throughout the blood at collection, pockets of blood without adequate fluoride exposure can continue glycolysis before separation occurs. Fluoride does not require refrigeration to function and works effectively at room temperature once properly mixed. It does not react with headspace oxygen to consume glucose as a chemical byproduct. Prolonged tourniquet time can affect several analytes but is not the mechanism specifically tied to fluoride's role in this tube type.
  30. The correct answer is C (SPS's primary function is inhibiting host defence factors, not simply preventing coagulation like EDTA): while SPS does provide some anticoagulant activity, its defining and clinically important role is neutralizing complement, phagocytic cells, and certain antibiotics so that any bacteria or fungi present can be recovered and identified through incubation. EDTA's defining role is instead preserving cellular morphology for counting through irreversible calcium chelation. The two additives are chemically distinct compounds with different structures and purposes, not interchangeable formulations. Recognizing SPS's unique host-defence-inhibiting property is essential for understanding why blood cultures use a dedicated additive rather than a standard anticoagulant tube.
  31. The correct answer is B (ACD better preserves cellular integrity for downstream DNA extraction procedures over time): acid citrate dextrose supports nucleated cell viability over a longer holding period than EDTA, which is valuable when specimens may sit before DNA extraction is performed, such as with paternity or HLA testing sent to specialized labs. ACD is an anticoagulant rather than a clot activator, so it does not concentrate anything into serum, and it does not produce serum at all since it prevents clotting. There is no legal prohibition on EDTA for these tests in Canada, and plasma clarity is not the deciding clinical factor. Cell viability preservation is the genuine reason ACD is often specified for these applications.
  32. The correct answer is D (PST avoids the mandatory clotting wait time, producing a result faster in a time-sensitive situation): because heparin prevents clotting rather than promoting it, a PST tube can be centrifuged almost immediately after collection, while an SST tube must sit through a full clotting interval before spinning is appropriate. For genuinely stat orders, this time savings can meaningfully affect patient care, which is why PST is often the preferred choice despite SST being usable for many of the same tests under routine conditions. SST tubes are not legally restricted from early centrifugation, they are simply clinically inappropriate to spin too soon. Tube volume and analyzer compatibility are not the actual reasons behind this preference.
  33. The correct answer is C (EDTA's irreversible calcium chelation prevents proper recalcification, invalidating the coagulation result): coagulation testing depends on being able to add calcium back to trigger and time the clotting cascade, which is only possible with citrate's reversible binding. Because EDTA holds calcium so tightly that it cannot be released back into solution, any coagulation result obtained from EDTA plasma is invalid and must be rejected rather than reported. No analyzer can automatically compensate for using the wrong additive tube. Recognizing this incompatibility is essential for preventing a serious specimen-labelling and safety error in the lab.
  34. The correct answer is A (Reject the specimen and recollect since any visible clot invalidates the coagulation testing): a properly mixed citrate specimen should never form a visible clot, so its presence indicates a collection or mixing problem, such as a delayed transfer or inadequate inversion, that compromised the additive's ability to bind calcium throughout the sample. Any clotting factors already consumed in forming that clot are permanently unavailable, making the resulting PT or PTT unreliable regardless of how the clot is physically handled afterward. Filtering, adjusting centrifuge speed, or reporting with a caveat do not correct the underlying loss of clotting factors. A fresh, correctly collected specimen is the only acceptable resolution.
  35. The correct answer is D (EDTA plasma contains fibrinogen and lacks the exact protein profile validated for serum-based electrophoresis): because EDTA prevents clotting entirely, the resulting plasma retains fibrinogen and other clotting proteins that would normally be consumed during clot formation in a serum specimen, altering the banding pattern the test is validated against. This is a specimen-type compatibility issue rather than a volume, stopper, or protein-denaturing problem. Standard lavender tube sizes provide ample volume for many tests, so size is not the limiting factor here. Using the wrong specimen type for a validated assay can produce a misleading or uninterpretable result.
  36. The correct answer is B (The gel must reach full migration to form a stable, complete barrier between plasma and cells): the gel's density is engineered to settle into position only under adequate centrifugal force applied for a sufficient duration, and incomplete migration leaves gaps that allow cellular components to re-contaminate the plasma layer over time or during transport. Heparin plasma tubes do not involve clotting at all, so there is no clotting delay to compensate for. The gel does not dissolve into the specimen under correct centrifugation parameters, and centrifugation speed has no bearing on heparin's chemical activity. Following the manufacturer's specified speed and time ensures a reliable, stable barrier.
  37. The correct answer is A (EDTA itself can contain trace zinc contamination from manufacturing that would skew a zinc result): some EDTA salts used in standard tube manufacturing can introduce trace amounts of certain metals, including zinc, which would compromise the accuracy of a plasma zinc measurement even in an otherwise low-contamination royal blue tube. Heparin does not bind zinc more tightly than EDTA in a way that concentrates it, and this is not the actual mechanism at play. EDTA functions perfectly well as an anticoagulant regardless of a tube's metal-content manufacturing standard. Plasma yield differences between the two additives are not clinically significant for this application, so contamination risk is the deciding factor.
  38. The correct answer is C (The tube's additive coating failed to mix adequately, leaving localized areas without heparin exposure): heparin coats the interior tube wall and must be distributed throughout the entire blood volume by gentle inversion, so inadequate mixing can leave small regions of blood that never contacted enough heparin to be fully anticoagulated. This localized under-anticoagulation can allow minor fibrin formation even though the bulk of the specimen remains unclotted. Heparin's effectiveness is not temperature-dependent in the way described, and this finding does not automatically indicate tube mislabeling as EDTA, which would produce a different type of interference instead. Correct inversion technique at collection prevents this problem.
  39. The correct answer is D (Coagulation test results are directly ratio-dependent, since excess or insufficient citrate skews calcium availability): unlike many anticoagulants where a modest excess has little practical effect, coagulation testing measures precise clotting times after recalcification, so any deviation from the validated 9:1 ratio directly changes how much calcium is available to trigger the reaction being timed. This makes the ratio clinically significant rather than a simple manufacturing convenience or sizing standard. The ratio requirement applies to all patients, not only pediatric collections, since the underlying chemistry is identical regardless of age. This strict tolerance is why underfilled citrate tubes must always be rejected.
  40. The correct answer is B (Prolonged heat exposure can promote red cell swelling and platelet clumping, altering CBC parameters): EDTA whole blood specimens are meant to be stored at controlled room or refrigerated temperatures, and excessive heat can accelerate changes such as cell swelling or platelet aggregation that distort mean cell volume, platelet counts, and other CBC indices. This is a real preanalytical concern distinct from the additive's chemical function itself. The claim that EDTA remains entirely unaffected by heat is inaccurate, and EDTA does not chemically convert into a clot activator under thermal stress. Chemistry specimens in gold tubes can also be affected by heat, so the effect is not exclusive to lavender tubes.
  41. The correct answer is C (Formulation choice can affect assay validation, so labs must use the specific solution their procedures require): ACD solutions A and B differ in their exact citrate and dextrose concentrations, and laboratory procedures are validated against a specific formulation, so substituting the wrong one could introduce a variable the assay was never tested against. This is a real specimen-integrity consideration, not simply an interchangeable labelling difference, and the two solutions are not identical in their active ingredient concentration. The distinction is not based on patient age, and while shelf life can differ slightly between formulations, that is not the primary laboratory concern here. Following the specific procedure's stated tube formulation requirement is essential.
  42. The correct answer is A (Air in the butterfly tubing can under-fill the citrate tube, distorting the critical blood-to-additive ratio): butterfly needle sets contain tubing with dead space filled with air rather than blood, and if a citrate tube is drawn first, that air can occupy part of the vacuum draw and prevent the tube from filling to its intended level. Since citrate's accuracy depends entirely on a precise ratio, even a modest underfill from this air-space effect can meaningfully distort coagulation results. A discard tube absorbs that initial air pocket so the citrate tube that follows fills correctly. This practice is specific to citrate's ratio sensitivity and is not a universal requirement for every tube type or collection method.
  43. The correct answer is D (Heparin does not act through calcium chelation, so recalcification cannot trigger a timed clotting reaction): coagulation assays work by adding calcium back to citrated plasma to restart and time the clotting cascade from a controlled starting point. Heparin never removed calcium in the first place, so recalcification has no meaningful triggering effect, and heparin's direct antithrombin-mediated action would continue actively suppressing clot formation throughout the test, making timing meaningless. Heparin does not degrade clotting factors themselves, and tube volume or optical interference are not the relevant issues. This mechanistic incompatibility is why citrate remains the only appropriate additive for PT and PTT testing.
  44. The correct answer is B (Even distribution ensures both the fluoride's metabolic inhibition and the oxalate's anticoagulation act throughout the specimen): a grey-top tube contains two functionally distinct components, sodium fluoride for halting glycolysis and potassium oxalate or EDTA for preventing clotting, and both need to reach every part of the blood volume to work reliably. Skipping proper mixing risks leaving localized areas where glucose continues being metabolized or where microclots can form. Fluoride is already chemically active on contact and does not require a separate activation step through mixing. While preventing hemolysis is one benefit of gentle inversion generally, it is not the specific reason both additives in this tube require thorough distribution.
  45. The correct answer is A (Reject the specimen since most laboratory policies require citrate tubes to be filled within a narrow tolerance of the line): because coagulation results are extremely sensitive to the blood-to-citrate ratio, most Canadian laboratory policies set a narrow acceptable fill tolerance, often requiring at least around 90 percent of the labelled draw volume, well above the 80 percent seen in this scenario. There is no accepted practice of manually noting an approximate fill percentage and proceeding, since the resulting distortion in calcium availability cannot be reliably corrected through interpretation alone. Adjusting centrifuge speed does nothing to fix an already-incorrect additive ratio. Recollection with a properly filled tube is the appropriate response.
  46. The correct answer is C (Heparin plasma can be analyzed immediately without waiting for a clot to form, saving critical time): in urgent or critical care settings, the roughly 30 minute clotting wait required by a clot-activator tube is often clinically unacceptable, while heparin's anticoagulant action allows the specimen to be centrifuged and analyzed right away. This time advantage, not specimen volume or gas interference properties, is the genuine reason heparin tubes are favoured for rapid critical testing. Many point-of-care systems do accept other specimen types depending on the specific device, so heparin is not universally the only compatible option. Speed to result is the deciding clinical factor in this scenario.
  47. The correct answer is D (Yes, since EDTA contamination in a citrate tube would chelate additional calcium and falsely prolong results): trace EDTA carried over from a prior tube draw out of order, or from a poorly cleaned collection device, can bind extra calcium beyond what citrate alone accounts for, artificially prolonging PT and PTT results even though the patient's true coagulation status is normal. This is a real and clinically important preanalytical error the laboratory must consider when an unexplained prolongation is reported. EDTA and citrate are chemically distinct compounds and do not produce identical effects in every context, and contamination occurring only after centrifugation would have no opportunity to interact with the plasma's clotting factors. Recognizing this possibility can prompt a repeat draw before assuming a true clinical abnormality.
  48. The correct answer is B (Heparin, since it potentiates antithrombin III rather than removing calcium from the specimen): citrate and EDTA both achieve their anticoagulant effect by binding calcium, whether reversibly or irreversibly. SPS, the additive used in blood culture bottles, works by an entirely different mechanism again: it inhibits complement, phagocytes, and certain antibiotics rather than chelating calcium, so it does not belong in a calcium-binding group either. Heparin leaves calcium entirely untouched and instead dramatically boosts antithrombin III's natural ability to inactivate thrombin and other clotting factors. This fundamental mechanistic difference is why heparin plasma still contains free calcium available for other testing purposes.
  49. The correct answer is C (EDTA's irreversible calcium chelation can better inhibit certain enzymatic degradation processes affecting cytokine stability): because many enzymatic reactions, including some proteolytic processes that can degrade sensitive analytes, depend on calcium as a cofactor, EDTA's thorough and irreversible calcium removal can help protect fragile analytes like certain cytokines from ongoing breakdown after collection. Immunoassay platforms are broadly compatible with multiple specimen types, so EDTA is not uniquely compatible in that sense. Both EDTA and heparin substantially reduce platelet activation risk compared to no anticoagulant, so this is not a meaningful distinguishing factor, and plasma yield differences between the two are not clinically significant. Specimen stability for the specific analyte drives this type of additive selection.
  50. The correct answer is A (Sodium citrate with reversible calcium chelation, allowing recalcification for timed coagulation testing): citrate's defining and testable property is that its calcium binding can be reversed by adding calcium back during PT or PTT analysis, which is what allows the analyzer to precisely time the clotting cascade from a controlled starting point. Sodium fluoride's core role is inhibiting glycolysis enzymatically rather than chelating calcium, and it is potassium oxalate or EDTA within the same grey tube that provides anticoagulation. Heparin never chelates calcium at all, instead potentiating antithrombin III, and SPS's defining role is inhibiting host defence factors for blood culture growth rather than acting through calcium chelation. Matching mechanism to additive rather than memorizing colour alone is exactly the level of understanding this domain tests.
  51. The correct answer is A (SPS neutralizes complement, phagocytes, and certain antibiotics in the sample): sodium polyanetholesulfonate inhibits the patient's own bactericidal defences and inactivates some antibiotic classes already circulating in the blood. This preserves any organisms present so the microbiology lab can recover and identify them on culture media. It is not simply a pH adjuster or a calcium chelator, and it does not destroy white cells outright. Drawing blood cultures before antibiotics are started further maximizes the chance of a positive, clinically useful result.
  52. The correct answer is B (Acid citrate dextrose, which preserves white cell DNA for typing studies): ACD tubes are commonly used for blood bank compatibility work, DNA and paternity testing, and HLA phenotyping because the anticoagulant maintains viable white cells and their genetic material well. Sodium fluoride and potassium oxalate belong to grey-top tubes intended for glucose stability, not DNA integrity. Heparin tubes are not the standard choice for this category of identity testing. The laboratory's specific instruction to use yellow over lavender reflects this established preference for ACD.
  53. The correct answer is C (It reversibly binds calcium, removing the cofactor the clotting cascade needs): sodium citrate chelates ionized calcium, and because calcium is essential to several coagulation factor reactions, the cascade cannot proceed while it is bound. This binding is reversible, which is why the citrate-plasma sample can later be recalcified in the laboratory analyzer to trigger the exact reaction being timed for PT/INR. Citrate does not degrade fibrinogen, coat the tube, or rely on cooling. Because this mechanism depends on a precise blood-to-citrate ratio, an underfilled tube will distort results.
  54. The correct answer is D (The fixed citrate volume becomes relatively too concentrated for the reduced plasma): the tube is manufactured with a set amount of liquid citrate calibrated to a 9:1 blood-to-additive ratio, so a short draw leaves proportionally more citrate relative to plasma. That excess citrate binds more calcium than intended, artificially prolonging clot-based results once the sample is recalcified. This is not primarily about reagent dilution, oxidation, or tissue fluid contamination. Because the effect is specific to the fixed-volume citrate chemistry, other additive tubes are comparatively more forgiving of a modest underfill.
  55. The correct answer is B (SST clot activator speeds clotting, and the gel only separates cells afterward): the gold tube's clot activator, typically silica particles, promotes rapid, complete clotting rather than preventing it, and the thixotropic gel simply forms a physical barrier between serum and cells during centrifugation. This is the opposite mechanism from citrate, which prevents clotting by chelating calcium so a liquid plasma sample results instead of serum. The gel does not bind calcium or fibrinogen chemically. This distinction is why SST tubes are unsuitable for coagulation testing, which requires plasma that never clotted.
  56. The correct answer is A (The separator gel forms a stable barrier that keeps serum from re-contacting cells): after centrifugation the gel settles at the interface between the packed cells and the serum, physically blocking further exchange of substances like potassium or glucose between the two layers. This makes gold tubes convenient for most chemistry, serology, and immunology testing since serum quality is preserved longer than in a plain red tube. The clot activator speeds clotting but does not lyse cells or remove lipids, and the tube still produces serum, not plasma. A plain red tube remains preferable only when gel interference with a specific assay must be avoided.
  57. The correct answer is D (It potentiates antithrombin III, which then inactivates thrombin and other clotting factors): heparin dramatically accelerates antithrombin III's natural ability to neutralize thrombin and several activated clotting factors, stopping the cascade without touching calcium at all. This is a fundamentally different pathway from citrate or EDTA, both of which work by chelating calcium instead. Because calcium remains available in heparinized plasma, heparin tubes can sometimes be used for stat ionized calcium testing when EDTA or citrate plasma would not be suitable. Heparin plasma is favoured for rapid chemistry results because no clotting time is needed before centrifugation.
  58. The correct answer is C (EDTA's stronger, irreversible calcium binding best preserves cell size and morphology): EDTA chelates calcium so tightly and completely that it prevents clotting and platelet clumping more effectively than heparin, keeping red cells, white cells, and platelets in a state closest to their in vivo appearance for automated counting and manual smear review. It does not liquefy blood by a different mechanism than chelation, and it has no effect on cell division or hemoglobin structure. Because this irreversible chelation alters calcium-dependent chemistry results, EDTA tubes are unsuitable for most chemistry panels and for coagulation testing.
  59. The correct answer is A (Fluoride halts ongoing glycolysis, preventing red cells from continuing to consume glucose): sodium fluoride is a glycolysis inhibitor that stops red blood cells and any bacteria present from metabolizing glucose after the draw, which is critical whenever transport or processing will be delayed. The actual anticoagulant in the tube is typically potassium oxalate or EDTA, not the fluoride itself. Fluoride does not accelerate clotting or bind glucose directly. Because glucose falls roughly ten percent per hour in an unpreserved sample, choosing grey over gold protects the clinical accuracy of a delayed glucose or lactate result.
  60. The correct answer is C (It is manufactured to minimize trace metal contamination that could skew results): royal blue tubes are produced with strict controls on trace metal content in the glass, stopper, and any additive so that testing for substances like lead, zinc, or copper is not falsely elevated by contamination from the collection device itself. The specific additive inside, whether none, EDTA, or heparin, depends on which trace element or toxicology test is ordered, not on any lead-binding chemistry. Stopper thickness and instrument compatibility are not the defining feature. This is why substituting an ordinary tube would risk an inaccurate, clinically misleading result.
  61. The correct answer is B (A 9:1 blood-to-citrate ratio, since deviation changes how much calcium remains available): the standard fill is nine parts blood to one part liquid citrate, and because citrate works by chelating calcium, any deviation changes how much free calcium remains after recalcification during testing. A short draw leaves relatively too much citrate and falsely prolongs clot-based results, while an overfilled tube dilutes the citrate too much. This ratio has nothing to do with platelet counts, which are not measured from this tube. Maintaining an exact fill is therefore essential to reporting a trustworthy INR or PTT.
  62. The correct answer is D (Heparin potentiates a natural inhibitor without touching calcium, while EDTA chelates calcium directly): heparin works indirectly by boosting antithrombin III's inactivation of thrombin, leaving calcium levels essentially unchanged, whereas EDTA works by directly and irreversibly binding calcium so the cascade cannot proceed at all. This difference explains why heparin plasma remains usable for some calcium-dependent chemistry tests while EDTA plasma does not. Neither additive destroys fibrinogen, and they are not simply concentration variants of one mechanism. Recognizing this distinction prevents the trainee from choosing the wrong tube for a calcium-sensitive order.
  63. The correct answer is C (Phagocytes, which would otherwise engulf and destroy circulating bacteria before culturing): SPS inhibits phagocytic cells alongside complement proteins and select antibiotics, together protecting any bacteria or fungi present so the microbiology laboratory can recover a positive culture rather than a false negative. Erythrocytes are not part of the immune defence being neutralized here, and platelets and lymphocyte antibody production are not the primary targets of SPS either. This broad inhibitory effect is precisely why yellow blood culture bottles are drawn before, not after, any antibiotic administration whenever clinically possible. Timely, correct-order collection maximizes diagnostic yield.
  64. The correct answer is D (The additive preserves white blood cell viability better for typing and crossmatch studies): acid citrate dextrose maintains healthier, more viable leukocytes than many other anticoagulants, which is valuable for blood bank compatibility testing, HLA phenotyping, and related tissue typing work. ACD does not lyse red cells or separate different people's cells within a single tube, and its citrate concentration is formulated for cell preservation rather than maximal anticoagulant strength. This is why laboratories specifically request yellow over lavender or green for donor compatibility workups. Proper tube selection here directly affects transplant matching accuracy.
  65. The correct answer is A (Some analytes can adsorb onto or interact with gel, so plain serum avoids that interference): certain drugs and hormones are known to interact with separator gel to a degree that can falsely lower measured levels, so manufacturers sometimes specifically exclude gel tubes for those assays. A plain red tube contains no clot activator or gel and typically clots more slowly, not faster, than gold. It still produces serum, not plasma, and it contains no special hormone-stabilizing additive. Following the assay manufacturer's tube requirement protects the clinical reliability of the result.
  66. The correct answer is B (A lithium salt heparin tube would falsely elevate a lithium level ordered on the sample): when heparin is formulated as the lithium salt, drawing a therapeutic lithium level in that same tube introduces exogenous lithium and produces a falsely high result, so sodium or ammonium heparin, or a different tube entirely, must be used instead. The tube still centrifuges normally to yield plasma and does not spontaneously clot. This interference is specific to lithium testing, not glucose. Recognizing additive-specific interference like this is essential whenever multiple tests share one collection tube.
  67. The correct answer is D (ACD's citrate concentration and ratio are formulated for cell preservation, not clot-time accuracy): ACD tubes are optimized to keep white cells and other formed elements viable for typing work, and their citrate concentration and blood-to-additive ratio differ from the standardized formulation coagulation testing depends on for a reliable, reproducible clot time. ACD does contain citrate, just at a different concentration than light blue tubes. Neither tube type is defined by glass-only construction or the absence of vacuum draw. This is why laboratories keep the two citrate-based tube types strictly separate by intended use.
  68. The correct answer is A (A grey fluoride tube, because fluoride limits ongoing metabolism that could alter the ethanol level): sodium fluoride is an effective preservative for blood alcohol testing because it slows continued cellular and microbial metabolism during storage and transport, helping keep the ethanol concentration stable until analysis. EDTA, SST gel, and heparin do not offer this same metabolic preservation effect for ethanol specifically. Red cells do not produce ethanol under any of these additives regardless. This is one reason grey-top tubes are the standard choice whenever glucose, lactate, or alcohol stability over time matters clinically or legally.
  69. The correct answer is C (Inadequate mixing left some heparin undissolved, so part of the sample never contacted the additive): heparin must be evenly distributed throughout the blood by the recommended number of gentle inversions immediately after filling, and if mixing is delayed or insufficient, localized clotting can begin before the additive reaches that portion of the sample. Heparin's anticoagulant action does not simply expire within seconds of collection under normal conditions. Tourniquet time and needle gauge are not the mechanism behind heparin's calcium-independent anticoagulation. Proper technique at the bedside is therefore essential to obtaining a usable plasma sample.
  70. The correct answer is B (Heparin can cause a blue background staining artifact that obscures white cell morphology): heparin is known to interfere with Wright-Giemsa staining, producing a diffuse blue background on the smear that makes evaluating white cell detail more difficult, which is one reason EDTA remains the preferred anticoagulant for CBC and differential work. Heparin does not dissolve platelets or uniformly shrink red cells, and it does not cause outright red cell rupture. EDTA's superior preservation of cell size, shape, and staining characteristics is exactly why the lavender tube is the correct choice for this order rather than green.
  71. The correct answer is B (Fluoride inhibits glycolysis while oxalate acts as the actual anticoagulant preventing clot formation): sodium fluoride's role is preserving glucose and lactate levels by halting ongoing cellular metabolism, while potassium oxalate, or sometimes EDTA depending on the manufacturer, is the component that actually prevents the sample from clotting. Fluoride alone has only weak anticoagulant properties and is not relied upon for that purpose. The two additives are not redundant chelators working the same pathway, nor does either activate clotting. Understanding this division of labour explains why grey tubes list both ingredients on the label.
  72. The correct answer is C (Less plasma volume is present per unit of whole blood, so relatively more citrate binds available calcium): when hematocrit is markedly elevated, the plasma fraction of the total blood volume shrinks, so the standard fixed citrate volume becomes disproportionately concentrated relative to the smaller plasma pool, chelating more of the available calcium than intended and falsely prolonging clot times. Citrate is not destroyed faster by high hematocrit, and plasma calcium concentration itself is not directly changed by hematocrit. This is a recognized, testable exception to the routine 9:1 ratio that laboratories account for with adjusted tubes when hematocrit is significantly abnormal.
  73. The correct answer is A (Each additive needs enough gentle mixing to dissolve and distribute fully without causing hemolysis): the recommended inversion count for each tube type is based on ensuring the specific additive, whether citrate, EDTA, heparin, or fluoride, mixes thoroughly throughout the sample while minimizing mechanical trauma to the cells that vigorous or excessive mixing could cause. This is a deliberate, evidence-based standard, not an arbitrary branding choice, and more inversions are not automatically better since over-mixing risks hemolysis. Under-mixing risks microclots or incomplete additive action instead. Following the correct count for each additive protects both anticoagulant function and specimen integrity.
  74. The correct answer is D (Degraded additive may allow glycolysis to continue, producing a falsely low glucose result): as additives age past their stability date, their potency can decline, meaning the fluoride may no longer fully suppress glycolysis, allowing red cells to continue consuming glucose after the draw and yielding a falsely decreased result. Expired additive does not reliably cause full clotting, since fluoride and its accompanying anticoagulant are separate functions. There is no known transformation of fluoride into another compound simply from aging. This is exactly why expiration dates on collection tubes must be checked before every use, not treated as a formality.
  75. The correct answer is A (Royal blue tubes are manufactured with different additive options, so the correct one must match the analyte): royal blue trace-element tubes are available as plain, EDTA, or heparin versions, and the correct one depends on whether the ordered test requires serum, EDTA plasma, or heparin plasma, so laboratories must verify the exact tube matches the assay requirement. It is inaccurate to say these tubes never contain additive, since several formulations exist. The tube's low-contamination manufacturing does not chemically adapt itself to different metals. Selecting the wrong internal additive for a trace element or toxicology order can still compromise result accuracy despite the tube's overall low-contamination design.
  76. The correct answer is D (Light blue and lavender chelate calcium directly, while green heparin acts indirectly through antithrombin III): citrate in light blue tubes and EDTA in lavender tubes both prevent clotting by directly binding available calcium, though citrate does so reversibly and EDTA irreversibly, while heparin in green tubes works through an entirely different pathway by potentiating antithrombin III rather than touching calcium. This grouping is why heparin plasma can sometimes support calcium-dependent testing that citrate or EDTA plasma cannot. Confusing these mechanisms could lead to selecting an inappropriate tube for a calcium- or coagulation-related order.
  77. The correct answer is B (Both yield comparable serum results for routine lipids, though SST offers a longer post-spin barrier): for most routine lipid analytes, either tube produces acceptable serum once clotted and centrifuged, with the SST tube's added benefit being the physical gel barrier that helps maintain serum integrity if analysis is slightly delayed after centrifugation. Gel does not destroy lipid molecules, and plain red tubes do clot, just without a clot activator to speed the process. Lipids are a serum-based test, not one requiring citrate plasma. The practical choice often comes down to laboratory workflow and any known gel-interference concerns for other analytes on the same requisition.
  78. The correct answer is C (Trace EDTA carryover into a citrate tube can chelate additional calcium and distort coagulation results): because both additives affect calcium and coagulation-related testing is especially sensitive to even small contamination, drawing citrate before EDTA in the sequence reduces the risk that trace EDTA carried on the needle or tube adaptor alters the citrate tube's calcium-dependent chemistry. Citrate carryover into EDTA does not destroy white cells, and citrate is not first purely because of fill volume. EDTA tubes are not broadly incompatible with tubes drawn afterward in general. This order-of-draw principle exists specifically to protect coagulation result accuracy.
  79. The correct answer is D (Certain lipophilic drugs can adsorb into or interact with the gel, artificially lowering measured levels): some medications have chemical properties that allow them to partition into or bind onto separator gel, reducing the concentration measurable in the serum above it and producing a falsely low therapeutic drug level. The gel does not chemically convert the drug into a metabolite or meaningfully change sample pH. This interaction is not limited to antibiotics and can affect other drug classes as well. Laboratories flag known problem analytes so phlebotomists select a plain tube whenever gel interference has been documented.
  80. The correct answer is B (Both additives function by binding calcium, which falsely lowers the measured calcium concentration): since citrate and EDTA prevent clotting precisely by chelating calcium, any sample collected in either tube will show an artificially low or even unmeasurable calcium level, making both tube types fundamentally unsuitable for calcium testing regardless of proper mixing or fill volume. Neither additive releases extra calcium from the tube itself. The concern is not about clotting speed but about the additive's direct chemical action on the analyte being measured. A plain, gold, or appropriate heparin tube is instead used for accurate calcium results.
  81. The correct answer is A (Without a glycolysis inhibitor, ongoing cellular metabolism can falsely lower the reported glucose value): red blood cells continue consuming glucose after collection unless a glycolysis inhibitor like sodium fluoride is present, so a gold tube left standing for a prolonged period before separation risks a falsely decreased glucose value that could mislead a tolerance test curve. Gold tubes do not chemically raise glucose, and centrifugation limits are not the relevant concern here. Gold tubes do not require immediate refrigeration to function correctly, either. This is precisely why grey-top tubes remain the standard when any meaningful processing delay is anticipated.
  82. The correct answer is C (Lavender EDTA binds calcium irreversibly, so the sample cannot later be recalcified for clot-based testing): unlike citrate, whose calcium binding can be reversed in the laboratory by adding calcium back for coagulation analyzers, EDTA's chelation is essentially permanent, which is precisely why lavender tubes are not used for coagulation studies even though both additives prevent clotting through calcium removal. Light blue citrate is the reversible one, not EDTA, and gold gel does not chelate calcium at all. Green heparin does not bind calcium and remains usable for many chemistry tests. This permanence is a defining, testable feature distinguishing EDTA from citrate.
  83. The correct answer is C (Sodium fluoride with an anticoagulant, since fluoride limits ongoing anaerobic metabolism that raises lactate): lactate, like glucose, is sensitive to ongoing cellular metabolism after collection, and cells continuing anaerobic glycolysis in an unpreserved sample can produce falsely elevated lactate over time, which the grey tube's fluoride specifically counters. Citrate, heparin, and EDTA each work through calcium or clotting-cascade mechanisms unrelated to halting glycolytic lactate production. This is why grey-top tubes are the correct choice for both glucose and lactate testing, especially when transport delays are expected. The incident highlights the clinical importance of matching the additive to the metabolic behaviour being measured.
  84. The correct answer is A (The specific assay may require serum, and EDTA can interfere with certain trace metal chemistries): different trace element and toxicology assays are validated against either serum or a particular plasma type, and because EDTA or other additives can interfere with some metal assays, the laboratory specifies the exact formulation, plain, EDTA, or heparin, that matches the analytic method being used. Royal blue tubes are manufactured in multiple additive versions, so a plain option genuinely exists as a deliberate choice, not a size limitation. Clotting speed is not the deciding factor for this trace element order. Matching the correct formulation protects both accuracy and low-contamination integrity.
  85. The correct answer is D (An underfilled tube left excess unbound citrate available to chelate calcium during testing): a short draw is one of the most frequent, entirely preventable causes of a falsely prolonged coagulation result because it leaves relatively too much citrate for the reduced plasma volume, chelating more calcium than the standardized ratio intends. Overfilling would dilute citrate and could shorten rather than prolong results, and correct inversion counts are designed to avoid, not cause, factor damage. Butterfly needle use with a discard tube is a separate technique consideration and does not by itself shorten citrate's reaction chemistry. This is why fill volume is checked before any coagulation specimen is accepted.
  86. The correct answer is B (The clot activator speeds clot formation, while the gel only creates a barrier after centrifugation): these are two distinct, sequential functions within the same tube, the clot activator promoting rapid, complete clotting before centrifugation begins, and the gel then migrating to form a stable physical barrier between serum and cells during the spin itself. Neither component chelates calcium, since the tube's purpose is to produce clotted serum, not anticoagulated plasma. The clot activator does not dissolve the gel; the two work independently at different stages of processing. Understanding this sequence clarifies why SST is inappropriate whenever a truly unclotted plasma sample is required.
  87. The correct answer is B (A markedly elevated potassium and low calcium, reflecting EDTA's anticoagulant and chelating chemistry): EDTA is typically formulated as a potassium salt and its strong calcium chelation would produce a characteristic pattern of falsely elevated potassium alongside falsely decreased calcium if it contaminated an electrolyte sample, a well-recognized red flag for laboratories investigating unexpected results. This pattern is distinct from what heparin contamination would produce, since heparin does not chelate calcium or contain a potassium salt in most formulations. EDTA has no meaningful direct effect on glucose. Recognizing this signature pattern helps laboratories catch tube mix-ups before releasing a misleading clinical result.
  88. The correct answer is D (Heparin plasma avoids the time needed for clot formation, allowing faster centrifugation and analysis): because heparin prevents clotting immediately rather than requiring the sample to fully clot first, plasma can be separated and analyzed sooner, which matters for analytes like ammonia that are unstable and rise quickly after collection if processing is delayed. Heparin does not chemically neutralize ammonia itself; it only speeds the path to analysis. Cold-chain compatibility is not exclusive to heparin tubes. Plasma and serum ammonia should reflect the same true physiological level when processed correctly, so the difference in preference is about speed, not a baseline chemical offset.
  89. The correct answer is C (The blood has already fully clotted in the SST tube, so no liquid, uncoagulated plasma remains to test): coagulation testing requires plasma that never clotted so the analyzer can time how long it takes to form a clot under controlled conditions, and once blood has been allowed to clot in an SST tube, that essential unclotted state is permanently gone and cannot be restored by any laboratory process. The gel itself does not chemically destroy clotting factors; the clotting has simply already happened. Stopper colour recognition is not the underlying scientific issue. This is why tube selection at the bedside, not just laboratory processing, is critical for coagulation orders.
  90. The correct answer is A (Plasma still contains clotting factors and fibrinogen because clotting was chemically prevented from occurring): because an anticoagulant like EDTA, citrate, or heparin stops the coagulation cascade before it can consume clotting factors, the resulting plasma retains fibrinogen and other factors, unlike serum, which forms only after those factors have already been used up during clot formation. Plasma and serum are not chemically identical fluids for this reason. Clot activators promote serum formation, not plasma, so that statement has the relationship reversed. Fibrinogen is not physically removed by centrifugation; it is consumed biochemically during clotting in tubes without anticoagulant.
  91. The correct answer is D (Reduced chelating capacity could allow partial clotting before testing, invalidating the timed result): degraded additive may not chelate calcium as effectively as intended, risking premature partial clot activation before the sample reaches the analyzer, which would invalidate the precisely timed PT/INR measurement the test depends on. This would not necessarily cause an instant, visibly obvious clot at the moment of draw. Citrate degradation is chemically related to its calcium-binding function, not unrelated to it. Citrate does not spontaneously convert into an entirely different additive class like heparin. Rejecting visibly compromised tubes protects the accuracy of a clinically important anticoagulation monitoring result.
  92. The correct answer is C (Citrate plasma is formulated specifically for clot-time testing, and other assays may not be validated on it): laboratories validate each assay against a specific specimen type, and because citrate plasma's chemistry is optimized and standardized for coagulation testing, other analytes are frequently not validated for accuracy on that same matrix and require their own dedicated tube instead. Volume alone is not typically the limiting factor for this restriction. Drug molecules are not generally destroyed by citrate simply through contact. Coagulation testing does not necessarily consume the tube's entire contents, but appropriate specimen validation, not leftover volume, governs whether another test can share the tube.
  93. The correct answer is B (Citrate's fixed-volume chemistry is uniquely ratio-dependent, so an inadequate fill invalidates the entire result): because citrate's calcium-chelating action depends on a precise blood-to-additive ratio, even a modest underfill can meaningfully distort PT and PTT results in a way that EDTA tubes, which are comparatively more tolerant of minor volume variation, generally are not. This makes protecting the light blue tube's minimum fill a genuine clinical priority when total obtainable volume is limited. CBC testing does not inherently require a larger blood volume than coagulation testing. Recognizing which additive is most volume-sensitive helps a phlebotomist make a defensible collection decision under difficult draw conditions.
  94. The correct answer is A (EDTA is commonly formulated as a potassium salt, adding exogenous potassium directly into the sample): dipotassium or tripotassium EDTA introduces additional potassium into the sample simply from the additive's own chemical formulation, which is a well-documented cause of falsely elevated potassium whenever EDTA plasma is mistakenly used for an electrolyte panel. This is distinct from hemolysis-related potassium release, though hemolysis is a separate, additional concern for any tube type. EDTA does not interfere with the analyzer's electrode mechanically, and it does not convert sodium into potassium chemically. This is a specific, testable reason gold or plain tubes remain the standard for routine electrolyte panels.
  95. The correct answer is C (A green heparin tube, since it yields plasma through antithrombin potentiation rather than calcium binding): heparin is the only common anticoagulant among these options that produces plasma without relying on calcium chelation, making it the appropriate choice whenever an assay specifically needs plasma with calcium levels left essentially undisturbed. Citrate's chelation is not negligible; it is the defining feature of how that tube prevents clotting. EDTA's chelation affects calcium-dependent chemistry broadly, not exclusively coagulation testing. A plain red tube produces serum after clotting, not plasma at all, so it cannot satisfy a plasma-specific requirement regardless of its additive-free status.
  96. The correct answer is B (Yellow protects living organisms from immune destruction, while light blue protects clotting factors from premature activation): the yellow bottle's SPS inhibits the body's own antimicrobial defences so bacteria or fungi survive long enough to be cultured, while the light blue tube's citrate chelates calcium so the coagulation cascade cannot activate prematurely, preserving the sample in an unclotted state until testing. These two tubes serve entirely different clinical purposes and do not use interchangeable chemistry. Both do prevent some form of premature reaction, but describing them as generic is inaccurate given how specifically each additive is engineered for its target. This distinction is fundamental to understanding additive selection across the exam blueprint.
  97. The correct answer is D (The clot activator and gel in an SST tube are not designed to preserve viable organisms for microbial growth): SST tubes are engineered for chemistry and serology testing, not for supporting or protecting microbial viability, so they lack the SPS-based inhibition of complement, phagocytes, and antibiotics that blood culture bottles rely on to keep organisms alive for identification. There is no evidence the clot activator actively and rapidly kills bacteria on contact, but the tube simply is not validated or designed for this purpose either way. Gold tubes are not sterile-locked in a way that prevents opening for culture, and they do not support equivalent organism growth even with longer incubation. This is why the correct bottle type must be used for any suspected bloodstream infection workup.
  98. The correct answer is A (EDTA's irreversible calcium chelation both falsely lowers calcium and permanently prevents recalcification for clot timing): because EDTA binds calcium so tightly and permanently, a calcium level drawn in this tube would read falsely low, and a coagulation test would be unusable since the calcium needed to trigger a timed clot reaction can never be added back effectively. EDTA's chelation is irreversible, not reversible like citrate's, which is precisely why it fails both tests for the same underlying reason. EDTA does interact directly and strongly with calcium; that is its defining mechanism. EDTA does not prematurely activate clotting factors; it prevents clotting entirely. This dual mechanistic failure is why lavender tubes are excluded from both order types.
  99.
