The correct answer is B (Drawing citrate first avoids possible tissue thromboplastin contamination affecting the coagulation result): tissue thromboplastin released at the start of venipuncture can activate clotting factors, and because coagulation testing is extremely sensitive to any premature activation, citrate is drawn early in the sequence to minimize this contamination risk relative to other tube types. The concern is not clot activator leaking backward from a tube drawn later, since order-of-draw sequencing is designed to prevent forward, not backward, contamination in this context. Vacuum pressure differences between tube types are not the reasoning behind this particular rule. Gel solidification inside the needle is not a recognized mechanism either. This sequencing rule exists specifically to protect the accuracy of coagulation results.
  100. The correct answer is D (Citrate prevents clotting through calcium chelation, while fluoride preserves glucose by inhibiting glycolysis, not clotting): citrate's role is genuinely anticoagulant, stopping the clotting cascade by removing available calcium, while fluoride's primary contribution is metabolic preservation of glucose and lactate by halting glycolysis, with the grey tube relying on a separate ingredient like potassium oxalate for its actual anticoagulant action. Confusing these two distinct roles, preventing clotting versus preserving an analyte after the fact, is exactly the kind of mechanism-based error the committee identified. Neither additive functions as a clot activator, and the two are not interchangeable substitutes for one another. Understanding this distinction reduces exactly the kind of selection error the quality review uncovered.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Additive carryover between tubes can distort results in tubes drawn afterward): trace amounts of an additive from one tube can transfer into the next tube collected via the needle or tubing. This carryover changes the chemical composition of subsequent specimens, sometimes enough to alter a reported result. EDTA carryover into a later tube, for example, can falsely elevate potassium and falsely lower calcium. Following the standardized order minimizes this risk across every draw.
  2. The correct answer is D (21 gauge, the standard choice for a normal adult vein): a 21 gauge needle provides an appropriate bore for routine adult venipuncture on healthy, easily accessible veins. Remember that a larger gauge number corresponds to a smaller physical bore, so 21 gauge sits comfortably between the finer pediatric options and oversized needles unnecessary for this patient. Using an unnecessarily large bore risks patient discomfort and vein trauma. A 21 gauge needle balances flow rate with vessel safety for this presentation.
  3. The correct answer is D (The light blue tube containing sodium citrate anticoagulant, used for coagulation testing): the sodium citrate coagulation tube is drawn second, right after any blood culture collection and before serum tubes with clot activator. This sequence protects the citrate tube's critical 9:1 blood-to-anticoagulant ratio from contamination by additives carried over from tubes drawn earlier. Placing it ahead of the serum tube also prevents clot activator from later interfering with coagulation testing. The chart confirms this fixed position in the standardized order.
  4. The correct answer is C (Straight needle with an evacuated tube system holder): a straight needle and holder combination is the default method for a healthy, accessible antecubital vein with no special draw considerations. This method is efficient for filling multiple tubes in sequence without added equipment complexity. Winged sets and syringes are reserved for fragile, small, or difficult veins that need gentler handling. Since none of those factors apply here, the evacuated tube system remains the appropriate first choice.
  5. The correct answer is A (The winged infusion set, with its flexible tubing and short bevel): the short needle and flexible tubing of a winged set allow controlled, low-angle access to small hand veins that a rigid straight needle cannot safely reach. Its wings also give the phlebotomist a stable, precise grip during insertion into a mobile or fragile vessel. The device shown attaches to a standard holder, so multiple tubes can still be collected in sequence. This makes it the preferred choice for hand vein draws.
  6. The correct answer is A (A retractable or shielded safety-engineered needle device for routine venipuncture): Canadian occupational health and safety requirements now expect safety-engineered devices as the standard for venipuncture, since these designs actively reduce accidental needlestick exposure. A retractable or shielded mechanism activates automatically or with minimal effort after use, covering the sharp point before disposal. This protects both the phlebotomist and downstream waste handlers from injury. Facilities are expected to supply these devices rather than relying solely on technique or gloves.
  7. The correct answer is D (The green-top tube containing heparin additive, used mainly for plasma chemistry testing): the heparin tube sits in the fourth position of the standardized order, collected after the serum tube and before the EDTA tube. This placement limits the chance that clot activator from the serum tube interferes with heparinized plasma testing. It also keeps heparin carryover away from the EDTA tube's cell-based testing further down the sequence. The chart confirms this fixed fourth position for green-top tubes.
  8. The correct answer is B (Use single-patient-use disposable tourniquets or clean reusable ones between patients): infection control expectations require either a disposable tourniquet discarded after one patient or a reusable tourniquet properly cleaned before reuse. Tourniquets contact skin directly and can harbour organisms if reused without cleaning. Facilities may choose either approach, but a shared, uncleaned tourniquet creates unacceptable cross-contamination risk between patients. Consistent adherence to whichever policy is in place protects both patients and staff.
  9. The correct answer is C (It pierces the tube stopper to establish vacuum draw): the rear needle sheath inside the holder barrel punctures the evacuated tube's rubber stopper once the tube is pushed forward. This action opens the pathway between the vein and the tube, allowing the tube's internal vacuum to draw blood in automatically. The front needle remains in the vein while successive tubes are exchanged over the same rear sheath. This double-pointed design is what allows one venipuncture to fill multiple tubes.
  10. The correct answer is B (Yes, because air in the winged set tubing can under-fill the citrate tube): the flexible tubing of a winged infusion set holds a short air-filled segment before blood begins to flow. If citrate is the first tube drawn, that air can prevent the tube from filling completely, skewing the critical 9:1 blood-to-anticoagulant ratio needed for accurate coagulation testing. A discard tube collected first removes that air from the line. This step is unnecessary with a straight needle drawn directly into a holder, since there is no air-filled tubing segment.
  11. The correct answer is B (Blood culture bottle or tube, to protect sterility): blood culture collections are drawn first to protect the sterility of the sample, since any contamination from needle exposure to skin flora or additive carryover could produce a false-positive culture result. This priority overrides every other consideration in the standardized order. Every anticoagulant and clot-activator tube follows only after the culture bottle is secured. The chart reflects this fixed first position clearly.
  12. The correct answer is C (EDTA carryover can prevent the serum tube from clotting normally): EDTA is a strong calcium-chelating anticoagulant, and even trace carryover into a serum tube can interfere with the clotting cascade that serum tubes rely on. This can leave fibrin strands or incomplete clot formation in the serum tube, compromising the specimen for chemistry testing. The standardized order draws serum tubes before EDTA specifically to prevent this contamination. Reversing that sequence risks producing an unusable or inaccurate serum sample.
  13. The correct answer is D (It pierces the tube stopper so vacuum draws blood safely): a blood transfer device attaches to the syringe and pierces each tube's stopper in turn, letting the tube's own vacuum pull blood directly from the syringe barrel. This avoids the unsafe practice of removing the needle and manually pouring blood into open tubes. Using the device also preserves the correct order of draw across tubes filled from the same syringe. This maintains both safety and specimen integrity for the collection.
  14. The correct answer is C (23 gauge, commonly used in a butterfly configuration): a 23 gauge needle, typically part of a winged infusion set, provides a fine enough bore to safely access small pediatric or geriatric veins without excessive trauma. Its smaller diameter suits the reduced vessel size while still permitting adequate blood flow for testing. Larger gauges like 18 or 21 risk collapsing or rupturing a fragile infant vein. This makes 23 gauge the standard choice for this patient population.
  15. The correct answer is B (The grey-top tube containing fluoride and oxalate additives, drawn last in sequence): the grey-top tube containing a glycolysis inhibitor and oxalate anticoagulant is collected last in the standardized order. Its additive combination has less potential to interfere with the tests performed on earlier tubes, so it is positioned at the end of the sequence. This placement also protects earlier collections from any carryover of oxalate or fluoride. The chart confirms grey as the final position among the six standard tube types.
  16. The correct answer is C (The blood culture first, to protect it from contamination): blood cultures take absolute priority in the order of draw because sterility is essential to producing a reliable result. Drawing the culture bottle first avoids any risk of introducing additive carryover or skin flora picked up during subsequent tube changes. A contaminated blood culture can lead to false-positive results and unnecessary antibiotic treatment. The chemistry panel and every other tube follow only after the culture has been safely secured.
  17. The correct answer is A (Approximately 7 to 10 centimetres above the site): applying the tourniquet roughly 7 to 10 centimetres above the intended puncture site allows enough venous engorgement to make the vein palpable without placing it so close that it interferes with needle insertion or skin cleaning. The flat strap and quick-release buckle shown allow rapid, even application and release. Correct positioning also reduces the risk of hemoconcentration affecting results if left on too long. This distance is the standard reference point taught in phlebotomy training.

An under-filled tube leaves too much citrate relative to blood, which artificially prolongs coagulation results such as PT and INR. This makes the specimen unreliable regardless of how carefully it is otherwise handled. Laboratories reject these tubes rather than risk reporting an inaccurate coagulation result.

This position keeps the tube's clot activator away from the citrate tube collected just before it, preserving the coagulation specimen's integrity. It also positions serum ahead of heparin so clot activator carryover cannot trigger unwanted clotting in the anticoagulated tube that follows. The chart confirms this fixed third position.

This design directly addresses the leading cause of bloodborne pathogen exposure among phlebotomy staff. Conventional exposed needles carry a much higher injury risk during and after the draw. Facilities adopt these devices specifically to meet current occupational health and safety expectations.

Requisition size, collection speed, and bandage allergies have no bearing on this equipment choice. Vein condition is the deciding factor here.

Current safety policy treats the entire holder-and-needle unit as single-patient equipment. This device shown reinforces why the holder itself, not just the needle, must be discarded after each patient.

This is one of the clearest clinical reasons EDTA is positioned after chemistry and serum tubes in the standardized order. Understanding the underlying additive interactions helps explain why the sequence is fixed rather than flexible.

The additive-carryover reasoning behind the sequence applies consistently regardless of which specific facility a phlebotomist works in. CLSI guidance extends well beyond microbiology into general specimen collection practices including venipuncture order. While provinces may have local scope-of-practice variations, the underlying scientific rationale for the sequence itself is not simply a matter of local choice or a single mandatory colour code.

The correct answer is A (A standard straight needle with a multi-sample holder and 21 gauge needle): for a healthy adult with a large, accessible antecubital vein, a straight needle in a multi-sample holder paired with a standard 21 gauge needle is the appropriate default, offering efficient, low-complication collection of multiple tubes. A syringe or winged set is generally reserved for more difficult, small, or fragile veins rather than being the default choice here. An extremely fine gauge needle is unnecessary for this vein type and risks slower flow or hemolysis with standard vacuum tubes. Equipment selection should match the vein presented rather than defaulting to the equipment used for difficult cases.
  80. The correct answer is D (Sterility must be protected before any other tube is opened): blood culture bottles are collected first specifically to protect their sterility from contamination that could occur if the needle or holder had already contacted other additive tubes. A contaminated culture can produce a false-positive result, leading to inappropriate antibiotic treatment for the patient. This priority is not related to blood volume requirements or stopper colour recognition. Turnaround time for results is a laboratory workflow consideration and does not determine collection order.
  81. The correct answer is C (A 23 gauge winged infusion set suited to small pediatric veins): a small-gauge winged infusion set is well suited to the small, fragile hand veins typical of pediatric patients, offering better manoeuvrability and reduced trauma than a rigid straight needle. A standard adult gauge needle risks excessive vein damage in this population. Pouring blood directly from a syringe into a tube is never appropriate, since it risks hemolysis, contamination, and inaccurate additive ratios. Tourniquet application time should still follow standard safe duration guidelines regardless of patient age or device type.
  82. The correct answer is B (The rules still matter conceptually but carryover risk is not present here): understanding the order of draw remains foundational knowledge for any phlebotomist, but the specific carryover risk the sequence is designed to prevent only becomes relevant when multiple tube types are collected in the same venipuncture. With only a single gold tube ordered, there is no subsequent tube for additive carryover to affect. This does not mean the concept is irrelevant, since the same phlebotomist may need to apply it correctly on the very next patient. A discard tube and a preceding citrate tube are not required simply because a single tube is being collected.
  83. The correct answer is B (It remains an acceptable choice for many patients even with average veins): while a winged infusion set is especially useful for small or fragile veins, it can also be a reasonable choice for average adult veins when the phlebotomist prefers its manoeuvrability, and it connects readily to a standard multi-sample holder for sequential tube collection. It is not restricted exclusively to pediatric patients. Abandoning the holder is unnecessary, since the two devices are designed to work together. A discard tube is only required when the citrate tube specifically is the first tube collected through the winged tubing, not for every draw.
  84. The correct answer is A (Note the deviation and recollect the citrate tube in the correct sequence): documenting the deviation and recollecting the citrate specimen following the correct order of draw is the only response that actually protects the coagulation result once heparin has been drawn ahead of citrate. Heparin carryover genuinely can interfere with citrate-based coagulation testing, so proceeding as if nothing occurred understates the real risk. Manually adding additive to an already-collected tube is never an acceptable correction and would further compromise the specimen's validity. Relabeling tubes to misrepresent the actual collection sequence would falsify laboratory records and is never appropriate.
  85. The correct answer is A (The vein was judged somewhat smaller or more fragile than average): a 22 gauge needle is commonly selected over the standard 21 gauge when a vein appears somewhat smaller or more fragile than typical, since the reduced bore size lowers the risk of vein trauma while still permitting adequate flow for most routine tubes. A smaller-bore needle does not draw blood faster than a larger one, since gauge and flow rate move in opposite directions. There is no blanket provincial mandate specifying a single gauge for all outpatient draws. Gauge selection is a clinical judgment based on vein assessment rather than patient preference alone.
  86. The correct answer is C (Retraining staff on correct citrate tube sequencing and rationale): a cluster of order-of-draw related coagulation rejections points to a knowledge or technique gap among staff at that specific clinic, making targeted retraining on citrate tube sequencing and the reasoning behind it the most direct corrective action. Switching to syringe-only collection would not resolve a sequencing knowledge gap and introduces its own workflow changes. Discontinuing coagulation testing is a disproportionate response to a correctable training issue. Needle gauge is unrelated to additive carryover and would not address the pattern observed.
  87. The correct answer is D (The light blue sodium citrate tube): sodium citrate is positioned second, immediately after blood cultures and before every other additive tube, so no other anticoagulated tube is ever collected ahead of it in the standard sequence. This early position protects the coagulation-sensitive citrate tube from contamination by heparin, EDTA, or fluoride additives collected later. Serum, though not anticoagulated, still follows citrate to avoid clot activator carryover affecting the coagulation result. Heparin and the fluoride tube are both collected well after citrate, never before it.
  88. The correct answer is B (Vein condition, not just patient age, should guide gauge selection): needle gauge should be selected based on the actual condition of the vein being accessed at the time of collection, and a sclerosed or hardened vein from repeated prior draws may require a different, often smaller, gauge than would otherwise be used for a healthy adult vein of the same patient. Simply repeating whatever gauge was used previously ignores changes in vein condition over time. Vein health is directly relevant to gauge choice, not unrelated to it. A larger bore is not automatically the correct response to a sclerosed vein and may worsen trauma.
  89. The correct answer is A (It was not an error, since a straight needle has no air-filled tubing segment): the discard-tube requirement before a first-drawn citrate tube exists specifically to account for the air-filled dead space in winged-set tubing, which does not exist with a straight needle collected directly into a holder. Since there is no equivalent dead space to fill, omitting the discard tube in this scenario is correct technique, not an error. This rule applies regardless of vein size, since it is a mechanical property of the tubing rather than a patient-specific concern. The scenario also specifies citrate was drawn first, not last, so that distractor misstates the facts given.
  90. The correct answer is A (Tubes are advanced onto the rear needle point one at a time in sequence): once the front needle is in the vein, each tube is advanced onto the sheathed rear needle point in turn, allowing the vacuum to draw blood while the front needle remains stationary in the vein. This design allows several tubes to be collected in the correct order of draw from a single venipuncture without repositioning the needle. The holder assembly itself is not replaced between tubes, only the individual tubes are exchanged. The rear needle point stays retracted within its sheath before and after each tube rather than being freely exposed.
  91. The correct answer is B (Contamination risking a false-positive blood culture result): drawing a blood culture after a serum tube risks introducing clot activator or skin surface residue that could contaminate the culture and cause a false-positive result, potentially leading to inappropriate antibiotic treatment. This is precisely why blood cultures are always collected first in the sequence, ahead of any other tube type. A shortened coagulation time relates to citrate contamination, not culture contamination, and was not the risk in this scenario. Underfilled tubes and EDTA-related potassium changes involve different equipment and additive issues unrelated to blood culture sterility.
  92. The correct answer is D (21 gauge for the healthy adult, 22 for the smaller veins, 23 for fragile veins): matching gauge to individual vein condition means selecting a standard 21 gauge for the healthy adult with normal veins, a 22 gauge for the patient with slightly small but otherwise normal veins, and the smaller-bore 23 gauge, often as a winged set, for the geriatric patient with thin fragile veins. Applying the same gauge to every patient ignores real differences in vein condition that affect both safety and specimen quality. Assigning gauge randomly disregards clinical judgment entirely. The relationship between gauge number and bore size means the healthy adult vein would receive the largest bore, not the smallest, under correct reasoning.
  93. The correct answer is C (Yes, because the order of draw applies only to tubes actually collected): the standard order of draw governs the relative sequence of whichever tubes are actually ordered and collected, so skipping tube types that were not requested for a given patient is entirely acceptable and does not violate the rule. Serum does not need to be physically collected simply because it occupies a position in the general reference sequence. EDTA following citrate directly is fine as long as no other tube type that should precede EDTA was actually ordered. A discard tube is only required for winged-set tubing dead space when citrate is first, not for this general skip-ahead situation.
  94. The correct answer is A (Too small a gauge under standard vacuum can still cause hemolysis): choosing an unnecessarily small gauge needle for a patient with normal veins can actually increase the risk of hemolysis, since the narrow bore under standard vacuum pressure can shear red blood cells during collection. Comfort is only one factor, and a smaller needle does not guarantee a painless experience for every patient. Gauge does have a measurable effect on specimen quality through its influence on flow rate and cell integrity. Smaller gauge needles generally fill tubes more slowly, not faster, due to the reduced bore size.
  95. The correct answer is C (Reduced reliance on staff consistently completing cleaning steps): the strongest real argument for switching to disposable tourniquets is that it removes dependence on staff consistently and correctly completing cleaning protocols between every patient, which is a recognized point of potential lapse. Disposable tourniquets function identically to reusable ones in restricting venous return and have no effect on the separate, essential step of palpating a vein before venipuncture. Disposable tourniquets are not inherently more effective at distending veins, and application time recommendations remain the same regardless of tourniquet type. Palpation remains a required, separate step in vein selection no matter which tourniquet type is used.
  96. The correct answer is B (Relying on the tube's own vacuum reduces the risk of hemolysis): allowing the tube's built-in vacuum to draw the correct volume through the transfer device, rather than forcing blood in with manual plunger pressure, reduces mechanical stress on red blood cells and lowers the risk of hemolysis. Manual pressure applied by the phlebotomist can also overfill or underfill the tube, disrupting the intended additive ratio. The transfer device is designed to work with vacuum draw rather than requiring active plunger force. Order of draw is maintained by the sequence in which tubes are attached to the device, not by how forcefully blood is pushed into them.
  97. The correct answer is A (Additive carryover between tubes can distort subsequent test results): the central consequence of violating the order of draw is that additive residue from an earlier tube can carry over into a later one, distorting analytes such as potassium, calcium, or coagulation results depending on which tubes are involved. This does not automatically require a repeated venipuncture, since some tubes in the sequence may remain unaffected depending on which specific violation occurred. Tourniquet timing is a separate technique issue unrelated to tube sequence. Laboratories can often still process unaffected tubes even when one specific result is compromised by a sequencing error.
  98. The correct answer is B (The shorter, more flexible needle setup suits shallower, mobile veins): the winged set's shorter, flexible needle and tubing, combined with a shallower insertion angle, make it a more practical and controlled choice for the shallower, more mobile veins typically found on the back of the hand. A straight needle can technically access hand veins, but doing so is often more difficult and riskier given how shallow and mobile these veins are compared to the antecubital fossa. Order of draw itself does not change based on which site is used for collection. Hand veins generally call for a smaller, not larger, gauge needle given their reduced size and increased fragility.
  99. The correct answer is D (Fluoride and oxalate carried forward could interfere with later tubes' testing): if the fluoride and oxalate tube were collected out of its correct last position, its additives could carry over into the serum, heparin, and EDTA tubes collected afterward, potentially inhibiting enzymatic reactions or distorting cellular morphology in those specimens. This is precisely why it is placed last in the standard sequence rather than earlier. Fluoride and oxalate are not inert with respect to other tube types, making this a genuine carryover risk rather than a negligible one. Not every tube in the sequence would necessarily need to be discarded, since the citrate tube collected before the misplaced fluoride tube would be unaffected by this particular error.
  100. The correct answer is A (All three factors jointly determine specimen quality and patient safety): equipment selection, needle gauge, and order of draw are interconnected because each decision affects the others, such as how tubing dead space in a winged set interacts with citrate positioning, or how gauge choice interacts with vacuum strength to influence hemolysis risk. Together they determine whether a specimen is collected safely and yields accurate results. Order of draw is not the only factor affecting accuracy, since gauge mismatch and equipment choice can independently distort results as well. The grouping reflects genuine clinical interdependence rather than being an arbitrary exam convenience.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (The blood culture bottle to preserve sterility): blood cultures are drawn first in the order of draw because any contamination introduced from skin flora or additive carryover could produce a false-positive culture result. Collecting the sterile bottle before opening any additive tube protects the specimen from contamination during the venipuncture. This priority applies regardless of how many other tubes are ordered on the same requisition. Sterility takes precedence over every other collection consideration in this sequence.
  2. The correct answer is A (Clot activator carryover from the serum tube could interfere with coagulation testing): drawing the citrate tube before the serum tube prevents trace clot activator from the red-top stopper being carried into the coagulation specimen. Even a small amount of contaminating activator can trigger premature clot formation and distort PT and PTT results. This is why sodium citrate sits second in the order of draw, right after blood cultures. Protecting the accuracy of coagulation testing depends on this strict sequencing.
  3. The correct answer is C (The serum or SST tube, to keep clot activator ahead of anticoagulant carryover): the serum tube is drawn third, after citrate, so that any residual clot activator on the needle or in the tubing does not reach a later anticoagulated tube like EDTA or heparin. If clot activator carries into an anticoagulant tube, it can trigger microclot formation that compromises testing on that specimen. Following the fixed sequence protects every downstream tube from this cross-contamination. This step comes immediately after the citrate tube and before the heparin tube.
  4. The correct answer is D (Heparin before EDTA, following the fixed CLSI-based order of draw): the standard sequence places heparin tubes ahead of EDTA tubes because EDTA carryover is a particularly potent source of test interference. Even trace EDTA reaching a later tube can falsely elevate potassium and falsely lower calcium results through its strong chelating action. Following the fixed order, rather than choosing based on convenience, prevents this cross-contamination from ever occurring. This rule holds even when a citrate tube and blood culture are not part of the draw.
  5. The correct answer is B (The grey tube is drawn last, after EDTA, in the fixed sequence): the sodium fluoride and potassium oxalate additive in the grey-top tube is drawn last because oxalate carryover into an earlier tube can interfere with several other assays. Placing it at the end of the sequence, after EDTA, keeps its additive from contaminating any tube collected before it. The chart illustrates this fixed CLSI-based order, which every Canadian phlebotomy program teaches identically. Following the printed sequence removes any guesswork about tube placement during a busy multi-tube draw.
  6. The correct answer is A (EDTA carryover chelated calcium and displaced potassium into the specimen): trace EDTA that migrates into a later-drawn tube binds calcium ions, producing a falsely low calcium result, while the potassium salt form of EDTA can also falsely elevate measured potassium. This pattern is a well-recognized carryover artifact rather than a true physiological finding. It is exactly the scenario the order of draw is designed to prevent by keeping EDTA tubes after heparin tubes. Reviewing collection sequence should be the first step when this specific result pattern appears.
  7. The correct answer is C (Draw a discard tube first to clear air from the tubing): a winged set has a length of flexible tubing that contains air, and if the citrate tube is the first one filled, that air displaces some of the vacuum draw and under-fills the tube. An under-filled citrate tube skews the critical 9:1 blood-to-anticoagulant ratio, invalidating coagulation results. Drawing a non-additive or citrate discard tube first removes the air from the line before the actual specimen is collected. This step is only required when citrate is the first tube drawn through winged tubing.
  8. The correct answer is D (No, because a straight needle has no air-filled tubing segment): the discard-tube rule exists specifically to clear air trapped inside winged-set tubing before it can under-fill a citrate tube. A straight needle inserted directly into a holder has essentially no dead space between the needle and the tube, so no air pocket exists to displace vacuum. Because of this, a citrate tube drawn with a straight needle fills to its correct volume on the first attempt. The discard step is unnecessary and would only waste a tube in this situation.
  9. The correct answer is B (21 gauge, the standard choice for normal adult veins): a 21 gauge needle provides an appropriately sized bore for a healthy, easily accessible adult vein under standard vacuum draw pressure. It balances adequate blood flow with minimal patient discomfort and vessel trauma for typical antecubital venipuncture. Smaller gauges are reserved for fragile or difficult veins, while larger-bore needles are not routinely needed for this patient presentation. This makes 21 gauge the default selection taught in Canadian phlebotomy training for uncomplicated adult draws.
  10. The correct answer is D (The sequence prevents additive carryover from distorting later test results): each tube in the chart contains a different additive, and trace amounts can transfer from one tube to the next through the needle or holder during a multi-tube draw. Following the fixed order keeps additives that could interfere with downstream testing, like clot activator or EDTA, from reaching tubes drawn afterward. Skipping the sequence risks producing inaccurate results even when the venipuncture technique itself was flawless. This is why the chart is a mandatory reference, not an optional guideline.
  11. The correct answer is C (22 gauge, suited to smaller or more fragile adult veins): a 22 gauge needle has a slightly narrower bore than the standard 21 gauge, reducing the vacuum pressure pulling on a smaller vessel while still allowing adequate blood flow for most routine tests. This makes it appropriate for adult veins that are accessible but not robust enough to comfortably tolerate a larger needle. Selecting 21 gauge here would risk unnecessary vein trauma, while an even smaller gauge is typically reserved for pediatric or geriatric patients. Matching gauge to vein condition reduces both hemolysis risk and patient discomfort.
  12. The correct answer is A (The gold SST tube): once the sodium citrate light blue tube has been collected second in the sequence, the next tube drawn is the serum or SST tube, placed third to keep its clot activator ahead of any anticoagulant tubes. The green heparin tube follows in fourth position, then lavender EDTA fifth, and grey fluoride last. The chart lays out this exact progression so a phlebotomist can confirm tube order at a glance during a busy multi-tube draw. Deviating from this sequence risks additive carryover between the remaining tubes.
  13. The correct answer is D (23 gauge winged butterfly set suited to small hand veins): a 23 gauge needle has the narrowest standard bore used in venous collection, making it appropriate for small, fragile hand veins commonly found in geriatric patients. Pairing it with a winged infusion set gives the phlebotomist better control and a lower angle of insertion, both important for shallow or delicate vessels. A larger gauge straight needle risks collapsing or rupturing a vein this small. This combination is a common standard choice for difficult pediatric and geriatric collections alike.
  14. The correct answer is B (Excess vacuum pressure through a too-narrow bore caused hemolysis): when a needle gauge is smaller than necessary for the vein and collection method, the standard vacuum pressure in the tube can pull blood through the narrow bore too forcefully, rupturing red blood cells. This produces visible hemolysis, seen as a pink or reddish tinge in the separated serum or plasma. Choosing an appropriately sized needle for the vein and system in use, rather than defaulting to the smallest option, prevents this artifact. Hemolysis of this kind can compromise several common chemistry results and require redraw.
  15. The correct answer is C (Blood culture, coagulation panel, then CBC): the fixed sequence places blood cultures first to protect sterility, then the sodium citrate coagulation tube second, ahead of any anticoagulant tube that could otherwise interfere with clotting results. The EDTA tube for the CBC is collected later in the sequence, after both blood cultures and citrate. Following this order as shown on the reference chart prevents contamination and additive carryover between these three specimen types. Reordering the sequence risks a compromised culture or an inaccurate coagulation result.
  16. The correct answer is A (A straight needle with an evacuated tube system and holder): for a healthy, easily accessible antecubital vein, the evacuated tube system is the standard and most efficient collection method, allowing multiple tubes to be filled directly in sequence without additional handling steps. A winged set or syringe offers finer control that this straightforward vein does not require. Reserving those alternative methods for genuinely difficult or fragile veins keeps routine collections quick and minimizes unnecessary equipment costs. This is the default approach taught for uncomplicated adult venipuncture in Canada.
  17. The correct answer is D (A winged butterfly set attached to a multi-sample holder): the short, flexible tubing and small-gauge needle of a winged set allow a shallower, more controlled insertion angle suited to a small wrist vein. Attaching it to a multi-sample holder still permits several tubes to be filled in sequence, just as a straight needle would for a larger vein. A syringe would work but offers no advantage here and adds an extra transfer step. This combination is the practical standard for small or difficult veins that still require multiple specimens.
  18. The correct answer is B (A syringe method, controlling draw pressure manually by hand): a syringe lets the phlebotomist regulate the speed and force of the draw directly by hand, rather than relying on the fixed vacuum pressure of an evacuated tube, which can collapse an already fragile or unstable vein. This finer control is especially useful after previous attempts have already compromised the vessel. Once collected, the blood must be transferred into the tube using a proper blood transfer device rather than by removing the needle and pouring. This method suits difficult, small, or rolling veins where standard vacuum draw has already failed.
  19. The correct answer is C (Heparin is drawn before EDTA in the standard sequence): the chart places the green heparin tube in the fourth position and the lavender EDTA tube in the fifth, reflecting how strongly EDTA carryover can interfere with subsequent testing if it were drawn earlier. This ordering protects the heparin tube's results from EDTA's potent chelating and potassium-elevating effects. The relative position of these two tubes holds regardless of whether a citrate tube or blood culture also appears in the draw. Following the chart exactly removes any ambiguity about which anticoagulant tube comes first.
  20. The correct answer is D (Its oxalate additive could contaminate earlier tubes if drawn sooner): the sodium fluoride and potassium oxalate additive in the grey-top tube is placed last in the standard sequence precisely because oxalate carryover can interfere with several other assays if it reaches an earlier-drawn tube. Moving it ahead of EDTA would risk exactly the contamination the fixed order is designed to prevent. Saving a small amount of collection time is never worth compromising the accuracy of every other tube in the draw. The standard sequence should be followed exactly regardless of workflow pressure.
  21. The correct answer is B (A safety-engineered device with a retractable or shielded needle): Canadian occupational health and safety standards require the use of safety-engineered sharps that shield or retract the needle immediately after use, reducing the risk of accidental needlestick injury to healthcare workers. Conventional unshielded needles no longer meet current workplace safety expectations in Canadian clinical settings. This requirement applies across venipuncture devices, including straight needles, winged sets, and syringe needles. Choosing an approved safety device is a standard, non-negotiable step in equipment selection.
  22. The correct answer is A (Discard the holder, since holders are single-patient-use only): even when the needle attached to a multi-sample holder has not yet been used, the holder itself can become contaminated with blood from the previous patient during tube changes. Canadian infection control practice therefore requires that the entire holder be discarded after use on one patient, never reused across patients regardless of needle condition. Attaching a new needle to an already-used holder does not make it safe for reuse. This rule exists specifically to prevent bloodborne pathogen transmission between patients.
  23. The correct answer is C (Reusable tourniquets must be properly cleaned between each patient): unlike disposable single-patient-use tourniquets, which are discarded after one application, a reusable fabric tourniquet must be disinfected according to facility protocol before it touches another patient's skin. Failing to clean it between uses creates a real risk of transmitting microorganisms from one patient to the next. Disposable tourniquets remove this cleaning step entirely by being discarded immediately after use. Both types remain acceptable in Canadian practice provided the appropriate infection control step is actually followed.
  24. The correct answer is B (Attach a blood transfer device and pierce each tube's stopper): a blood transfer device connects to the syringe and allows the vacuum inside each evacuated tube to draw the correct volume automatically, in the proper order of draw, without exposing staff to an open needle or uncapped tube. Removing the needle and pouring blood directly is unsafe and risks both specimen contamination and needlestick exposure. This device is the standard, required accessory whenever a syringe collection needs to be distributed into multiple tubes. It preserves both safety and the correct additive ratio in each tube.
  25. The correct answer is D (Follow the fixed sequence, culture first, regardless of urgency): clinical urgency does not change the fixed order of draw, since contamination or additive carryover introduced by skipping the sequence can compromise every specimen collected afterward, including the urgently needed chemistry result. The fixed order exists precisely so that time pressure never becomes a reason to deviate from the correct sequence. A contaminated blood culture or a compromised coagulation result can cause far greater delay than following the proper order in the first place. Consistency in following the sequence protects both accuracy and patient safety even in a fast-paced setting.
  26. The correct answer is C (No, a discard tube is only required when citrate is drawn first): the discard-tube rule exists specifically to protect the critical blood-to-anticoagulant ratio in a sodium citrate coagulation tube, which is under-filled if air in winged tubing displaces part of the vacuum draw. An EDTA tube does not depend on a precise fill ratio in the same way, so no discard tube is needed before it even when using a winged set. This rule applies narrowly to citrate tubes drawn first through winged tubing, not to every additive tube. Understanding this distinction prevents unnecessary tube waste during routine collections.
  27. The correct answer is A (Trace additive can transfer between tubes and alter later results): even a small residue of additive left on the needle or in the holder as it moves from one tube to the next can be introduced into a subsequently collected tube. Depending on which additive it is, this can chelate ions, activate or inhibit clotting, or otherwise skew the results of that later specimen. This is why the fixed order of draw exists, arranging tubes so the additives least likely to cause harmful interference come first. Understanding this mechanism is fundamental to explaining why sequence matters during collection, not after.
  28. The correct answer is D (Excessive vacuum through the narrow bore may hemolyze the sample): a small gauge needle like 23 gauge has a narrow internal bore, and pairing it with a tube designed for higher standard vacuum can pull blood through that narrow passage with more force than the cells can tolerate. This mismatch is a recognized cause of mechanical hemolysis, distinct from any technique error during the venipuncture itself. Selecting a reduced-draw or pediatric-volume tube for use with a small-gauge winged set avoids this problem. Matching tube vacuum to needle gauge is as important as matching needle gauge to vein size.
  29. The correct answer is B (It confirms the needle is shielded, preventing accidental exposure): a safety-engineered device only protects staff from needlestick injury once its retraction or shielding mechanism has actually activated, and this does not happen automatically in every case if the device is faulty or used incorrectly. Visually confirming engagement before placing the needle in a sharps container closes the loop on this critical safety step. Skipping this confirmation defeats the purpose of using a safety device in the first place. This habit is part of standard Canadian occupational safety practice for every venipuncture.
  30. The correct answer is A (Disposables avoid cleaning steps but add ongoing per-use cost): single-patient-use disposable tourniquets are discarded after each patient, eliminating the need for a cleaning step but adding a recurring supply cost for every collection performed. Reusable fabric tourniquets have a lower per-use cost but require proper disinfection between patients to remain safe, adding a workload and compliance consideration. Both options remain acceptable in Canadian practice as long as the appropriate infection control step for the chosen type is consistently followed. The manager's decision should weigh these tradeoffs against site volume and staffing capacity.
  31. The correct answer is C (Clot activator carryover may cause premature clotting in the tube): if a serum tube containing clot activator is drawn before the citrate tube, trace activator can be carried into the citrate specimen and trigger clotting activity that the citrate anticoagulant was meant to prevent. This compromises the entire coagulation panel, since accurate PT and PTT testing depends on blood remaining properly anticoagulated until analysis. The correct sequence avoids this by collecting citrate before any clot-activator tube. A rushed or reordered draw like this typically requires a redraw rather than a salvage attempt.
  32. The correct answer is D (It bypasses the transfer device, risking exposure and contamination): removing the needle and pouring blood directly into an open tube exposes the phlebotomist to a splash or needlestick hazard and removes the sterile, vacuum-controlled transfer that a proper blood transfer device provides. This shortcut also risks disrupting the correct order of draw when multiple tubes are involved, since pouring does not allow controlled sequential filling. The blood transfer device exists precisely to prevent this unsafe practice while still allowing a syringe-collected specimen to be distributed correctly. This step should never be skipped regardless of time pressure.
  33. The correct answer is A (No, since a straight needle and holder has no air-filled tubing that could under-fill the tube): the discard-tube requirement applies specifically to winged sets, where trapped air in the tubing can under-fill whichever tube is drawn first through it. A straight needle and holder has no meaningful air-filled dead space, so no discard tube is ever needed with this equipment regardless of tube order. Drawing serum before citrate is still an order-of-draw error worth correcting for future collections, since clot activator carryover could affect the citrate result, but it does not on its own create a discard-tube requirement. The citrate tube will still fill correctly without any extra step.
  34. The correct answer is B (The citrate tube first, to keep it ahead of clot activator exposure): even with only two tubes involved, the fixed order of draw still applies, placing sodium citrate ahead of any serum tube so that clot activator carryover cannot interfere with coagulation testing. Clinical urgency for the chemistry result does not override this sequencing requirement. Following the standard order regardless of how many tubes are ordered keeps the process consistent and prevents mistakes during busier draws with more tubes. This habit of always defaulting to the fixed sequence protects specimen integrity in every scenario.
  35. The correct answer is C (The retraction prevents accidental reuse and needlestick exposure): a retractable lancet is engineered to be single-use, with the blade automatically withdrawing into the housing immediately after puncture so it cannot accidentally injure staff or be reused on another patient. This design satisfies Canadian occupational safety requirements for sharps that prevent needlestick and blade injuries during and after use. The trigger platform shown activates the puncture, and the mechanism is permanently disabled after firing. This is why capillary lancets are always discarded immediately after a single stick, regardless of how minor the puncture appeared.
  36. The correct answer is A (Capillary collection using a heel-stick and appropriate lancet): for an infant needing only a small blood volume, capillary collection by heel-stick avoids the technical difficulty and unnecessary trauma of attempting venous access on very small or fragile veins. A properly sized, depth-controlled lancet limits puncture depth to a safe range for a newborn's heel. Venous methods, whether straight needle or a larger holder, are reserved for situations needing larger volumes or when venous testing is specifically required. Matching the collection method to both the required volume and the patient's age is a core equipment selection decision.
  37. The correct answer is D (A 23 gauge winged set drawn with careful syringe control): a small-gauge winged set gives the phlebotomist a shallow, controllable insertion angle appropriate for a toddler's small hand vein, while attaching it to a syringe allows manual control of draw pressure to avoid collapsing the vessel. A large-bore straight needle would be far too traumatic and risky for a vein this size. A lancet is meant for capillary, not venous, collection and would not yield an appropriate venous specimen for this test. This combination is a standard pediatric approach when only a small venous volume is required.
  38. The correct answer is C (The controlled puncture depth suited to fingertip capillary sampling): lancets are manufactured with a fixed, shallow puncture depth appropriate to the site they are intended for, and a fingertip device is calibrated differently than one designed for heel-stick use on a newborn. Selecting the correct depth avoids both an inadequate sample and unnecessary tissue trauma. The retractable blade shown springs back immediately after triggering, ensuring the device cannot be reused. Choosing the right lancet depth for the intended puncture site is part of correct equipment selection, not an interchangeable detail.
  39. The correct answer is B (No, a fresh holder is required for each separate venipuncture): even for the same patient, each individual venipuncture is treated as its own event requiring a fresh, unused needle holder, since the holder can become contaminated with blood residue during the earlier collection. This standard applies regardless of how little time has passed between draws. Attaching a new needle to an already-used holder does not address the contamination risk inside the barrel itself. Using a fresh holder every time is a simple, consistent rule that avoids any ambiguity about reuse.
  40. The correct answer is A (No, because citrate is not the first tube drawn through the tubing): the discard-tube rule for winged sets applies only when the citrate tube is the very first specimen collected, since that is the situation where trapped tubing air would otherwise under-fill it. Once the heparin tube has already been drawn first, the air in the tubing has already been displaced, so the citrate tube collected second will still fill correctly. Drawing heparin before citrate is itself an order-of-draw error worth correcting for future collections, since heparin carryover could affect the citrate result, but it does not change whether a discard tube is needed. Knowing exactly when the discard-tube rule applies prevents both under-filled citrate tubes and unnecessary extra draws.
  41. The correct answer is C (It allows a shallower angle and finer control for fragile veins): the wings and flexible tubing shown let the phlebotomist stabilize the needle at a lower, more controlled insertion angle, which is particularly useful for veins that are prone to rolling or collapsing under a steeper straight-needle approach. This added control reduces the number of failed attempts on a genuinely difficult vein. The device still requires a proper safety mechanism to meet Canadian occupational safety standards, and it does not change the fixed order of draw, which still applies to every tube collected through it. Choosing this device is about vein accessibility, not bypassing standard collection rules.
  42. The correct answer is D (Explain that a larger gauge number means a smaller needle bore): needle gauge follows an inverse relationship, so as the gauge number increases, the actual internal diameter of the needle decreases. A 23 gauge needle therefore has a smaller bore than a 21 gauge needle, making it appropriate for smaller or more fragile veins rather than for faster or higher-volume draws. This inverse relationship is a foundational concept in equipment selection, since choosing the wrong direction could lead to a poorly matched needle for a given vein. Correcting this misunderstanding early prevents equipment selection errors throughout a phlebotomist's career.
  43. The correct answer is A (It sets no sequencing requirement here, since only one tube is drawn): the order of draw exists to manage additive carryover between multiple tubes collected in the same venipuncture, so when only a single tube is ordered, there is no subsequent tube for any carryover to affect. No discard tube or additional sequencing step is needed in this situation. The sequence becomes relevant again as soon as a second tube with a different additive is added to the same draw. Recognizing when the rule does and does not apply prevents unnecessary extra steps during simple single-tube collections.
  44. The correct answer is B (The rear needle pierces the tube stopper, activating vacuum draw): the double-pointed needle shown has a front end in the vein and a rear, sheathed end inside the holder barrel, and pushing each new tube into the holder advances it onto this rear needle, piercing the stopper and allowing the tube's internal vacuum to draw blood. This mechanism is what allows several tubes to be filled in sequence from a single venipuncture without withdrawing the needle. The rear needle stays sheathed between tube changes to prevent blood leakage from the barrel. This design is central to how the evacuated tube system supports correctly sequenced multi-tube draws.
  45. The correct answer is C (Re-prep the site with the proper antiseptic before collecting): blood culture collection requires a more rigorous antiseptic skin preparation than routine alcohol cleaning, because the goal is to minimize any risk of introducing skin flora into a sterile culture bottle. Proceeding with only an alcohol wipe increases the chance of a false-positive contaminated culture result. Re-prepping the site correctly before collection, even if it adds a short delay, protects the reliability of the culture result. This extra step reflects why blood cultures are treated with the highest sterility priority in the entire order of draw.
  46. The correct answer is A (23 gauge winged set, chosen for fragile geriatric veins): the narrow bore of a 23 gauge needle combined with the flexible, low-angle control of a winged set is well suited to thin, fragile geriatric veins that bruise or collapse easily under a standard straight needle. This combination minimizes trauma while still allowing adequate blood flow for most routine tests. A larger gauge straight needle would risk further bruising or vessel damage in a patient with this history. Selecting equipment based on the patient's actual vein condition, rather than defaulting to standard adult equipment, is central to safe collection in this population.
  47. The correct answer is D (The transfer device is attached, then each tube is pierced in order): as shown, the blood transfer device connects to the syringe barrel and allows each evacuated tube to be pierced and filled by its own internal vacuum, in the correct order of draw, without opening the syringe or exposing the needle. This preserves both the standard sequencing rules and staff safety during specimen distribution. Removing the needle to pour blood by hand, as one distractor suggests, bypasses this safety design entirely and should never be done. The transfer device is a required accessory whenever a syringe collection needs to be split into multiple tubes.
  48. The correct answer is B (Carefully activate the shield manually and dispose of it safely): if a safety mechanism does not engage automatically as expected, the phlebotomist must not treat the needle as safe and should carefully trigger the shielding manually, if the device allows it, before immediately placing it in an approved sharps container. Continuing to use the device or setting it down unshielded creates a genuine needlestick risk to staff. This kind of equipment malfunction should also be reported according to facility protocol so the device can be reviewed. Patient safety and specimen collection always come second to addressing an active sharps hazard.
  49. The correct answer is C (To distend the vein adequately without crowding the puncture site): placing the tourniquet roughly 7 to 10 centimetres above the intended site allows enough venous pooling to make the vein more visible and palpable, while still leaving adequate working space to prepare and access the site without the strap interfering. Positioning it too close crowds the collection area, while positioning it too far away reduces its effectiveness at distending the vein. The quick-release flat buckle shown allows the tourniquet to be released promptly once blood flow has been established. Correct tourniquet placement is a simple but important step in successful vein selection.
  50. The correct answer is A (Order of draw protects specimen accuracy, equipment protects patient safety and comfort): the order of draw exists to prevent additive carryover between tubes, directly protecting the accuracy of laboratory results, while equipment selection, including needle gauge and device type, focuses on matching the collection method to the patient's vein condition to minimize trauma, hemolysis risk, and discomfort. These two concepts work together during every venipuncture but address different goals rather than one determining the other. A phlebotomist must apply both correctly at the same time to perform a safe and accurate collection. Understanding this distinction helps a new hire prioritize both considerations rather than treating them as a single rule.
  51. The correct answer is A (Stop and resolve the mismatch with registration before proceeding): a mismatch between the two identifiers means identity is not yet confirmed and drawing now risks a misidentified specimen. Registration or the ordering department needs to correct or verify the record before collection continues. Proceeding on a partial match, even a very close one, is exactly the scenario the two-identifier rule exists to prevent. Patient safety takes priority over turnaround time in this situation.
  52. The correct answer is A (Compare the wristband information directly against the requisition): the wristband is the only identifier tied physically to the patient, since beds and posted names can be assigned to a different person after a transfer or discharge. Room and bed numbers are never acceptable as identifiers on their own. Checking the wristband against the requisition confirms full legal name and a unique identifier match before any collection begins. This step cannot be skipped even when other visual cues appear consistent.
  53. The patient must instead be asked an open question to state their own name and date of birth. This active method exposes a mismatch that a passive yes-or-no question would hide. Relying on agreement alone is a recognized identification error.
  54.   55. Refusing to engage at all would be dismissive and unnecessary. This balance protects the patient from receiving inaccurate or premature clinical interpretation.
  56. The correct answer is D (Respect the refusal, document it, and notify the ordering provider): a competent adult patient has an absolute right to refuse any procedure, including a routine blood draw, and no explanation is required from them. The phlebotomist must document the refusal accurately and notify the ordering provider so follow up can occur. A family member cannot override a competent patient's own decision. Proceeding despite a clear refusal is not permitted under any circumstance.
  57. Removing the parent or rushing without explanation tends to increase fear and resistance. Promising no pain at all is dishonest and undermines trust if discomfort does occur. Honest, calm, age-appropriate communication supports cooperation and reduces distress.
  58. Communication should be adjusted rather than skipped, since clarity supports both consent and cooperation. Using identical technique and timing as for a younger adult ignores real physiological differences. Slowing down and adapting communication is part of appropriate care, not an inefficiency.
  59.   60. Using a qualified interpreter protects both patient understanding and the validity of consent.
  61. Refusing outright or demanding justification disregards the patient's dignity and autonomy. Treating the request as mere inconvenience overlooks its genuine importance to the patient. Reasonable accommodation reflects respectful, culturally sensitive practice.
  62. Pausing to reassess, involve nursing or security as appropriate, or reschedule is the safer and more respectful path. Simply switching arms ignores the underlying distress driving the reaction. The encounter should also be documented regardless of the outcome.
  63. The correct answer is B (Photo identification and a witnessed, sealed chain-of-custody process): forensic and legal specimen collections require a more rigorous identification and handling process than routine clinical draws, including photo identification, witness signatures, and sealed evidence packaging to preserve legal defensibility. Ordinary verbal identification alone is insufficient for these purposes. Chain-of-custody documentation tracks the specimen from collection through final testing. This heightened process protects the integrity of results that may be used in legal proceedings.
  64. Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Explain the collection process and refer result questions to the ordering provider): phlebotomists can explain the collection procedure but must not interpret results or discuss possible diagnoses, since that falls outside their scope of practice. Offering a general interpretation or checking prior chart trends risks giving inaccurate or premature medical information. Reassuring the patient with a guess about normal results is also inappropriate and could be false. Questions about clinical meaning belong with the ordering provider.
  2. The correct answer is C (Say most people feel a brief pinch and describe what to expect): honest, measured reassurance builds trust without making a promise that cannot be guaranteed. Claiming there will be no pain at all is dishonest and can damage trust if the patient does feel discomfort. Avoiding the question or exaggerating other patients' pain does not help the patient prepare emotionally. Clear, realistic communication is part of good therapeutic communication in phlebotomy.
  3. The correct answer is C (Close the curtain fully and speak quietly during the interaction): patients have a right to privacy and dignity during any procedure, so the phlebotomist should proactively close the curtain fully and keep the conversation at a low volume rather than waiting for the patient to raise a concern. A partially closed curtain still allows visual and audible exposure to others nearby. Rushing the procedure does not address the underlying privacy issue and could compromise proper technique. Protecting privacy is the phlebotomist's responsibility, not something the patient should have to request.
  4. The correct answer is A (Explain the procedure in simple age-appropriate terms and involve the caregiver): pediatric collection benefits from simple, honest explanations suited to the child's developmental level plus involving the caregiver for comfort and distraction. Removing the caregiver often increases fear rather than reducing it. Restraining a child without explanation is distressing and should only be used as a last resort with proper technique and support. Age-appropriate communication and caregiver involvement improve cooperation and specimen quality.
  5. The correct answer is B (Allow extra time and use gentler technique appropriate to fragile skin and veins): geriatric patients often have fragile skin and veins along with possible hearing or cognitive considerations, so extra time and gentler handling reduce the risk of bruising, skin tearing, or vein damage. A larger needle or an overly tight tourniquet would increase trauma risk rather than help. Rushing increases the chance of a failed draw or injury. Adjusting pace and technique to the patient's physical condition is part of safe, individualized care.
  6. The correct answer is D (Speak calmly, repeat simple explanations, and involve a caregiver or substitute decision-maker): patients with cognitive impairment respond best to calm, simple, and repeated explanations, along with support from a caregiver or substitute decision-maker who can help with consent and reassurance. A single long explanation is unlikely to be retained and may increase confusion. Ignoring the patient and speaking only to the caregiver disregards the patient's dignity. Patience and repetition are appropriate accommodations for this population.
  7. The correct answer is A (Arrange a qualified medical interpreter rather than relying on a family member): a qualified medical interpreter should be used, especially for consent-related conversations, because family members, including minors, may mistranslate, omit information, or feel uncomfortable relaying sensitive medical content. Relying on a grandchild for convenience risks a genuine misunderstanding about the procedure. Skipping assent or using gestures alone does not provide the patient a real opportunity to understand and agree. Accurate communication protects both patient rights and specimen validity.
  8. The correct answer is C (Reasonably accommodate the request if staffing allows without delaying urgent care): cultural and religious considerations around modesty and phlebotomist gender should be reasonably accommodated when the test is not urgent and staffing permits, reflecting respect for patient dignity. Outright refusal without attempting accommodation disregards a legitimate patient concern. Accommodation should not compromise timely care for urgent testing needs. Balancing patient preference with practical staffing is the appropriate approach.
  9.   79. The correct answer is A (Explain honestly that the first sample could not be used and a new one is needed): honest, clear communication about why a repeat draw is necessary respects the patient's right to understand their care and maintains trust. Avoiding the topic or requesting another draw silently leaves the patient confused and can reduce their willingness to cooperate. Claiming the second draw is unrelated when it is not is misleading. Blaming equipment without confirming the actual cause risks giving inaccurate information to the patient.
  80. The correct answer is D (Respect the patient's wish and avoid discussing any results in front of the relative): a patient's explicit request about who may hear their health information must be respected, and in any case a phlebotomist does not discuss results at all, which reinforces the appropriate boundary here. Sharing information despite the patient's clear request violates confidentiality regardless of how supportive the relative appears. Ordering the relative out of the room is not the phlebotomist's role to decide. Discussing results with the relative without the patient's knowledge is a serious breach of patient confidentiality.
  81. The correct answer is A (The median cubital vein, which is usually large, well anchored, and less painful to puncture): This vein is generally larger and better anchored than nearby vessels, so it moves less during needle insertion and causes less discomfort for the patient. It also sits further from the brachial artery and median nerve than the basilic vein, which lowers the risk of nerve or artery injury. Canadian phlebotomy training programs consistently teach it as the first site to evaluate before considering alternatives.
  82. The correct answer is C (Arteries carry blood under high pressure, raising the risk of uncontrolled bleeding and spasm): Because arterial blood is pumped under much higher pressure than venous blood, an accidental or intentional arterial puncture can cause forceful bleeding and arterial spasm that is harder to control. Arterial blood gas sampling is instead performed by staff who have received specialized additional training for that specific procedure. Veins remain the standard target because they are safer to access and still provide an accurate picture of whole-body blood composition.
  83. The correct answer is B (A venous valve, a one-way structure that helps prevent backward blood flow): Veins contain valves along their length that keep blood moving toward the heart and prevent it from pooling backward under low pressure. When a tourniquet increases pressure below the valve, it can bulge and become visible or palpable as a small lump. Recognizing a valve is important because puncturing directly over one can reduce blood flow into the tube and cause a failed or difficult draw.
  84. The correct answer is D (Capillaries, the microscopic vessels used deliberately for small-volume heel stick collection): Capillaries are the tiny vessels where exchange between blood and tissue occurs, and a heel or finger stick deliberately punctures this capillary bed to obtain only a small volume. This approach avoids the risks of venipuncture on a newborn's fragile, small veins while still yielding enough sample for screening. The same principle applies to many point-of-care glucose tests performed on adults.
  85. The correct answer is B (It sits centrally, is well anchored, and is generally furthest from major nerves and arteries): The median cubital vein typically connects the cephalic and basilic veins across the antecubital fossa and is anchored well enough to resist rolling. Its central position also keeps it comparatively further from the brachial artery and median nerve than the basilic vein. These features are why it is usually the first site evaluated during a routine blood draw.
  86. The correct answer is A (The cephalic vein, which in some patients rolls more or sits slightly deeper): The cephalic vein runs along the lateral, thumb side of the antecubital fossa and serves as a reasonable second option when the median cubital vein cannot be used. In some patients it can roll more easily under the skin or sit at a slightly deeper level, which can make anchoring and puncture technique more important. It remains a safer alternative to the basilic vein because of its distance from major nerves and arteries.
  87. The correct answer is D (Extra care is needed because the basilic vein lies closer to the brachial artery and median nerve): The basilic vein sits on the medial side of the antecubital fossa, nearer to the brachial artery and median nerve than the median cubital or cephalic veins. This proximity means that probing blindly or redirecting the needle increases the risk of nerve or arterial injury. As a result, it is used only when no better site is available, and the phlebotomist must withdraw and reassess rather than continue searching under the skin.
  88. The correct answer is C (The M-pattern replaces the single median cubital vein with two separate connecting veins instead of one): the H-pattern describes the median cubital vein connecting the cephalic and basilic veins in a relatively straight horizontal line, resembling the letter H. The M-pattern is a common variation where a median cephalic vein and a median basilic vein together replace that single connection, creating an M-shaped layout instead. Recognizing both patterns helps a phlebotomist correctly identify vein anatomy across different patients rather than assuming one fixed layout.
  89. The correct answer is A (Arterial puncture carries higher pressure-related risks and requires specialized additional training): Arteries carry blood under much higher pressure than veins, so a puncture can cause forceful bleeding and requires firm, prolonged pressure afterward to achieve hemostasis safely. Because of this elevated risk, arterial blood gas collection is restricted to personnel who have completed dedicated training beyond standard phlebotomy certification. This division of responsibility protects both the patient and the person performing the puncture.
  90. The correct answer is B (That even a generally safer site still requires careful technique near deeper structures): Although the median cubital vein is usually the preferred site because it sits away from the brachial artery and median nerve, those structures still exist nearby in the antecubital fossa. Labeling them reminds the phlebotomist that correct needle angle and depth remain important even at a comparatively low-risk site. This reinforces that safe technique, not just site selection, protects the patient from injury.
  91. The correct answer is D (Serum is plasma after clotting factors have been consumed during the clotting process): Plasma is the liquid portion of blood that still contains clotting factors such as fibrinogen, while serum is obtained after a sample is allowed to clot, which consumes most of those factors. Because fibrinogen is essentially absent from serum, the two fluids differ in composition even though they come from the same original sample. This distinction determines which tube type a laboratory requests for a given test.
  92. The correct answer is C (It chelates calcium, preventing the clotting cascade so the cells never clot): EDTA works by binding calcium, a cofactor the clotting cascade requires to proceed, which stops the sample from clotting inside the tube. Because clotting never occurs, centrifuging the sample separates plasma, which still contains clotting factors, from the cellular components. This makes EDTA tubes appropriate for tests such as a complete blood count that require intact cells or true plasma.
  93. The correct answer is B (The sample must be allowed to clot fully before centrifugation separates the layers): Tubes without an anticoagulant, such as a red-top or serum separator tube, rely on the blood's natural clotting process to form a clot before the sample is spun down. Centrifuging too early can produce an incompletely separated or unusable sample because clotting has not finished. Once clotting is complete, centrifugation separates the serum from the clotted cellular material for testing.
  94. The correct answer is A (Fibrinogen is consumed during clotting, so it is essentially absent from serum): Fibrinogen is a clotting factor that converts to fibrin during the clotting process to form the clot itself, so by the time serum is separated from that clot, fibrinogen has largely been used up. Because plasma samples are collected with an anticoagulant that prevents clotting, fibrinogen remains present and measurable in plasma. This is why coagulation-related tests typically require a plasma sample rather than serum.
  95. The correct answer is C (It may roll more easily or sit slightly deeper in some patients than the median cubital vein): The highlighted vein in this diagram is the cephalic vein, running along the lateral side of the antecubital fossa near the thumb. In some patients it can roll under the skin more readily or sit at a slightly greater depth, which can affect anchoring and needle angle during puncture. It remains a reasonable second choice when the median cubital vein is unavailable, since it stays further from major nerves and arteries than the basilic vein.
  96. The correct answer is D (Erythrocytes carry oxygen throughout the body bound to hemoglobin within the cell): Red blood cells, or erythrocytes, contain hemoglobin, a protein that binds oxygen in the lungs and releases it to tissues throughout the body. When the number of these cells is reduced, less oxygen can be delivered to tissues, which commonly produces fatigue and shortness of breath. This link between erythrocyte count and oxygen delivery is why a complete blood count is often ordered when these symptoms are reported.
  97. The correct answer is A (Leukocytes, the white blood cells that function as the body's immune defenders): Leukocytes, commonly known as white blood cells, are the cellular component of blood responsible for identifying and responding to infection or foreign material. A differential count breaks this population down into subtypes to help clinicians determine what kind of immune response is occurring. This is distinct from erythrocytes and thrombocytes, which serve oxygen transport and clotting functions instead.
  98. The correct answer is B (Thrombocytes, the platelets involved in forming the initial plug that seals an injury): Thrombocytes, or platelets, gather at the site of a vessel injury and form a plug as the first step in stopping bleeding. When platelet numbers are low, this initial plug cannot form effectively, which allows bleeding to continue longer and bruising to occur more easily. This is a separate mechanism from the clotting factor cascade, though both contribute to overall hemostasis.
  99.
