The correct answer is D (Primary hemostasis, the rapid formation of a platelet plug at the injury site): Primary hemostasis is the body's first response to vessel injury, in which platelets adhere to the damaged site and aggregate to form a temporary plug. This happens quickly, before the slower clotting factor cascade of secondary hemostasis has fully converged on fibrin formation. Together, these two phases work in sequence to stop bleeding effectively.
  100. The correct answer is C (The cephalic vein generally remains a lower-risk option since it sits further from these structures): Even though the brachial artery and median nerve are labeled in this diagram for anatomical reference, the cephalic vein's lateral position keeps it further from those structures than the medially located basilic vein. This relative distance is a key reason the cephalic vein is preferred over the basilic vein when the median cubital vein is unavailable. The labeling serves as a reminder of nearby anatomy rather than a warning against using this particular vein.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Secondary hemostasis, the clotting factor cascade that converges on fibrin formation): Secondary hemostasis involves a cascade of clotting factors that activate one another in sequence, ultimately converting fibrinogen into fibrin strands that reinforce the initial platelet plug. This produces a stronger, more stable clot than the platelet plug alone could provide. Understanding this sequence explains why certain collection tubes contain additives that interfere with specific steps of this cascade.
  2. The correct answer is A (Citrate chelates calcium, which the clotting cascade requires to proceed): Like EDTA, sodium citrate works by binding calcium ions, which several steps of the clotting factor cascade depend on to function. Because the exact ratio of citrate to blood volume matters for coagulation testing, these tubes must be filled to their designated volume to keep the anticoagulant concentration accurate. This precise binding effect allows the laboratory to later add calcium back in a controlled way to study clotting time.
  3. The correct answer is D (The puncture depth must avoid the underlying bone beneath the epidermis and dermis): A capillary puncture only needs to pass through the epidermis into the dermis, where the capillary bed lies close to the surface, without going deep enough to strike the underlying bone. This is especially important in infants, whose bone is closer to the skin surface than in adults. Warming the site beforehand can improve blood flow but does not change the depth limit required for safety.
  4. The correct answer is C (A capillary heel stick, which yields the small sample volume needed for screening): Newborn screening programs typically use a capillary heel stick because it provides enough blood to fill the required test card spots without the risks associated with venipuncture on a newborn's small, fragile veins. This method targets the capillary bed rather than a specific named vein or artery. The small sample volume obtained is sufficient for the metabolic and genetic conditions included in standard screening panels.
  5. The correct answer is A (It should be used only when no better option exists, given its proximity to these structures): The basilic vein sits on the medial side of the antecubital fossa, closer to the brachial artery and median nerve than the median cubital or cephalic veins. Because probing near these structures raises the risk of nerve or arterial injury, this vein is reserved for situations where the other two options are not usable. The diagram's labeling of the nearby artery and nerve reinforces why extra caution is required here.
  6. The correct answer is B (A capillary fingerstick, providing a small sample suitable for a handheld glucose meter): Point-of-care glucose testing is designed around small capillary samples obtained through a fingerstick, which a handheld meter can analyze within seconds. This avoids the need for a full venipuncture and central laboratory processing when a rapid result is what the clinical situation requires. Capillary sampling is deliberately chosen here for its speed and small required volume, not as a substitute for every type of blood test.
  7. The correct answer is D (The affected arm should generally be avoided because of lymphedema and infection risk): When lymph nodes have been removed as part of a mastectomy, the affected limb has reduced lymphatic drainage, which raises the risk of lymphedema and infection following a needle puncture. Whenever possible, the phlebotomist should use the unaffected arm instead to avoid these complications. This precaution is a standard part of pre-draw site assessment in Canadian phlebotomy practice.
  8. The correct answer is C (Avoid the limb with the fistula and select a suitable site on the other arm): A dialysis fistula or graft is a surgically created vascular access point that must be protected from unrelated punctures, since damage or infection there can compromise the patient's ability to receive dialysis. The phlebotomist should select an alternate site on the opposite limb whenever one is available. This precaution takes priority even if the fistula limb otherwise appears to have accessible veins.
  9. The correct answer is B (Select a vein on the opposite arm rather than drawing near the active IV line): Drawing blood from a limb with an active intravenous line risks contaminating the sample with the infused fluid, which can significantly alter test results such as electrolyte or glucose values. Whenever another suitable site is available, the phlebotomist should choose the opposite arm instead. If no alternate site exists, specific protocols exist for drawing below or after temporarily stopping the IV, but the opposite arm remains the preferred choice.
  10. The correct answer is A (Withdraw the needle and reassess rather than continuing to probe near these structures): Because the basilic vein lies close to the brachial artery and median nerve, blind probing or repeated redirection after a failed attempt significantly raises the risk of nerve or arterial injury. The recommended approach is to withdraw the needle, reassess the site, and consider an alternate vein or arm rather than continuing to search under the skin. This principle applies specifically because of the deep structures shown near this particular vein.
  11. The correct answer is C (Redirection near the basilic vein risks contacting the nearby median nerve): The basilic vein's proximity to the median nerve means that moving the needle in different directions while searching under the skin increases the chance of contacting or injuring the nerve. Nerve injury can cause lasting pain, numbness, or reduced function, which is a serious complication for a routine procedure. This risk is specifically elevated at this site compared with the median cubital vein, which sits further from major nerves.
  12. The correct answer is D (Withdraw the needle slightly and reassess rather than probing extensively under the skin): Extensive probing under the skin increases the risk of nerve, artery, or tissue injury, particularly near sites like the basilic vein where deeper structures lie close by. The safer approach is to withdraw the needle slightly, reassess the chosen site, and consider a different vein or arm if a second reasonable attempt also fails. This principle protects the patient even when it means the draw takes longer or requires a second attempt.
  13. The correct answer is A (Plasma, which contains water, proteins, clotting factors, and electrolytes): Plasma is the liquid portion of blood that carries dissolved substances including water, proteins such as albumin, clotting factors, and electrolytes throughout the circulatory system. It surrounds and suspends the cellular components, such as red and white blood cells and platelets, before any separation occurs. Understanding plasma's composition helps explain why certain tests require plasma rather than serum or whole blood.
  14. The correct answer is B (Plasma, the liquid portion of blood that carries dissolved electrolytes and proteins): Plasma is the fluid medium in which electrolytes such as sodium, potassium, and chloride are dissolved and transported throughout the circulatory system. This is also where plasma proteins, water, and clotting factors are found, distinguishing plasma from the cellular components of blood. An electrolyte panel therefore typically requires a plasma or serum sample rather than testing on whole blood cells.
  15. The correct answer is D (Both structures are generally further from the median cubital vein than from the basilic vein): Although the brachial artery and median nerve are labeled in this diagram for anatomical context, the median cubital vein's more central position keeps it comparatively further from these structures than the medially located basilic vein. This relative distance is a major reason the median cubital vein is preferred as the first site to assess. The labeling still reminds the phlebotomist to maintain proper needle angle and depth even at this generally safer site.
  16. The correct answer is C (Heparin prevents clotting, so plasma can be obtained without waiting for a clot to form): Heparin works differently from citrate or EDTA but still prevents the clotting cascade from completing, allowing the sample to be centrifuged for plasma immediately rather than waiting for natural clotting. This makes heparin tubes useful when a faster turnaround is clinically necessary, such as for a stat electrolyte panel. Serum tubes, by contrast, require time for clotting to finish before they can be processed.
  17. The correct answer is A (Plasma, because clotting factors remain present rather than being consumed by clotting): Because plasma is collected using an anticoagulant that prevents the clotting cascade from occurring, its clotting factors remain intact and measurable. Serum, in contrast, is produced after the clotting process has consumed most of these factors to form the clot itself. This is why coagulation-related research and diagnostic testing typically specify a plasma sample rather than serum.
  18. The correct answer is B (The accessory median vein, associated with the M-pattern venous arrangement): The accessory median vein is an additional vessel that, when present, branches the usual H-shaped connection between the cephalic and basilic veins into an M-shaped layout. This variation is not present in every patient but is common enough that phlebotomists are trained to recognize it. Understanding this variation helps avoid confusion when mapping veins that do not match the more typical H-pattern.
  19. The correct answer is C (The H-pattern, the most common configuration of these three connecting veins): The H-pattern describes the arrangement where the median cubital vein forms a relatively straight horizontal connection between the cephalic and basilic veins, resembling the letter H. It is generally considered the most common venous configuration encountered in the antecubital fossa. Recognizing it helps a phlebotomist quickly identify the median cubital vein as the likely first site to assess.
  20. The correct answer is D (The cephalic vein remains a reasonable next choice given its distance from these structures): Even with the brachial artery and median nerve labeled in the diagram for anatomical orientation, the cephalic vein's lateral position keeps it comparatively distant from these structures. This supports its role as a reasonable second option when the median cubital vein cannot be used, ahead of the basilic vein, which sits closer to these same structures. The labeling is meant to orient the phlebotomist, not to suggest this site should be avoided.
  21. The correct answer is B (Toward the heart, preventing blood from flowing backward under low pressure): Venous valves are structured to allow blood to move in one direction, toward the heart, while preventing it from flowing backward due to the relatively low pressure within the venous system. This is different from arteries, which do not require valves because they carry blood under high pressure directly from the heart. Recognizing a valve's location matters because puncturing directly over one can reduce blood flow during a draw.
  22. The correct answer is A (Arteries have a thicker, more muscular wall compared with the thinner walls of veins): Arteries are built with a thicker, more muscular wall that allows them to withstand the higher pressure generated as the heart pumps blood outward. Veins have thinner walls and rely instead on one-way valves and surrounding muscle contraction to help move blood back toward the heart under much lower pressure. This structural difference is a major reason veins are safer to puncture for routine blood collection.
  23. The correct answer is D (Capillaries, the microscopic vessels specialized for exchange between blood and tissue): Capillaries are extremely thin-walled microscopic vessels that connect arterioles to venules, and their structure allows oxygen, carbon dioxide, nutrients, and waste products to pass between blood and surrounding tissue. Arteries and veins are built for transport over distance rather than exchange, with thicker walls unsuited to this function. This is also why capillaries are deliberately targeted for certain small-volume collection methods.
  24. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin, the protein within erythrocytes that binds and transports oxygen): Hemoglobin is the oxygen-carrying protein found within red blood cells, and it is responsible for binding oxygen in the lungs and releasing it to tissues throughout the body. When hemoglobin levels are low, tissues receive less oxygen, which commonly produces symptoms such as fatigue and pallor. This is distinct from clotting-related proteins such as fibrinogen or thrombin, which serve an entirely different function.
  25. The correct answer is A (Only when the median cubital and cephalic veins are not usable options): Even though this particular view does not label the brachial artery and median nerve, the basilic vein's medial position keeps it closer to those structures than the other two commonly used veins. For this reason, it is generally reserved as a site to consider only after the median cubital and cephalic veins have been ruled out. This principle holds regardless of whether a given diagram happens to display the nearby deep structures.
  26. The correct answer is B (An increased immune response as leukocytes mobilize to fight the infection): White blood cells, or leukocytes, are the immune cells responsible for detecting and responding to pathogens such as bacteria, and their numbers commonly rise when the body mounts an active immune response. This elevation is a useful clinical clue that helps a physician confirm and monitor an infection. It is distinct from erythrocyte or platelet changes, which reflect oxygen transport and clotting function rather than immune activity.
  27. The correct answer is D (Exposure of underlying vessel wall tissue caused by the injury itself): When a vessel wall is damaged, underlying tissue that is normally hidden becomes exposed to circulating blood, and this exposure triggers platelets to adhere to the site and to each other, forming the initial plug of primary hemostasis. This rapid response happens before the slower clotting factor cascade of secondary hemostasis reinforces the plug with fibrin. The trigger is specifically the physical injury and tissue exposure, not changes in white cell count or hemoglobin.
  28. The correct answer is C (Fibrin, the insoluble protein mesh that the clotting cascade ultimately converges to form): The clotting factor cascade of secondary hemostasis ultimately converts fibrinogen into fibrin, an insoluble protein that forms a mesh reinforcing the initial platelet plug. This final step stabilizes the clot and allows it to hold until the underlying vessel injury has healed. Understanding this convergence point explains why anticoagulants such as citrate and EDTA, which interfere earlier in the cascade, are able to prevent clot formation entirely.
  29. The correct answer is A (Reduced lymphatic drainage in the affected limb raises the risk of lymphedema and infection): When lymph nodes are removed, the affected limb loses some of its normal lymphatic drainage capacity, which increases the risk of fluid buildup, known as lymphedema, and infection following a needle puncture. This physiological concern, not a difference in blood composition or vein size, is the reason the limb should generally be avoided. Selecting the unaffected arm whenever possible protects the patient from these preventable complications.
  30. The correct answer is B (The median cubital vein, identifiable by its central, well anchored position): Among the three commonly encountered antecubital veins, the median cubital vein sits centrally and is generally the most anchored, connecting the cephalic and basilic veins across the fossa. Its central position and stability are why it is typically identified and assessed first for a routine venipuncture. The cephalic and basilic veins, by contrast, sit laterally and medially, respectively, at the outer edges of the antecubital fossa.
  31. The correct answer is B (Veins sit closer to the skin surface and carry blood under much lower pressure): Lower pressure means controlled, predictable bleeding after puncture and a reduced risk of hematoma or arterial spasm. Veins are also more superficial and accessible than the deeper arteries, making them safer and easier targets for routine collection. Arterial puncture is reserved for arterial blood gas testing performed only by specially trained personnel because of the higher risk involved.
  32. The correct answer is A (The median cubital vein, because it offers a large, stable, low-risk site): The median cubital vein is the preferred first choice because it tends to be large and well anchored, making it less prone to rolling during puncture. It also sits farther from the brachial artery and median nerve than the basilic vein, lowering the risk of accidental injury. These properties make it both more comfortable for the patient and easier for the phlebotomist to access reliably.
  33. The correct answer is C (This is the cephalic vein, a reasonable second choice when needed): The cephalic vein runs along the lateral, thumb-side aspect of the forearm and is a standard secondary option when the median cubital vein is unavailable. It is still a legitimate venipuncture site in adults of any age, not a structure limited to children. Its main drawback compared to the median cubital vein is that it can roll more easily and sometimes sits slightly deeper.
  34. The correct answer is D (Palpate carefully since the cephalic vein can roll and sit deeper): Careful palpation lets the phlebotomist assess depth and mobility before inserting the needle, which matters because the cephalic vein tends to roll more and can sit slightly deeper than the median cubital vein in some patients. Skipping palpation increases the chance of a missed or repeated puncture attempt. Anchoring the vein firmly with the thumb during insertion remains an essential technique regardless of which vein is chosen.
  35. The correct answer is B (The medial site lies closer to the brachial artery and median nerve): The basilic vein, located on the medial side of the antecubital fossa, sits closer to the brachial artery and median nerve than the median cubital or cephalic veins. This proximity raises the risk of nerve injury or arterial puncture if the needle is inserted incorrectly or redirected during the draw. Because of this elevated risk, the basilic vein is generally reserved as a last resort and handled with extra caution.
  36. The correct answer is D (The H-pattern, the most common venous configuration described in training): The H-pattern describes a simple, relatively straight connection formed by the median cubital vein linking the cephalic and basilic veins across the antecubital fossa. It is the most frequently taught and encountered configuration in phlebotomy training because of how commonly it appears in patients. Recognizing it helps a phlebotomist quickly identify the median cubital vein as the preferred central site.
  37. The correct answer is A (The M-pattern, created by an accessory median vein branching the area): The M-pattern arises when an accessory median vein branches between the cephalic and basilic veins, creating an additional connecting vessel not present in the simpler H-pattern. This variation does not indicate any abnormality preventing venipuncture and does not by itself say anything about arterial depth. Recognizing the pattern simply helps the phlebotomist choose the most accessible branch for puncture.
  38. The correct answer is C (The brachial artery and the median nerve, both deep structures): The basilic vein sits in close proximity to the brachial artery and the median nerve, which is why it carries higher risk than the median cubital or cephalic veins. Injury to either structure can occur if a needle is inserted too deeply or redirected blindly while searching for the vein. This is why the basilic vein is used only when no better alternative exists and always with extra caution.
  39. The correct answer is B (Withdraw slightly and reassess rather than redirecting the needle blindly): Because the basilic vein lies close to the brachial artery and median nerve, blind probing or redirecting the needle risks striking one of these structures. Withdrawing slightly and reassessing the site allows the phlebotomist to relocate the vein by palpation before making another careful attempt. Continuing to probe deeper or rotate the needle instead increases the chance of nerve injury or arterial puncture.
  40. The correct answer is C (Withdraw and reassess site or vein rather than digging repeatedly): This principle protects the patient from unnecessary tissue trauma and reduces the risk of nerve or arterial injury near a difficult site such as the basilic vein. Digging or probing repeatedly in the same tract raises complication risk without improving the odds of successful collection. Reassessing the vein by sight and palpation before a second attempt is the safer and more professional approach.
  41. The correct answer is A (EDTA prevents clotting, so clotting factors remain present in the fluid): EDTA is an anticoagulant, meaning the sample never clots inside the tube, and the resulting liquid portion after centrifugation is plasma rather than serum. Because clotting never occurs, clotting factors including fibrinogen remain intact in the plasma. This differs from serum, which is only obtained after clotting has been allowed to consume those factors.
  42. The correct answer is D (Serum lacks fibrinogen because clotting factors were consumed forming the clot): Allowing a sample to clot fully before centrifugation means clotting factors, including fibrinogen, are used up forming the clot itself. The resulting supernatant, serum, is essentially free of fibrinogen, unlike plasma from an anticoagulated tube where clotting never occurs. Neither a higher red cell count nor a citrate additive is what distinguishes serum from plasma in this comparison.
  43. The correct answer is C (It is large, well anchored, and situated away from major nerves): The median cubital vein is preferred first because it tends to be large, well anchored to surrounding tissue, and positioned farther from the brachial artery and median nerve than the basilic vein. Being well anchored also means it rolls less than the cephalic vein, making needle placement more predictable. Veins never carry oxygenated blood, since that is a distinguishing feature of arteries instead.
  44. The correct answer is B (Fibrinogen, because clotting factors were consumed forming the clot): Fibrinogen is a clotting factor that converts into fibrin as a clot forms, so once a sample has clotted, essentially no fibrinogen remains in the resulting serum. Plasma, by contrast, is obtained from an anticoagulated tube where clotting never occurs, so fibrinogen and other clotting factors remain present. Both erythrocytes and dissolved electrolytes are handled differently and are not the substance distinguishing plasma from serum in this comparison.
  45. The correct answer is D (Erythrocytes, the red blood cells that carry oxygen via hemoglobin): Erythrocytes contain hemoglobin, the iron-containing protein that binds and transports oxygen throughout the body, so a low hemoglobin value paired with a reduced red cell count points directly to this component. Leukocytes and thrombocytes serve immune and clotting functions respectively rather than oxygen transport. Plasma proteins carry other substances but do not carry oxygen the way hemoglobin within erythrocytes does.
  46. The correct answer is A (Leukocytes, the white blood cells that function as immune cells): A white blood cell differential measures the various types of leukocytes, which serve as the body's immune cells and respond to infection or inflammation. Erythrocytes and thrombocytes serve oxygen transport and clotting roles respectively, not immune defence. Fibrinogen is a plasma protein involved in clotting and is unrelated to the white cell differential ordered in this scenario.
  47. The correct answer is B (Thrombocytes, the platelets involved in forming an initial clot): Thrombocytes, also called platelets, are the cell fragments that adhere to an injured vessel wall and to one another to form the initial plug during primary hemostasis. A low platelet count directly impairs this early clotting response, raising the risk of excessive or prolonged bleeding. Erythrocytes and leukocytes serve oxygen transport and immune functions and are not the primary component involved in initial clot formation.
  48. The correct answer is C (This lateral vein generally carries lower risk than the medial basilic vein): The cephalic vein sits farther from the brachial artery and median nerve than the basilic vein does, giving it a lower overall risk profile even though deep structures still exist somewhere in the region. It remains a usable second-choice site when the median cubital vein cannot be located. It is not positioned directly over the median nerve, which lies closer to the medial basilic site instead.
  49. The correct answer is D (Primary hemostasis, the stage involving initial platelet plug formation): Primary hemostasis describes the initial response to vessel injury, in which platelets adhere to the damaged wall and to each other to form a temporary plug. This stage occurs before the clotting factor cascade of secondary hemostasis converges on stable fibrin formation. Fibrinolysis and vasodilation describe separate processes that are not the initial platelet-based response being described here.
  50. The correct answer is A (Secondary hemostasis, the clotting factor cascade converging on fibrin): Secondary hemostasis describes the sequential activation of clotting factors that ultimately produces fibrin, reinforcing and stabilizing the initial platelet plug formed during primary hemostasis. Primary hemostasis refers to the earlier platelet adhesion stage rather than the factor cascade itself. Hemolysis and anticoagulation describe unrelated processes involving red cell breakdown and clot prevention respectively.
  51. The correct answer is C (Citrate chelates calcium, a factor the clotting cascade requires to proceed): Sodium citrate binds and removes free calcium from the sample, and because calcium is a required cofactor for several steps of the clotting cascade, secondary hemostasis cannot proceed to completion. This is precisely why citrate tubes are used for coagulation studies, since the anticoagulant effect can later be reversed by adding calcium back in the laboratory. Citrate does not destroy platelets or denature clotting proteins through heat, since its mechanism is calcium binding.
  52. The correct answer is D (The depth of the underlying bone, which the lancet must not strike): Controlling puncture depth on a newborn's heel is essential because striking the underlying calcaneus bone can lead to a serious complication such as osteomyelitis. This concern is specific to how thin tissue layers are in infants, making bone proximity far more relevant than in adult venipuncture. Neither skin colour nor subcutaneous fat thickness alone accounts for this specific safety consideration.
  53. The correct answer is B (This vein typically sits a safe distance above those deeper structures): The median cubital vein is generally preferred because, although the brachial artery and median nerve do lie in the same general region, this vein typically sits a comfortable distance above them. This margin reduces the risk of accidental arterial or nerve injury compared with the basilic vein, which lies closer to those same deep structures. The deep structures shown are anatomically real and relevant, which is exactly why site selection and technique matter.
  54. The correct answer is A (Lymphedema risk, since impaired lymphatic drainage affects that limb long-term): Removal of lymph nodes during mastectomy surgery can permanently impair lymphatic drainage in the arm on that side, increasing susceptibility to swelling and infection, a condition known as lymphedema. Venipuncture on that limb can worsen swelling or introduce infection risk, so an alternative site is generally chosen instead. The arm's circulatory system and venous blood content are unaffected by lymph node removal, so the concern relates specifically to lymphatic drainage.
  55. The correct answer is D (Puncture risks damaging the fistula and disrupting its critical blood flow): A dialysis fistula is a surgically created connection that provides high blood flow needed for hemodialysis, and venipuncture near it risks damaging the fistula or disrupting that essential flow. This risk exists regardless of vein size, sensation, or clotting behaviour, so a different limb is generally selected instead whenever possible. Protecting the fistula is a priority because it is often the patient's only reliable dialysis access site.
  56. The correct answer is C (Drawing near the IV can dilute or contaminate the sample collected): Blood drawn from a limb receiving intravenous fluids can be diluted or contaminated by the infused fluid, producing inaccurate laboratory results. This is the primary reason a different arm is chosen whenever another accessible site is available. IV fluid does not alter blood type, and veins remain palpable and usable again after the IV line is eventually removed.
  57. The correct answer is B (Capillary puncture suits small-volume testing and limited accessible veins in infants): Newborn screening requires only a small blood volume, and infants have limited accessible veins compared with adults, making capillary puncture the practical and appropriate collection method. Venipuncture on an infant is possible in some circumstances but is not the routine choice for this type of screening. Capillary blood shares the same basic cellular components as venous blood, and provincial screening programs do not require arterial sampling.
  58. The correct answer is B (It lies closer to the brachial artery and median nerve than others): Even when a diagram does not visually label the deep structures nearby, a phlebotomist must still remember that the basilic vein lies closer to the brachial artery and median nerve than the median cubital or cephalic veins. This knowledge should be applied regardless of what a particular illustration shows, since it reflects consistent anatomical fact. The basilic vein is generally assessed last, not first, and it is a venipuncture site rather than a capillary collection location.
  59. The correct answer is D (Specially trained personnel, given the elevated risk of arterial puncture): Arterial blood gas collection carries greater risk than routine venipuncture because arteries operate under much higher pressure and can bleed or spasm uncontrollably if not managed correctly. For this reason, this specific type of collection is reserved for personnel who have received specialized training beyond standard phlebotomy certification. It is not performed by family members, patients themselves, or newly certified phlebotomists without that additional training.
  60. The correct answer is C (Capillaries, the microscopic vessels where gas and nutrient exchange occurs): Capillaries are the microscopic vessels where oxygen, carbon dioxide, and nutrients actually pass between the bloodstream and surrounding tissue, connecting the arterial and venous systems. Arteries and veins primarily transport blood over larger distances rather than performing this exchange function directly. Lymphatic vessels play a separate role in draining excess interstitial fluid and are not part of the blood exchange process described here.
  61. The correct answer is B (Arteries, which carry blood away from the heart under high pressure): Arteries carry blood away from the heart under relatively high pressure, matching the student's description of blood being propelled outward before reaching capillary beds. Veins instead return blood toward the heart under low pressure, which is the opposite direction described in the scenario. Lymphatic vessels and capillaries serve different roles and are not the vessel type the student is describing here.
  62. The correct answer is A (One-way valves along the vein that prevent blood from flowing backward): Veins contain one-way valves along their length that prevent blood from flowing backward as it returns toward the heart under relatively low pressure. This structural feature distinguishes veins from arteries, which lack these valves and instead rely on high pressure generated directly by the heart. Veins do not generate their own independent pumping action, and they are not directly connected to the lymphatic system in this way.
  63. The correct answer is D (Withdraw and reassess, since deep structures nearby increase redirection risk): Whenever deep structures such as an artery or nerve lie near a venipuncture site, a missed attempt should prompt the phlebotomist to withdraw and reassess rather than redirect the needle blindly. Blind redirection increases the chance of striking the very structures the diagram highlights as nearby. Inserting a second needle beside the first or pushing deeper without reassessing both increase risk unnecessarily rather than reducing it.
  64. The correct answer is C (Median cubital first, then cephalic, then basilic as a last resort): The median cubital vein is preferred first because it is large, well anchored, and situated away from major nerves and arteries. The cephalic vein serves as a reasonable second choice, while the basilic vein is reserved for last because of its proximity to the brachial artery and median nerve. This order is taught consistently because it minimizes risk while maximizing successful collection on the first attempt.
  65. The correct answer is B (Water containing proteins, clotting factors, and electrolytes among other substances): The liquid portion of blood, known as plasma before clotting or serum after clotting, is mostly water containing dissolved proteins, clotting factors, and electrolytes among other substances. This liquid portion is distinct from the cellular components such as red cells, white cells, and platelets that separate out during centrifugation. It is not pure water, and healthy red cells remain intact rather than rupturing to release hemoglobin into the fluid.
  66. The correct answer is A (Plasma, since anticoagulant prevents the clotting process from ever occurring): Plasma comes from an anticoagulated tube in which clotting is prevented from occurring at all, so clotting factors remain present and unconsumed within the sample. Serum, by contrast, is what remains after clotting has occurred, meaning those same factors have already been consumed forming the clot. Clotting factors circulate within the plasma itself rather than existing only inside red blood cells.
  67. The correct answer is C (EDTA chelates calcium ions, a cofactor the clotting cascade requires): EDTA works by chelating, or binding, calcium ions within the sample, and since calcium is a required cofactor for several steps of the clotting cascade, the cascade cannot proceed to completion. This mechanism preserves the sample as anticoagulated whole blood suitable for cell counting and morphology review. EDTA does not lyse red cells, raise sample temperature, or physically coat platelets, since its effect works specifically through calcium binding.
  68. The correct answer is D (Palpate carefully and use extra caution given the nearby artery and nerve): When the basilic vein is the only remaining option, careful palpation and extra caution are essential because of its proximity to the brachial artery and median nerve shown in the diagram. This site is not equally risky to the other two veins, which is precisely why it is used only as a last resort. Anchoring the vein firmly and inserting at a controlled, shallow angle remain important techniques rather than steps to skip.
  69. The correct answer is A (Plasma, since heparin prevents clotting and cells never form a clot): Heparin is an anticoagulant that prevents the sample from clotting, meaning centrifugation separates the sample into cells and plasma rather than cells and serum. This makes heparin tubes useful for tests requiring a faster turnaround, since no clotting time needs to elapse before processing. Heparin has a real anticoagulant effect rather than no effect, and it does not convert plasma into lymphatic fluid.
  70. The correct answer is B (Hemoglobin, the iron-containing protein inside red blood cells binding oxygen): Hemoglobin is the iron-containing protein found inside erythrocytes that binds oxygen and carries it throughout the body, making it the molecule directly relevant to an elevated hemoglobin and hematocrit finding. Fibrinogen and thrombin are involved in clotting rather than oxygen transport, and albumin functions mainly to maintain plasma oncotic pressure and transport other substances. None of those other proteins carry oxygen the way hemoglobin does.
  71. The correct answer is D (Leukocytes, the white blood cells that mount the immune response): Leukocytes are the white blood cells broadly responsible for the body's immune defence, and their numbers typically increase in response to an acute bacterial infection. Erythrocytes and thrombocytes serve oxygen transport and clotting roles respectively rather than mounting an immune response. Plasma proteins alone do not account for the cellular immune response being investigated in this patient's bloodwork.
  72. The correct answer is C (Platelet plug formation, the primary hemostasis response to injury): Platelet plug formation, the process defining primary hemostasis, involves platelets adhering to the injured vessel wall and to each other to quickly control bleeding at a minor wound. This rapid cellular response explains why small cuts often stop bleeding within minutes without further intervention. Fibrinolysis describes clot breakdown rather than formation, and lymphatic drainage is unrelated to this initial bleeding control process.
  73. The correct answer is B (This vein sits well above and away from those deeper structures): Although the brachial artery and median nerve exist in the same general region of the antecubital fossa, the median cubital vein typically sits well above and away from these deeper structures, giving it the greatest safety margin among the three commonly assessed veins. This margin is smaller for the basilic vein, which lies closer to both structures. The deep structures shown are anatomically located in the same arm, and this vein is routinely and safely punctured during standard venipuncture.
  74. The correct answer is A (The epidermis and dermis, since capillaries lie within these layers): Capillary blood used for a fingerstick is found within the epidermis and upper dermis, so controlling puncture depth relative to these two skin layers ensures the lancet reaches capillary blood without penetrating too deeply. Muscle and bone lie well beneath these layers and are not the target for capillary collection. The dermis does contain blood vessels, which is precisely why it matters for this particular calculation of safe depth.
  75. The correct answer is D (Striking the underlying bone risks a serious complication called osteomyelitis): Because an infant's heel tissue is thin, striking the underlying calcaneus bone with a lancet can lead to osteomyelitis, a serious bone infection requiring further medical treatment. This is why puncture sites and depth are carefully selected to avoid the central curved area of the heel. The heel bone does have associated nerve endings, and bone contact does not reliably falsify glucose results or permanently change skin colour.
  76. The correct answer is B (Impaired lymphatic drainage increases infection and swelling risk after puncture): Reduced lymphatic drainage capacity means the affected limb is less able to manage fluid and immune responses, so venipuncture there carries an increased risk of swelling and infection. This is why an alternative arm is preferred whenever possible for a patient with this surgical history. Veins remain present and usable, blood clotting is unaffected, and blood type never changes as a result of lymph node removal.
  77. The correct answer is C (Puncture or pressure near the graft can disrupt its critical blood flow): An arteriovenous graft depends on a specific high-flow pathway to function for dialysis, and puncture or applied pressure such as a tourniquet near the graft can disrupt that critical flow. Protecting the graft matters because it may serve as the patient's essential and sometimes limited dialysis access site. The graft does not contain leftover dialysis fluid, cause complete numbness, or convert venous blood into arterial blood.
  78. The correct answer is A (The cephalic vein, located along the lateral, thumb-side aspect of the arm): The cephalic vein runs along the lateral, thumb-side aspect of the forearm, matching the position described in the diagram, and serves as a reasonable secondary venipuncture site. The basilic vein instead lies on the medial aspect of the arm, the opposite side from what is described here. Neither the brachial artery nor the median nerve is a venipuncture target, since both are deep structures rather than surface veins.
  79. The correct answer is D (Secondary hemostasis, the clotting cascade that forms stabilizing fibrin): Because the initial platelet plug forms normally, primary hemostasis is intact, pointing instead to an impairment in secondary hemostasis, the clotting factor cascade responsible for producing stabilizing fibrin. A genetic clotting factor deficiency prevents this cascade from completing, leaving the platelet plug without the reinforcement it needs to fully stop bleeding. Vasodilation and fibrinolysis describe separate processes unrelated to the clotting factor deficiency described in this scenario.
  80. The correct answer is C (Larger, well anchored veins farther from nerves and arteries are preferred): This principle combines the three anatomical factors that determine standard site preference: vein size and stability affect ease of puncture, anchoring reduces rolling, and distance from the brachial artery and median nerve reduces complication risk. This is why the median cubital vein is preferred first, the cephalic vein second, and the basilic vein last. Vein colour and patient preference alone do not account for the anatomical reasoning behind this taught order, and the three veins do not carry identical risk.
  81. The correct answer is B (Electrolytes such as sodium, potassium, and chloride reported on a chemistry panel): "lytes" is informal shorthand for an electrolyte panel, which typically requires a lithium heparin or serum separator tube depending on the ordering facility's protocol. Recognizing the abbreviation prevents drawing the wrong tube colour and having to recollect from the patient later. Electrolyte results guide urgent clinical decisions, so a mislabelled or wrong-tube specimen can meaningfully delay treatment. Confirming unfamiliar shorthand with a supervisor is standard practice whenever it is not spelled out in full on the requisition.
  82. The correct answer is A (Blood, as in tests analyzing whole blood, plasma, or its cellular components): the combining form "hemat/o" (like "hem/o") refers to blood, so a hematology panel evaluates blood cells and related components circulating in a patient's bloodstream. Recognizing this root helps a phlebotomist connect an unfamiliar test name to the correct tube type and draw order for the shift. Word roots form the foundation of medical terminology, letting staff decode new terms without memorizing every possible test name individually. Consistent root recognition meaningfully reduces requisition-reading errors during a fast-paced collection round.
  83. The correct answer is D (Hematoma, describing blood pooling and collecting beneath the surrounding skin): a hematoma forms when blood leaks from the vein into surrounding tissue during or after venipuncture, producing visible swelling and discoloration at the puncture site. Applying direct pressure immediately after needle withdrawal is the standard technique for preventing this collection complication from developing. Documenting the correct term ensures the patient's chart accurately reflects what occurred rather than a vaguer or medically incorrect description. Precise terminology also helps the next phlebotomist choose a different, unaffected site for any future draws.
  84. The correct answer is C (Glycated hemoglobin reflecting average blood glucose control over roughly the previous three months): HbA1c measures the percentage of hemoglobin that has become glycated by circulating glucose, giving clinicians a long-term picture of blood sugar control. Because it reflects an average across the entire red blood cell lifespan, fasting status does not meaningfully affect the final result reported. Understanding this abbreviation lets a phlebotomist correctly reassure a non-fasting patient that the draw can safely proceed exactly as ordered. Confusing HbA1c with a point-in-time glucose test could otherwise cause an unnecessary and avoidable rescheduling.
  85. The correct answer is B (Culture and sensitivity, requiring strict sterile technique to identify organisms and drug response): C&S orders identify infecting organisms and their antibiotic sensitivity, and collecting the specimen before antibiotics begin is essential because treatment can quickly suppress bacterial growth. Sterile technique prevents contaminating skin flora organisms from producing a misleading or falsely positive culture result later. Recognizing this abbreviation before the antibiotic dose is administered protects the diagnostic value of the entire specimen. A delayed or post-antibiotic C&S draw may need to be repeated later, adding both cost and delay for the patient.
  86. The correct answer is D (Thromb/o means clot and -penia means deficiency, so it means a low platelet count): the combining form "thromb/o" refers to a clot or clotting cell, and the suffix "-penia" means an abnormal deficiency, together describing an unusually low platelet count. Patients with thrombocytopenia are at increased bleeding risk, so a phlebotomist should apply extra pressure and monitor the site closely afterward. Breaking a term into its component parts is a reliable way to decode unfamiliar vocabulary appearing on any requisition. Misreading this term as a white cell abnormality could lead to overlooking an important bleeding precaution.
  87. The correct answer is A (The depression located on the inner surface of the elbow joint): the antecubital fossa is the preferred venipuncture site because the median cubital, cephalic, and basilic veins are typically large, accessible, and well anchored there. Using the precise anatomical term in documentation avoids ambiguity about exactly which site was actually accessed during a draw. Consistent terminology also helps future phlebotomists select an untouched site if the antecubital area has been used repeatedly before. Plain-language substitutes like "arm crease" are less precise and can be interpreted differently by different chart readers.
  88. The correct answer is C (Blood urea nitrogen, reflecting kidney function and overall protein metabolism): BUN measures urea nitrogen in the blood, a waste product normally cleared by the kidneys, and elevated results can indicate impaired renal function or dehydration. Confusing BUN with a coagulation test could lead a phlebotomist to give the patient inaccurate information about why the test was ordered. Correcting the trainee before consent protects the informed-consent process, since patients deserve an accurate explanation of what is being tested. BUN is very commonly paired with creatinine as part of a basic metabolic panel.
  89. The correct answer is A (Closer to the point of attachment or the trunk of the body): "proximal" describes a location nearer to where a limb attaches to the torso, and is the direct opposite of "distal." Choosing a venipuncture site correctly relative to an existing IV, meaning below it toward the hand rather than above it, prevents drawing blood diluted by infusing fluids. Understanding proximal versus distal is essential for reading chart notes accurately and selecting a genuinely safe collection site. Misinterpreting the direction could result in a specimen that gives falsely altered laboratory values.
  90. The correct answer is B (Different pathways of the overall blood clotting cascade working together): partial thromboplastin time (PTT) evaluates the intrinsic clotting pathway while international normalized ratio (INR) standardizes prothrombin time results reflecting the extrinsic pathway, and both are drawn in a light blue citrate tube. Ordering them together gives a fuller picture of coagulation status when starting or closely monitoring anticoagulant therapy. Recognizing both abbreviations helps the phlebotomist select the correct additive and fill the tube to its proper draw volume. An underfilled citrate tube can produce falsely prolonged results because of its fixed anticoagulant-to-blood ratio.
  91. The correct answer is C (A brief, sudden loss of consciousness commonly described as fainting): syncope describes a temporary loss of consciousness typically caused by a sudden drop in blood flow to the brain, which can occur during or after venipuncture from a vasovagal response. Recognizing and correctly documenting syncope allows staff to apply appropriate protocols, such as lowering the patient's head or having them lie down before standing. An inaccurate term in the incident report could mislead follow-up care or misrepresent the true severity of what occurred. Prompt, correctly labelled documentation also supports quality tracking of collection-related complications over time.
  92. The correct answer is D (They are easily misread for each other, risking a dosing or scheduling error): "QD" (daily) and "QOD" (every other day) look similar in handwriting and have historically been confused, which is why ISMP Canada lists them among abbreviations to avoid on orders and documentation. Contacting the ordering unit rather than guessing protects the patient from a scheduling or specimen-timing error based on a misread instruction. This same principle applies broadly to phlebotomy documentation, where clarity consistently prevents avoidable pre-analytical mistakes. Writing out the full term whenever in doubt is always safer than simply assuming the intended meaning.
  93. The correct answer is D (A liver function test panel that must be collected without any delay): "LFTs" abbreviates liver function tests, typically including enzymes such as ALT and AST, and "STAT" instructs the phlebotomist to collect and transport the specimen without any delay whatsoever. Prioritizing STAT orders ahead of routine draws is a core scheduling skill, since delayed critical results can affect urgent clinical decisions quickly. Misreading the panel abbreviation could send the wrong specimen type to the lab, wasting the urgency signalled by the STAT flag. Confirming both the panel and priority level at a glance keeps the collection workflow efficient during a busy shift.
  94. The correct answer is A (A sclerosed vein, hardened over time from repeated prior venipunctures): a sclerosed vein has become fibrotic and rigid from scar tissue after repeated venipunctures, making it a poor and often painful choice for future draws. Recognizing this finding by palpation before insertion allows the phlebotomist to select a healthier alternate site instead of forcing the attempt. Attempting to draw from a sclerosed vein often results in a failed or difficult collection and unnecessary patient discomfort. Accurate documentation of the finding also helps future staff members avoid attempting the same compromised site again.
  95. The correct answer is B (TSH tests thyroid function and NPO means nothing by mouth is not required here): TSH stands for thyroid stimulating hormone, a screening test for thyroid disorders, and NPO means "nothing by mouth," so the requisition is clarifying that fasting is not needed for this particular order. Understanding both abbreviations together prevents the phlebotomist from unnecessarily instructing the patient to fast before arriving for the appointment. Some tests genuinely require NPO status while others, like TSH, generally do not require any special preparation at all. Giving a patient incorrect fasting instructions can cause confusion or an entirely avoidable delay.
  96. The correct answer is C (Inadequate mixing, allowing clot formation despite the presence of the anticoagulant additive): EDTA prevents clotting only if the tube is gently inverted the recommended number of times immediately after filling, and a clotted specimen usually means mixing was skipped or was insufficient. This distinguishes the finding from hemolysis, which involves ruptured cells rather than an actual clot forming inside the tube. Documenting the correct cause helps identify a technique issue that genuinely needs correction rather than attributing the rejection elsewhere. A clotted CBC specimen cannot be accurately processed and requires a full recollection directly from the patient.
  97. The correct answer is A (How quickly red blood cells settle within a vertical tube, reflecting inflammation): erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) measures how far red blood cells fall in a calibrated tube over one hour, with a faster rate generally indicating an inflammatory process occurring somewhere in the body. It functions as a nonspecific screening test rather than a diagnosis of any single specific condition. Explaining this clearly helps the patient understand why the physician ordered it alongside other requested panels. ESR specimens are typically collected in a lavender or black-top tube depending on the facility's particular method.
  98. The correct answer is D (Fasting blood sugar, a glucose measurement requiring an overnight or extended fast): FBS measures glucose after a period without food, typically eight to twelve hours, since eating raises blood sugar and would produce a falsely elevated result. Confirming fasting compliance before collection ensures the reported result accurately reflects the patient's true baseline glucose control level. If a patient has not fasted, the phlebotomist should note this on the requisition or reschedule according to facility policy immediately. FBS remains a routine screening and ongoing monitoring test used widely for diabetes management.
  99. The correct answer is B (A thrombosed vein, internally blocked by a clot that prevents proper refill): a thrombosed vein contains a clot that obstructs normal blood flow, so even though the vessel may feel soft on palpation, it fails to refill because blood simply cannot pass through the blockage. Recognizing this distinction from a healthy vein prevents a phlebotomist from attempting venipuncture on a site unlikely to yield a usable specimen. Choosing terminology carefully in documentation and training helps build an accurate mental model of proper vein assessment. A thrombosed vein should always be avoided in favour of an alternate, patent site nearby.
  100. The correct answer is C (Closer to the midline of the body when compared to another structure): "medial" describes a position nearer to the body's imaginary centre line, and is the direct opposite of "lateral." Understanding this term helps a phlebotomist correctly locate a described site relative to another landmark, such as the basilic vein sitting medial to the cephalic vein in the antecubital area. Directional terms like medial and lateral appear frequently throughout chart notes and are essential for accurate site selection. Misinterpreting the direction could lead to searching the wrong side of the arm entirely for a documented landmark.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Quantity not sufficient, meaning too little specimen volume was actually collected): QNS indicates the tube did not contain enough blood for the ordered test, often because a citrate tube was underfilled relative to its required additive ratio. This is especially critical for coagulation testing, where volume accuracy directly affects the anticoagulant-to-blood ratio and overall test validity. Recognizing this abbreviation helps a phlebotomist understand why a specimen was rejected and prompts a proper recollection using the correct draw volume. Repeated QNS rejections also signal a need to review technique carefully, such as vein selection or tube-filling order.
  2. The correct answer is C (AMA, meaning the patient left the facility against medical advice given): documenting AMA clearly records that the patient chose to leave before the ordered service was completed despite being informed of the clinical recommendation. Accurate use of this abbreviation protects both the patient's record and the facility by clearly showing the sequence of events that occurred. Confusing AMA with an unrelated abbreviation like STAT or NPO would misrepresent what actually happened during the visit. Facility policy typically requires a supervisor to be notified whenever an AMA departure occurs during specimen collection.
  3. The correct answer is A (Surgical puncture performed to withdraw fluid from a body cavity or joint): the suffix "-centesis" specifically means a surgical puncture performed to remove fluid, distinguishing it clearly from "-itis," which instead means inflammation. Arthrocentesis, for example, describes puncturing a joint space to withdraw synovial fluid, a procedure related to but distinct from standard venipuncture. Correctly distinguishing suffixes prevents a phlebotomist from confusing procedure names when reviewing orders or assisting another department. Building fluency with common suffixes like -centesis, -itis, and -osis makes decoding new or unfamiliar terms far more efficient overall.
  4. The correct answer is D (Edema, describing an excess accumulation of fluid within the surrounding tissue): edema refers to swelling caused by excess fluid trapped within body tissue, and pressing the skin leaves a temporary indent commonly known as pitting. Drawing blood from an edematous limb is generally avoided because fluid can dilute the specimen and alter test results substantially. Choosing an alternate, unaffected site protects both specimen accuracy and overall patient comfort during the draw. Correctly naming the finding also ensures the medical team is aware of a condition that may need further evaluation.
  5. The correct answer is C (The lowest drug concentration, drawn immediately before the next scheduled dose): a trough level captures the medication's lowest circulating concentration, measured right before the next scheduled dose is administered to the patient. Drawing at the wrong time, such as after the dose has already been given, would produce a result that does not reflect the intended trough. Understanding this timing terminology is essential for medications with a narrow therapeutic range, where both trough and peak levels guide safe dosing decisions. Coordinating the draw time with nursing staff ensures the specimen is collected at the clinically meaningful moment.
  6. The correct answer is B (Hemoconcentration, an artificial rise in large molecules and cellular elements): leaving a tourniquet applied longer than about one minute causes plasma fluid to shift out of the vessel while larger molecules and cells stay behind, artificially raising their measured concentration. This pre-analytical error can affect results for tests like potassium, protein, or hematocrit collected at the same time. Explaining the correct term helps a new hire understand exactly why timing matters, not just that it simply does. Releasing the tourniquet promptly once blood flow begins helps keep the specimen representative of the patient's true physiological state.
  7. The correct answer is D (Hypo- means below or under normal, so hypoglycemia describes low blood sugar): the prefix "hypo-" means below or beneath normal levels, and is the direct opposite of "hyper-," which means above or excessive. Correcting the handout prevents patients from misunderstanding a condition that can cause symptoms like shakiness, confusion, or fainting when blood sugar drops too low. Distinguishing hypo- from hyper- is a foundational skill, since many medical terms pair these prefixes with the same root to describe opposite conditions. Accurate patient education materials support safer patient self-management between scheduled clinic visits.
  8. The correct answer is A (Tachycardia, describing a heart rate that beats noticeably faster than normal): the prefix "tachy-" means fast, and combined with "cardia," referring to the heart, the term describes a heart rate above the expected resting range. Noting this finding before the draw helps the phlebotomist watch for signs of anxiety or a vasovagal reaction that could affect the collection. Using the precise term rather than a vague description communicates the observation clearly to other clinical staff reviewing the chart. Distinguishing tachycardia from bradycardia, its slow-rate opposite, relies on correctly matching the prefix to its true meaning.
  9. The correct answer is D (Beneath or under, referring specifically to the subcutaneous tissue layer): the prefix "sub-" means beneath or under, so "subcutaneous" describes the layer of tissue just below the dermis, distinct from the vein itself accessed during standard venipuncture. Recognizing this prefix helps a phlebotomist understand instructions or terminology referring to injections or sampling from that specific tissue layer rather than a blood vessel. Confusing subcutaneous with intravenous terminology could lead to selecting an entirely wrong procedure or piece of equipment. Prefixes like sub-, intra-, and peri- each describe a distinct spatial relationship that genuinely matters for accurate technique.
  10. The correct answer is C (The differential is reported as a component of a complete blood count, not a separate draw): a complete blood count with differential (CBC with diff) is collected from the same lavender-top EDTA tube, with the differential breaking down the types of white blood cells present as part of one analysis. Understanding that "diff" is not a separate specimen prevents the trainee from unnecessarily drawing an extra tube and causing avoidable patient discomfort. Recognizing which abbreviations describe components of one test versus genuinely separate orders is a key requisition-reading skill. Overdrawing tubes also wastes supplies and adds an unnecessary needle stick for the patient.
  11. The correct answer is B (Phlebitis, describing inflammation developing along the wall of a vein): the suffix "-itis" means inflammation, and combined with "phleb/o," meaning vein, the term describes an inflamed vein presenting with redness, warmth, and tenderness along its course. Choosing a different site for venipuncture is important when phlebitis is present, since the affected vein may be fragile or genuinely painful to access. Documenting the correct term ensures the finding is clearly communicated to the wider care team for appropriate follow-up. Confusing phlebitis with an unrelated term could delay recognition of a site that needs clinical attention.
  12. The correct answer is A (Sodium citrate, in a light blue top tube filled precisely to the draw line): PTT and other coagulation studies require a sodium citrate tube filled precisely to its designated volume, since the ratio of anticoagulant to blood must remain consistent for accurate clotting-time results. Underfilling this tube produces an unreliable, falsely prolonged result and typically leads to outright specimen rejection at the lab. Recognizing that PTT pairs specifically with citrate, not EDTA or heparin, is essential coagulation-testing knowledge. A patient already on heparin therapy makes accurate PTT results especially important for guiding future dose adjustments.
  13. The correct answer is C (Within a structure, describing a location that sits directly inside the artery itself): the prefix "intra-" means within or inside, so "intra-arterial" describes a location directly inside an artery, distinct from "peri-arterial," which would describe tissue surrounding the vessel. This distinction matters clinically because arterial punctures carry different risks and require different technique than routine venipuncture. Explaining the prefix clearly helps the student avoid confusing terms that sound similar but describe very different anatomical relationships. Mastering directional and locational prefixes like intra-, peri-, and sub- builds a strong foundation for reading clinical documentation.
  14. The correct answer is D (The red blood cells ruptured, releasing their internal contents into the surrounding plasma): hemolysis occurs when red blood cells break open, releasing intracellular substances like potassium into the plasma and skewing results for several chemistry tests. Common causes include using a needle that is too small, drawing too forcefully, or shaking the tube instead of gently inverting it. Explaining this clearly to the patient helps them understand why a second draw is necessary rather than assuming an error reflects poorly on the earlier result. Preventing hemolysis on the repeat draw relies on proper technique and correct tube handling throughout the process.
  15. The correct answer is A (Toward the back surface of the body or a specific limb): "posterior" describes a position toward the back of the body, and is the direct opposite of "anterior," which describes the front. Applying this correctly means the wound sits on the back of the arm relative to the antecubital fossa, which is located on the front. Understanding anterior and posterior terminology helps a phlebotomist accurately picture a described location without needing to physically inspect the site first. These directional terms are used consistently across chart notes regardless of which department originally authored them.
  16. The correct answer is B (Collect and deliver the specimen to the lab without any delay whatsoever): STAT means the test must be collected, transported, and processed immediately because the result is needed urgently for clinical decision-making. In an emergency department context, a STAT electrolyte panel could directly affect treatment decisions within just minutes. Recognizing this abbreviation lets a phlebotomist correctly reprioritize their workflow ahead of routine, non-urgent orders waiting elsewhere. Delaying a STAT specimen defeats the entire purpose of the order and could contribute to a delay in critical patient care.
  17. The correct answer is C (The suffix -itis means inflammation, so hepatitis describes liver inflammation directly): the suffix "-itis" consistently means inflammation across medical terminology, so combined with "hepat/o," meaning liver, hepatitis describes an inflamed liver rather than a tumour or an enzyme deficiency. This same suffix pattern appears in terms like phlebitis and arthritis, all describing inflammation of a specific structure. Correcting a study partner's misunderstanding before an exam helps ensure both people carry accurate terminology into real clinical documentation. Knowing this suffix pattern reliably also speeds up decoding of any new inflammation-related term encountered later on.
  18. The correct answer is D (A standardized ratio calculated from prothrombin time results for consistent monitoring across labs): the international normalized ratio converts a patient's prothrombin time into a standardized value so results remain comparable regardless of which laboratory's reagents were used. This standardization is essential for patients on long-term warfarin, since dosing decisions depend on trending INR results reliably over time and across different sites. Explaining this clearly helps a trainee understand why INR, rather than raw prothrombin time alone, is the preferred reporting value. INR specimens are drawn in the same sodium citrate tube used for other coagulation testing.
  19. The correct answer is A (A misread abbreviation can cause the wrong tube, prep, or timing, an error nothing downstream can fix): a pre-analytical mistake, such as drawing the wrong tube or missing a fasting requirement because of a misread abbreviation, cannot be corrected later in the testing process no matter how carefully the lab performs its analysis. This is why clarifying an unclear requisition, even under real time pressure, is always worth the brief pause. Explaining the real-world consequence rather than just stating the rule helps a new hire internalize why it genuinely matters. Patient safety depends on getting this first step right every single time, without exception.
  20. The correct answer is B (An abnormally high level of potassium currently circulating within the blood): the prefix "hyper-" means excessive, and "kalemia" refers to potassium levels in the blood, so hyperkalemia describes dangerously elevated potassium that can affect cardiac rhythm. Recognizing this term as a critical value helps the phlebotomist understand why immediate notification protocols exist for results like this one. Explaining the term accurately also reinforces how prefix and root combinations reveal the meaning of an unfamiliar word without a reference guide. Hyperkalemia results are often confirmed with a repeat draw to rule out hemolysis as a false cause before treatment begins.
  21. Recognizing the word parts helps a phlebotomist explain to a colleague why a rough draw or excessive mixing risks rejection. The other options combine word parts that do not build this term. Word-part fluency lets staff decode unfamiliar terminology on the fly rather than memorizing every combination separately.
  22. This directional vocabulary appears constantly on requisitions and care notes. The remaining options describe medial, posterior, or contralateral positioning, which are different anatomical relationships entirely. Confusing these terms could lead a phlebotomist to select a compromised draw site.
  23. Misreading either abbreviation could delay urgent results or produce a specimen collected under the wrong fasting conditions. The other options describe routine timing or the opposite dietary status. Correctly decoding these two abbreviations together is routine work on a busy inpatient floor.

Recognizing QNS on a callback helps a phlebotomist quickly understand why a redraw is being requested.   61. The correct answer is B (Routine practices treat every patient's blood as potentially infectious regardless of history): a prior negative result cannot rule out a window period infection or an exposure since the last test. Canadian routine practices require the same barrier precautions for every patient, every time, without relying on disclosed history. This protects both the phlebotomist and future patients from any gap in known infection status. Consistent glove use removes the need to make a risk judgment case by case.
  62. The correct answer is D (Gloves must be changed and hand hygiene performed between every patient): gloves are a single-use barrier and must be replaced along with hand hygiene between every patient regardless of appearance, since contamination is often invisible. A visual check alone risks transmitting organisms between patients undetected. Reusing gloves based on how clean they look ignores the actual purpose of the barrier. Fresh gloves and hand hygiene at every patient encounter is the expected standard without exception.
  63. The correct answer is A (Washing hands with soap and water since alcohol rub does not kill spores): C. difficile forms spores that alcohol-based hand rub cannot reliably destroy, so mechanical friction and rinsing from soap and water are required. This is one of the specific exceptions to routine alcohol-rub use in Canadian infection control guidance. Skipping this step risks spreading spores to the next patient or surface touched. Soap and water should also be used whenever hands are visibly soiled.
  64. The correct answer is C (The Safety Data Sheet maintained on site under the WHMIS 2015 system): WHMIS 2015, Canada's GHS-aligned hazard communication system, requires that every hazardous chemical used in a workplace have an accessible Safety Data Sheet detailing hazards, handling, and first aid. Phlebotomists are obligated to know where SDS documents are kept and how to reference them for chemicals like antiseptics they use daily. Pictograms on the product label also flag hazards such as flammability at a glance. Relying on memory or informal sources does not meet this workplace safety obligation.
  65. The correct answer is B (Reposition the collection setup or move closer so disposal happens at point of use): Canadian occupational health and safety principles require immediate disposal of used sharps into a puncture-resistant container at the point of use, with no recapping, bending, or carrying an exposed needle elsewhere. If the container is not within reach, the phlebotomist should adjust position rather than delay disposal or transport an uncapped needle. Recapping and setting the needle down both create unnecessary exposure risk. Workstations should be arranged in advance so a sharps container is always accessible during collection.
  66. The correct answer is D (Wash the wound with soap and water, then report it to a supervisor immediately): immediate wound care with soap and water reduces contamination without squeezing or sucking the site, since neither action reduces infection risk. Prompt reporting to a supervisor initiates timely occupational health follow-up, which may include assessment for post-exposure prophylaxis. Delaying the report, even informally, can reduce the effectiveness of any indicated follow-up care. Every needlestick injury must be treated as urgent regardless of how minor it appears.
  67. The correct answer is C (A surgical mask and eye protection worn at close patient contact): droplet precautions require a surgical mask and eye protection when working within close range of a patient, since respiratory droplets can reach mucous membranes during close contact such as venipuncture. This differs from airborne precautions, which require a fit-tested N95 respirator and a negative pressure room for pathogens like tuberculosis or measles. Relying on gloves alone would leave the phlebotomist's respiratory and mucosal surfaces unprotected. The correct PPE level is determined by the specific transmission route of the confirmed or suspected pathogen.
  68. The correct answer is A (A gown and gloves, since contact precautions address organisms spread by touch): contact precautions layer a gown on top of the gloves already used under routine practices, protecting clothing and skin from organisms like MRSA or C. difficile that spread through direct or indirect touch. MRSA is not an airborne or droplet pathogen, so respirators and eye protection are not the primary additions for this precaution level. Skipping the gown risks carrying the organism on clothing to surfaces or the next patient. Matching PPE to the specific transmission route is essential for effective infection control.
  69. The correct answer is D (Blood-stained gauze and tourniquet in biohazard waste, packaging in general waste): correct waste segregation keeps biohazardous materials like blood-contaminated gauze and single-use tourniquets separate from general waste such as clean packaging. Mixing categories creates unnecessary infection risk and violates regulatory waste handling requirements for clinical sites. Sharps such as needles belong in a dedicated puncture-resistant container, distinct from soft biohazard waste. Correct sorting at the point of disposal protects downstream handling staff and supports proper waste management.
  70. The correct answer is B (The disinfectant's contact time, which must remain wet to kill organisms): most disinfectants require a specified contact time during which the surface must stay visibly wet for the product to effectively kill microorganisms. Wiping the surface dry immediately after application interrupts this process and leaves organisms potentially viable on the armrest. This practice, though it saves time, undermines the entire purpose of disinfecting between patients. Staff should follow the manufacturer's stated contact time rather than judging by appearance alone.
  71. The correct answer is A (Yes, any exposure or near-miss should be reported promptly and accurately): even exposures that appear minor, such as a splash to intact skin, should be reported promptly because the professional and legal obligation covers any exposure or near-miss, not only confirmed high-risk events. Prompt reporting supports both individual follow-up care if warranted and accurate workplace safety data over time. Underreporting, even of seemingly minor events, undermines the site's ability to identify patterns and prevent future incidents. Waiting or skipping the report is inconsistent with occupational health obligations.
  72. The correct answer is C (Repositioning equipment and the patient's arm to allow a neutral posture): ergonomic principles for repetitive phlebotomy tasks focus on positioning the patient, chair, and supplies so the phlebotomist can maintain a neutral spine and shoulder posture rather than twisting or reaching. Working faster or reducing breaks would increase strain rather than reduce it. Alternating hands does not address the underlying postural cause of the strain. Adjusting the physical setup addresses the root ergonomic issue directly.
  73. The correct answer is D (Stop using the equipment and report the concern following site protocol): a burning odour near electrical equipment is a recognized warning sign that should prompt immediate discontinuation of use and reporting through the site's safety process.
