Resuming use once the smell fades does not address a possible underlying electrical fault, which could recur or worsen. Spraying liquid near an outlet introduces additional electrical and fire hazards. General fire and electrical safety awareness in a clinical setting requires treating such signs seriously rather than dismissing them.
  74. The correct answer is B (Before patient contact, to prevent transferring organisms to the patient): the four moments of hand hygiene identify specific points in care where hand hygiene protects patients and staff, and hand rub applied before touching a new patient's arm falls under the before patient contact moment. This step is distinct from before a clean or aseptic procedure, which applies more specifically to the venipuncture site itself once it has been prepared. Performing hand hygiene at this moment reduces the risk of transferring organisms from the phlebotomist to the patient. Recognizing which moment applies helps ensure no step in the sequence is skipped.
  75. The correct answer is C (Hand hygiene performed specifically before starting the clean procedure itself): the moment before a clean or aseptic procedure requires hand hygiene immediately before an activity like venipuncture that involves entering a normally sterile space such as a blood vessel. This step is separate from the earlier before patient contact moment and must not be skipped even though gloves will also be worn. Gloves alone do not replace the need for hand hygiene, since donning gloves over unclean hands still risks contamination. Deferring this step until after the procedure defeats its purpose of preventing infection during the draw.
  76. The correct answer is A (Yes, hand hygiene after patient contact applies regardless of glove use): the after patient contact moment applies whenever contact with the patient or their immediate environment has occurred, including incidental contact after glove removal, not only when body fluids are visibly involved. Performing hand hygiene at this point prevents any organisms picked up during the encounter from being carried forward. Waiting to see if the surface looks soiled ignores that many organisms are not visible. This moment should be treated as routine after every patient interaction rather than conditional on other factors.
  77. The correct answer is D (PPE should not differ, since routine practices apply equally to both): routine practices are designed specifically so that PPE use does not depend on known or documented infection status, since status is often unknown or a patient may be in an infectious window period despite negative testing. Applying the same standard gloves and technique to every patient avoids inconsistent protection based on incomplete information. Treating an undocumented patient as higher risk, or a documented patient as requiring extra measures, misapplies the underlying principle. Consistency across all patients is the core strength of routine practices.
  78. The correct answer is B (Pictograms communicate specific hazards at a glance under WHMIS 2015): under WHMIS 2015, Canada's GHS-aligned system, hazard pictograms are a mandatory part of chemical labelling that quickly convey risks such as flammability or skin irritation even for products that seem routine, like alcohol-based antiseptics. This labelling supports staff awareness without requiring them to consult the full Safety Data Sheet for every quick reference. Pictogram requirements apply based on the chemical's actual hazard classification, not its country of origin or storage location. Recognizing these symbols is part of a phlebotomist's obligation to understand the hazards of chemicals used on the job.
  79. The correct answer is A (A fit-tested N95 respirator along with a negative pressure room): airborne precautions, used for pathogens like tuberculosis or measles that can remain suspended in air over distance, require a fit-tested N95 respirator combined with a negative pressure room to prevent transmission. A surgical mask and eye protection are appropriate for droplet precautions but do not provide adequate protection against airborne transmission. Gowns and gloves alone address contact transmission and are insufficient here on their own. Matching the specific PPE and environmental controls to the transmission route is essential for staff safety in this scenario.
  80. The correct answer is C (Both should be reported promptly, since near-misses reveal risk factors too): near-misses, where an exposure was narrowly avoided, still carry valuable information about workplace hazards and should be reported through the same formal process as actual exposures. Treating only confirmed exposures as reportable ignores the preventive value of identifying near-miss patterns before an actual injury occurs. Informal discussion without documentation fails to feed into the site's safety data and improvement process. Prompt, accurate reporting of both event types supports a stronger overall safety culture.
  81. The correct answer is D (Disposal of sharps directly at point of use with no intermediate step): correct sharps safety requires immediate disposal into a puncture-resistant container at the point of use, without recapping, bending, breaking, or setting the item down temporarily. Demonstrating this behaviour to a new hire reinforces the strict Canadian occupational health and safety principle against any intermediate handling step. Accumulating sharps on a tray before disposal increases the chance of an accidental injury during transport. This point-of-use disposal habit should be modelled consistently from the very first training session.
  82. The correct answer is B (Address the practice, since recapping used needles is never an accepted step): recapping a used needle is prohibited under strict sharps safety principles regardless of technique, including two-handed recapping, because any recapping attempt introduces unnecessary needlestick risk. Observing this practice should prompt a respectful correction rather than silence, since infection control is a shared workplace responsibility, not an individual preference. Waiting for an actual injury before speaking up ignores the preventable nature of the hazard. Reinforcing correct sharps disposal habits among coworkers supports overall site safety.
  83. The correct answer is A (Increased infection risk and a regulatory violation from waste mis-sorting): mixing biohazard and general waste due to poor labelling or convenient placement creates real infection risk for downstream handlers and violates regulatory requirements for waste segregation at clinical sites. Assuming both streams are handled identically ignores the different processing, transport, and disposal requirements for biohazardous material. This is not a minor issue or a cost benefit, since improper segregation can result in regulatory penalties and genuine exposure risk. Clear labelling and consistent bin placement are needed to prevent this kind of mis-sorting.
  84. The correct answer is C (Visible cleanliness does not rule out microbial contamination on surfaces): equipment and surfaces can carry microorganisms even when no blood or visible soiling is present, so routine disinfection between every patient is necessary regardless of appearance. Reducing the frequency to once per shift or only after an observed spill would leave contaminated surfaces in contact with multiple patients unnecessarily. Antiseptic swabs used on the patient's skin do not substitute for disinfecting the shared equipment and tray. Consistent between-patient disinfection is a core infection control practice independent of visible cues.
  85. The correct answer is D (Infectious status is frequently unknown and testing can miss a window period): routine practices are applied to every patient because infection status is often unknown at the time of collection, and a patient may be within a window period where an infection is present but not yet detectable by testing. This reasoning has nothing to do with reducing PPE costs or site size, which are not the basis for the standard. Patients with known infections still require venipuncture regularly for monitoring and treatment, so this is not a driver of the practice either. Universal application removes the need to guess which patients pose a risk.
  86. The correct answer is B (No, since the required contact time must elapse before further use): correct disinfection technique requires the surface to remain wet for the manufacturer's specified contact time before the item is used again, since killing organisms depends on that duration rather than simple application. Moving the tray into immediate use while wet interrupts this process and may leave organisms viable. Reusable trays absolutely should be disinfected between patients, so avoiding disinfection altogether is not an acceptable alternative. Staff should build the contact time into their workflow rather than treating disinfection as an instantaneous step.
  87. The correct answer is C (Gloves changed between every patient regardless of precaution status): gloves are the minimum PPE standard for every venipuncture performed at the site, changed between each patient, before any additional transmission-based precautions such as gowns, masks, or eye protection are layered on for specific patients. A mask worn continuously or a gown worn for every draw exceeds the baseline and is only appropriate when a specific precaution level requires it. Eye protection tied only to paediatric patients misunderstands the actual basis for adding that equipment, which is transmission route rather than patient age. Establishing gloves as the universal minimum keeps the baseline consistent across all patients.
  88. The correct answer is A (Retrieve the needle immediately using appropriate care and place it in the sharps container): a sharp placed in general waste must be corrected promptly, since general waste handlers are not equipped or expecting sharps exposure, creating real downstream injury risk. Leaving the needle for end-of-day removal or relying on the cap for protection does not meet the standard for sharps segregation and can still result in an injury during handling. A warning note does not remove the actual hazard from the waste stream. Immediate, careful correction restores proper segregation and protects everyone who handles the waste afterward.
  89. The correct answer is D (Accurate reporting of all incidents supports individual care and safety data): documenting even seemingly minor events, like a torn glove without direct blood contact, supports both potential individual follow-up and the accuracy of the site's overall occupational safety data. Limiting documentation to confirmed pathogen exposures would miss valuable information about equipment failures or process gaps that could lead to more serious incidents later. Treating reporting as a paperwork quota misrepresents its actual purpose in workplace safety. Consistent, prompt reporting regardless of perceived severity is the professional and legal expectation.
  90. The correct answer is B (Report the discomfort and review positioning and technique to reduce strain): repetitive strain symptoms should be reported so that ergonomic factors, such as posture and hand positioning during stabilization, can be reviewed and adjusted to reduce ongoing risk. Simply switching hands without addressing the underlying technique may only shift the strain rather than resolve it. Dismissing the discomfort as unavoidable overlooks the preventable nature of many repetitive strain injuries in phlebotomy work. A brace alone, without addressing technique, treats a symptom rather than the contributing cause.
  91. The correct answer is A (Transmission-based precautions are layered on top of routine practices as needed): transmission-based precautions, such as contact, droplet, or airborne precautions, add specific protective measures on top of routine practices rather than replacing them for a given patient. Routine practices, including baseline gloves and hand hygiene, remain in effect throughout the encounter regardless of any additional precaution level. Suggesting that routine practices only apply after precautions end misrepresents how the two systems function together. Understanding this layered relationship helps new staff correctly select PPE for each specific patient situation.
  92. The correct answer is C (Safely stop the draw, secure the site, then follow the site's evacuation procedure): general fire safety awareness in a clinical setting requires safely discontinuing the procedure, removing the needle and applying appropriate site care, before proceeding with the evacuation plan rather than abandoning a needle in the patient or ignoring the alarm outright. Waiting for a second confirmation delays action unnecessarily during a genuine emergency signal. A calm but prompt response balances patient safety during the procedure with the need to evacuate. Sites should train staff in advance on how to safely interrupt a draw during an emergency.
  93. The correct answer is D (Alcohol rub is faster and effective for most organisms when hands are not soiled): alcohol-based hand rub is generally preferred for routine hand hygiene because it is quick to use and effective against most organisms encountered in typical patient contact, provided hands are not visibly soiled. Soap and water remain necessary in specific situations, such as visibly soiled hands or spore-forming organism precautions, so they are not inappropriate for the setting overall. Alcohol rub is specifically not sufficient for spore-forming organisms, which is the opposite of the required exception. Skin damage comparisons are not the primary basis for the general preference for alcohol rub in routine care.
  94. The correct answer is B (Prompt reporting enables timely occupational health assessment and consistent data): requiring immediate reporting rather than individual discretion ensures every sharps injury receives timely occupational health assessment, which can be time-sensitive for certain follow-up measures. Relying on individual judgment risks inconsistent decisions about which injuries warrant follow-up, undermining both patient and staff safety. The requirement exists to protect health and generate reliable workplace safety data, not to evaluate individual staff performance. Delayed reporting can genuinely reduce the effectiveness of time-sensitive follow-up care, contrary to the idea that timing does not matter.
  95. The correct answer is A (Removing PPE in the correct order reduces the risk of self-contamination): a specific PPE removal sequence, typically gloves before gown before other items, is designed to minimize the chance that the phlebotomist's hands or skin contact a contaminated outer surface during removal. This principle applies broadly across precaution types, not only to airborne precautions, since contact-transmitted organisms present the same self-contamination risk during doffing. Treating the order as personal preference ignores the deliberate design behind standard doffing sequences. Hand hygiene at the end is important but does not eliminate the value of following the correct removal order beforehand.
  96. The correct answer is D (Infection status is frequently unknown and a patient can test negative during a window period): routine practices treat every patient's blood and body fluids as potentially infectious because a diagnosis may not yet be documented or a recently acquired infection may not show up on testing. This protects both the phlebotomist and future patients from an unrecognized exposure. Relying on chart history alone leaves a dangerous gap that routine practices are specifically designed to close. It is a foundational Canadian infection control principle applied consistently, not selectively.
  97. The correct answer is A (Each moment targets a distinct transmission risk point, so entry and pre-contact are separate triggers): the 4 moments framework identifies specific points where pathogen transfer risk changes, including before patient contact and before a clean or aseptic procedure. Entering a room and then touching a patient are treated as separate triggers because contamination can occur between them from surfaces or equipment. Skipping either step leaves a genuine gap in the chain of protection. This is why each moment is assessed independently rather than bundled together for convenience.
  98. The correct answer is C (Leave the bedside station and wash hands with soap and water at the nearest sink): alcohol-based hand rub is not effective on visibly soiled hands because organic material can shield organisms from the alcohol and reduce its action. Soap and water mechanically removes debris along with organisms and is the required choice in this situation. Wiping soiling off with a dry towel does not achieve the same level of cleaning as an actual wash. This distinction is a specific, testable exception to the usual preference for alcohol-based rub.
  99. The correct answer is A (Alcohol-based rub does not reliably kill spore-forming organisms like C. difficile): spores resist alcohol and require the mechanical friction and rinsing action of soap and water to be physically removed from the skin. This is why C. difficile precautions specifically call for soap and water rather than the usual alcohol-based rub. The dispenser's location is not the issue, and glove removal never eliminates the need for hand hygiene afterward. Recognizing this exception prevents a false sense of protection after contact with a spore-forming organism.
  100. The correct answer is B (Gloves are the minimum standard for venipuncture and must be changed between every patient): a fresh pair of gloves for each patient prevents cross-contamination even when no visible soiling is present, since microorganisms transfer without being seen. The supervisor's cost-saving approach directly contradicts recognized infection control standards and puts patients at risk. Torn or contaminated gloves must obviously be changed immediately, but that is not the only trigger. Following documented practice rather than informal shortcuts protects both staff and patients.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The Safety Data Sheet maintained on-site for that specific product): the Safety Data Sheet is the standardized WHMIS 2015 document that details a chemical's hazards, safe handling, and first aid measures, and employers are required to keep it accessible to staff. Marketing material and general internet searches are not authoritative or standardized sources for occupational hazard information. Every phlebotomist has an obligation to know how to locate and interpret the SDS for chemicals used on the job. This is a core WHMIS competency for anyone handling antiseptics and disinfectants regularly.
  2. The correct answer is B (Consult the Safety Data Sheet or a supervisor to confirm what the pictogram indicates): WHMIS pictograms convey specific hazard classes, and an unfamiliar symbol should prompt verification rather than assumption. Checking the current SDS or asking a knowledgeable supervisor ensures the product is handled with the correct precautions. Ignoring the change or discarding the bottle without reporting could leave a genuine hazard unaddressed for other staff. Staying current on hazard communication is an ongoing professional responsibility, not a one-time training event.
  3. The correct answer is C (Dispose of the needle into the sharps container immediately before responding to the call): used needles must be disposed of directly into a puncture-resistant sharps container at the point of use, with no delay and no intermediate handling. Setting it down, recapping, or passing it to someone else all introduce unnecessary sharps injury risk. This principle holds even when an urgent interruption occurs, because the momentary delay of disposing properly is far safer than carrying an exposed needle elsewhere. No competing task overrides this immediate disposal requirement.
  4. The correct answer is D (Wash the wound immediately with soap and water without squeezing it): immediate first aid calls for washing the site thoroughly with soap and water, since squeezing or sucking the wound is discouraged and offers no proven protective benefit. This should happen right away rather than being delayed for convenience. After washing, the injury must be reported promptly and occupational health follow-up initiated. Recognizing what NOT to do, such as squeezing, is as important as knowing the correct first step.
  5. The correct answer is A (Report the injury to a supervisor promptly regardless of how minor it appears): prompt reporting triggers timely occupational health follow-up, which may include assessment of the source patient and post-exposure options. Delaying or skipping the report because the injury seems small removes the opportunity for early intervention that can matter significantly. Private documentation with no notification defeats the purpose of a reporting system meant to protect the worker. This professional and legal obligation applies to every exposure, injury, and near-miss.
  6. The correct answer is B (A fit-tested N95 respirator and a negative pressure room for airborne precautions): tuberculosis spreads via airborne droplet nuclei that remain suspended and travel beyond close range, requiring a fit-tested respirator and a room engineered to prevent air escaping into the corridor. A surgical mask alone does not provide the filtration needed against airborne particles of this size. Gowns and gloves address contact risk but do not address the airborne transmission route at all. Matching the precaution category correctly to the pathogen is essential rather than defaulting to a single standard combination.
  7. The correct answer is D (A surgical mask and eye protection worn at close working distance): influenza is a droplet-transmitted pathogen, so a surgical mask combined with eye protection addresses the respiratory secretions that may be expelled during coughing at close range. An N95 respirator and negative pressure room are reserved for airborne pathogens with a different transmission mechanism, which would be excessive here. Relying on gloves or a gown alone leaves the mucous membrane exposure route unaddressed. Selecting the correct precaution level depends on accurately identifying how the specific pathogen actually spreads.
  8. The correct answer is B (The mask protects against residual droplets in the room and should stay on until clear of that space): under droplet precautions, gloves and gown are removed inside the room where the contamination risk is highest, but the mask stays in place until the phlebotomist has actually left the room and is clear of any residual airborne droplets, then is removed outside with proper hand hygiene to follow. Removing the mask too early, while still inside the room, exposes the phlebotomist to the very droplets the mask was protecting against. Treating PPE order as unimportant increases self-contamination risk considerably. This room-exit timing is a specific, teachable part of droplet-precaution doffing rather than an arbitrary habit.
  9. The correct answer is B (Mixing waste streams creates infection risk and constitutes a regulatory violation): biomedical waste segregation exists specifically because contaminated materials handled or disposed of as general waste expose downstream workers and the public to unnecessary risk. A full bin does not authorize the wrong disposal stream; instead a new bag should be started or a supervisor notified. Gauze visibly soiled with blood clearly meets the criteria for biohazard waste. Proper segregation is both an infection control principle and a legal requirement for the facility.
  10. The correct answer is A (The disinfectant must remain visibly wet on the surface for its stated contact time to work): most disinfectants require a specific dwell time while wet to actually kill target organisms, and drying the surface prematurely interrupts that process before it is complete. Wiping it on and immediately off achieves cosmetic cleanliness without the actual disinfection the product is meant to provide. This contact time requirement is printed on the product label or SDS and must be respected for every use. Rushing this step undermines infection control between patients even when the surface looks clean.
  11. The correct answer is D (Positioning the arm and tube holder to keep the wrist in a neutral, straight alignment): repetitive bent-wrist positioning during frequent draws is a recognized cause of cumulative strain injury, and maintaining a neutral wrist position reduces that stress directly. Needle gauge and draw speed do not address the underlying joint mechanics causing the ache. Alternating hands for the venipuncture itself is not a standard or safe adjustment, since dominant-hand technique is typically maintained for accuracy. Ergonomic positioning is a practical, evidence-based way to reduce repetitive strain over a full shift.
  12. The correct answer is C (Report the odour immediately and avoid using that outlet until it is assessed): a burning smell from electrical equipment is a recognized early warning sign of a fault that can progress to fire or shock hazard, even if the equipment currently seems to work. Prompt reporting allows a qualified person to assess and address the issue before it worsens. Continuing to use the outlet or attempting a personal inspection introduces unnecessary risk without the proper training or authority. General safety awareness in a clinical setting includes recognizing and escalating early hazard signs like this one.
  13. The correct answer is B (Both droplet and contact precautions should be applied to address both risk pathways): when a patient presents with more than one plausible transmission risk, such as respiratory symptoms and unexplained diarrhea, the corresponding precaution categories are layered together rather than choosing only one. This means combining a mask and eye protection with a gown and gloves as appropriate for each pathway. An unconfirmed diagnosis does not lower the need for precautions, since they are applied based on clinical presentation and risk. Recognizing when multiple categories overlap is an important real-world judgment skill.
  14. The correct answer is A (Near-misses should be reported like any actual exposure or injury event): reporting near-misses provides workplace safety data that can reveal patterns before an actual injury occurs, making early reporting genuinely valuable. Treating a near-miss as informal or optional undermines the purpose of the reporting system entirely. Waiting for a repeat occurrence delays the chance to correct a hazard proactively. Underreporting of any kind, including near-misses, weakens both individual awareness and site-wide safety improvement efforts.
  15. The correct answer is D (It increases sharps handling risk and is inconsistent with point-of-use disposal principles): moving a container between rooms introduces extra handling, transport, and opportunity for accidental contact with its contents, undermining the entire purpose of point-of-use disposal. Each collection area should have its own properly mounted or positioned sharps container rather than relying on a shared portable one. Fill level does not change the underlying handling risk of the practice itself. Point-of-use disposal is a consistent principle regardless of care setting.
  16. The correct answer is B (Speak up immediately, since bending used needles is prohibited regardless of container status): bending, breaking, or otherwise manipulating a used needle is strictly prohibited in Canadian occupational health and safety practice because it significantly raises the risk of an accidental puncture. A full-looking container should prompt replacement rather than manipulation of the sharp itself. Delaying the conversation or assuming an unofficial exception exists both allow a genuine hazard to continue unaddressed. Colleagues share responsibility for maintaining safe sharps handling in real time.
  17. The correct answer is C (Whether the disinfectant remained on the surface for its labeled contact time): the effectiveness of any disinfectant depends on maintaining a wet contact period long enough for the product to work, which the label or SDS specifies. A quick single wipe that dries immediately may leave organisms viable even though the surface appears clean. Visibility of the original spill and cloth colour coding do not determine microbial kill, only proper procedure does. Confirming contact time is met is the meaningful safety check here.
  18. The correct answer is A (Additional PPE is added when a patient is under a specific transmission-based precaution): gloves are the consistent minimum for venipuncture under routine practices, but items like gowns, masks, and eye protection are layered on when a documented contact, droplet, or airborne precaution applies. This tiered approach matches the level of protection to the actual transmission risk present. Treating gloves as always sufficient ignores situations where a known pathogen requires more, and treating PPE choice as purely personal preference removes the clinical basis for the decision. Understanding this tiering is essential for correct daily practice.
  19. The correct answer is D (Report the exposure accurately and promptly regardless of the affected body site): any blood or body fluid exposure, including a skin splash without penetration, should be reported accurately so that appropriate follow-up and workplace safety data can be captured. Limiting reporting to mucous membrane exposures alone would leave real incidents undocumented. A verbal mention to a coworker does not fulfill the formal reporting obligation expected of the phlebotomist. Complete and prompt reporting protects both the individual and the broader safety record.
  20. The correct answer is A (Gloves can have undetectable microtears and hands may become contaminated during removal): gloves are not a perfect barrier, and hands can become contaminated during the doffing process itself even when a tear is never noticed. This is precisely why hand hygiene remains one of the 4 moments regardless of whether gloves were worn. Treating gloves as a full substitute for hand hygiene contradicts established infection control principles at all times, not only during outbreaks. Staffing pressure does not change the underlying microbiological risk involved.
  21. The correct answer is A (The product's flammability and the need to keep it away from ignition sources): a flame pictogram under WHMIS 2015 indicates a flammable hazard class, which is directly relevant to isopropyl alcohol and should inform safe storage away from heat or open flame. It has no connection to expiry dating, sharps compatibility, or skin suitability. Recognizing hazard pictograms accurately allows a phlebotomist to handle common workplace chemicals with appropriate caution. This is a practical, everyday application of WHMIS knowledge on the job.
  22. The correct answer is B (Before touching the patient or beginning any part of the clean prep procedure): hand hygiene is triggered before patient contact and again before a clean or aseptic procedure such as site prep, meaning gathering supplies from a cart does not itself require it. The relevant moment arrives once the phlebotomist is about to touch the patient or begin the aseptic steps of the draw. Waiting until the entire procedure is finished misses the moments meant to prevent transmission during the encounter itself. Personal judgment about contamination is not a substitute for following the defined triggers.
  23. The correct answer is D (Container placement should support immediate, direct disposal at the point of use): sharps containers should be positioned so a used needle can be deposited immediately without extra movement, reaching, or repositioning that increases injury risk. A container on the floor beside the chair may require an awkward motion that works against safe, direct disposal. This principle applies across all patient populations and collection settings, not only specific ones. Thoughtful placement is a practical extension of the no-recapping, point-of-use disposal rule.
  24. The correct answer is B (Overfilled containers increase the chance of a needle protruding or causing injury during use): fill lines exist because a container packed beyond capacity can allow sharps to sit closer to the opening or even protrude, raising the risk of an accidental puncture during subsequent disposals. This is a genuine safety threshold, not an arbitrary suggestion, and closing ability alone does not confirm safe capacity. Treating this only as a cost issue overlooks the direct injury risk it creates for staff. Timely replacement before the fill line is reached is part of standard sharps safety practice.
  25. The correct answer is A (New gloves per patient prevent cross-contamination that visible inspection alone cannot catch): microorganisms transfer on gloved hands without any visible sign of contamination, so a fresh pair per patient is the recognized minimum standard for venipuncture rather than a matter of preference or optics. Supply tracking and patient perception are not the actual clinical reasons behind the practice. Describing it as a courtesy understates its role as an established infection control requirement. Explaining the true rationale helps reinforce patient understanding and trust in the process.
  26. The correct answer is C (A gown and gloves in addition to routine practices for the encounter): MRSA colonization calls for contact precautions, which add a gown and gloves to prevent transfer via skin, clothing, or nearby surfaces, regardless of whether drainage is currently present. An N95 respirator is unnecessary since MRSA is not an airborne pathogen. A surgical mask and eye protection address droplet exposure, which is not the primary concern here. Colonization status alone, not just active wound drainage, is enough to warrant contact precautions.
  27. The correct answer is D (Raise the discrepancy so the workflow can accommodate the labeled contact time): a disinfectant only achieves its intended kill rate when left wet on the surface for its full labeled contact time, so a workflow that shortcuts this leaves surfaces inadequately disinfected despite looking clean. Raising the issue allows the site to adjust scheduling, product choice, or technique appropriately. Silently switching products without proper review or authorization is not an appropriate individual fix. Visible cleanliness is not equivalent to actual disinfection and should not be treated as a reliable substitute.
  28. The correct answer is B (Follow-up including possible post-exposure assessment should be initiated promptly regardless of symptoms): occupational health follow-up after a sharps injury is designed to occur before symptoms would even be expected to appear, since early assessment and possible intervention are most effective when started promptly. Waiting for symptoms defeats the purpose of the follow-up system entirely. Framing follow-up as optional understates its role in worker protection after a genuine exposure. Wound care and documentation are necessary first steps but do not replace the follow-up process itself.
  29. The correct answer is C (She should confirm and follow the current site's specific colour and bin conventions): colour coding for waste segregation can vary between facilities and jurisdictions, so a phlebotomist moving to a new site needs to confirm the local convention rather than assume it matches prior training. Assuming a fixed universal standard exists, or treating colour as unimportant, both risk misdirected waste and a regulatory violation. Requesting a facility-wide change is not a reasonable personal fix for a new employee's orientation gap. Accurate segregation depends on correctly identifying the actual bins in use at each specific site.
  30. The correct answer is C (It protects against undiagnosed or unknown infection status present in any patient): routine practices exist because infection status is frequently unknown, recently acquired, or not yet reflected in test results, so applying the same baseline precautions to every patient closes that gap reliably. Supply ordering convenience and documentation compliance are incidental effects, not the actual clinical justification. Consistent application also does not remove the value of reviewing a chart, since additional transmission-based precautions may still be layered on when known. Understanding the real rationale reinforces why the practice cannot be applied selectively.
  31. The correct answer is C (Do not draw from that arm; notify the nurse to arrange an alternative): a dialysis fistula or graft is a surgically created, fragile vascular access that supplies life-sustaining treatment, and an untrained phlebotomist risks damaging it or causing bleeding, infection, or thrombosis. This arm is never used for routine venipuncture regardless of how difficult other sites appear. Facility policy typically requires a trained specialist or an alternate site such as the opposite arm or a capillary sample. Reporting the situation to the care team ensures the patient's access is protected for future dialysis sessions.
  32. The correct answer is A (Use the right arm for the current venipuncture instead): a hematoma indicates blood has leaked into surrounding tissue, and drawing through or near it risks contaminating the specimen with old blood and causing patient discomfort. Selecting an unaffected site on the opposite arm is the preferred approach whenever one is available. Only when no other site exists should the phlebotomist consider drawing distal to the hematoma, and even then extra caution is needed. Choosing an alternate arm also protects the integrity of laboratory results that depend on a clean venipuncture.
  33. The correct answer is B (Prefer the unaffected left arm whenever an alternative exists): removal of axillary lymph nodes disrupts normal lymphatic drainage on that side, creating an ongoing risk of lymphedema and infection even years after surgery. Venipuncture, tourniquet pressure, and needle puncture on the affected side can trigger or worsen swelling and increase infection risk. When a suitable alternative site is available, it should be used instead of the mastectomy side. If both arms are affected, the phlebotomist should consult the ordering provider or facility policy before proceeding.
  34. The correct answer is C (Draw distal to the IV, discard per protocol, and document it): when no alternate site exists, drawing below the IV site minimises dilution from infused fluid, but the facility's discard volume protocol must still be followed to remove fluid-contaminated blood from the tubing before collecting the actual sample. Documenting that the specimen was collected from an IV-side limb allows the laboratory to interpret unusual results appropriately. Drawing above the insertion point or through the running line would introduce infusate directly into the specimen and invalidate results. This approach balances patient need against specimen accuracy.
  35. The correct answer is C (A more rigorous chlorhexidine or two-step protocol with longer contact time): blood culture bottles are meant to grow any organism present, so skin flora introduced during collection can cause a false-positive result and lead to unnecessary antibiotic treatment for the patient. A chlorhexidine-based or two-step iodine and alcohol protocol, applied with a longer contact and dry time than routine venipuncture, greatly reduces this risk. Facility policy specifies the exact agent and timing to follow. Skipping or shortening this step compromises the diagnostic value of the culture.
  36. The correct answer is A (Wipe outward from the centre in a circular motion and let air dry): moving from the intended puncture point outward in expanding circles carries contaminants away from the site rather than back across it. Allowing the alcohol to air dry completely, rather than blowing on it or wiping it off, ensures the antiseptic has had time to work and prevents a stinging sensation on puncture. Re-palpating the cleaned site with an ungloved finger would reintroduce skin flora and defeat the purpose of the preparation. This routine technique is faster than the blood culture protocol but still protects specimen integrity.
  37. The correct answer is B (About 7 to 10 centimetres above the site): placing the flat strap this distance above the intended venipuncture point allows enough venous engorgement to make the vein palpable and visible without restricting arterial flow to the hand. Positioning it directly over the site would interfere with needle placement and cleaning, while placing it too far away, such as near the shoulder, reduces its effectiveness at distending the local vein. The quick-release flat buckle design shown allows rapid removal once the needle is in the vein and blood flow is confirmed. Correct placement also reduces patient discomfort during the brief period the tourniquet is applied.
  38. The correct answer is A (The medial or lateral plantar surface of the heel): these areas have adequate soft tissue depth over the underlying bone and avoid the major nerves, tendons, and arteries that run through the central and posterior portions of the infant's foot. The curved back of the heel and the very centre of the heel pad sit too close to the calcaneus and carry a higher risk of bone injury. Previously punctured sites should be avoided because they may be infected, scarred, or bruised, which affects both sample quality and infant comfort. Choosing the correct plantar surface protects the infant while still yielding an adequate capillary sample.
  39. The correct answer is A (Striking the calcaneus and risking osteomyelitis): an infant's heel bone lies close to the skin surface, and a puncture that exceeds the recommended maximum depth can penetrate into the bone itself, introducing bacteria and risking a serious bone infection. This is why heel sticks use a specifically sized retractable lancet rather than a standard adult device. Depth is controlled by both the lancet design and the angle and pressure the technician applies. Following the validated maximum depth for the infant's age and size is essential to prevent this preventable complication.
  40. The correct answer is B (The palmar lateral aspect of the middle or ring finger): this location has fewer nerve endings than the very centre of the fingertip, reducing patient discomfort while still providing adequate blood flow. The thumb and little finger are avoided because of thicker calluses on the thumb and insufficient tissue depth on the little finger. Puncturing dead centre on the pad increases pain and risks hitting the bone in patients with thin fingers. Selecting the correct lateral aspect balances sample adequacy with patient comfort during this brief procedure.
  41. The correct answer is C (Wipe it away and collect subsequent drops for testing): the first drop is often diluted with residual tissue fluid and alcohol from the site preparation, which can skew results for tests sensitive to dilution such as glucose. Wiping it away with a clean gauze and allowing a fresh drop to form ensures the collected sample better reflects the patient's actual blood composition. Excessive squeezing, sometimes called milking, should be avoided because it can introduce tissue fluid and cause hemolysis. This simple step is a routine part of correct capillary collection technique.
  42. The correct answer is D (Apply gentler tourniquet tension and remove tape carefully): aging skin loses collagen and elasticity, making it more prone to bruising, tearing, and skin shearing injuries from both tourniquet pressure and adhesive removal. Reducing tourniquet tension while still achieving adequate venous distension protects fragile capillaries near the surface. Slow, careful tape and bandage removal, sometimes with the skin supported by a free hand, prevents skin tears that are common in this population. These small adjustments significantly reduce complications without compromising the quality of the draw.
  43. The correct answer is A (Avoid blind probing and reassess with a different technique or site): repeatedly moving a needle under the skin in search of a vein that cannot be felt increases the risk of nerve injury, hematoma, and patient pain, and rarely improves success rates. Instead the phlebotomist should reassess using proper palpation technique, consider a different site or arm, or request assistance from a more experienced colleague or a vein-finding device where available. Deeper veins in patients with a larger body habitus may simply require a change in approach rather than force. Patient safety and comfort take priority over completing the draw on the first attempt.
  44. The correct answer is B (Coordinate with the care team about using the existing port): repeated venipuncture on already fragile, chemotherapy-affected veins increases the risk of pain, hematoma, and vein damage, and many facilities prefer using an existing central line or port when one is present and appropriately trained staff are available. Coordinating with the nursing or oncology team ensures the most appropriate and least traumatic access method is chosen for that specific patient and test. Automatically reusing the same peripheral site without assessment can accelerate vein deterioration. This collaborative approach reflects good practice for chronically ill patients with limited venous access.
  45. The correct answer is D (Stop the procedure and position the patient safely to prevent a fall): pallor, sweating, and nausea are classic early signs of a vasovagal reaction, and recognizing them promptly allows the phlebotomist to intervene before the patient loses consciousness. The needle should be removed and the patient reclined, or the head lowered if seated, to restore adequate blood flow to the brain and reduce injury risk from a fall. Continuing the draw or having the patient stand would worsen the reaction and increase danger. The patient should be monitored continuously until fully recovered before being allowed to leave the collection area.
  46. The correct answer is B (Its blade tip automatically retracts after each puncture): the spring-loaded mechanism activated by the trigger platform withdraws the blade back into the housing immediately after use, preventing accidental needlestick injury to the technician or reuse on another patient. This single-use, retractable design is intended for exactly one puncture and is discarded into a sharps container afterward rather than resharpened. A device requiring multiple triggers or leaving an exposed blade would present an unacceptable safety hazard. This engineered safety feature is now standard practice for capillary collection devices in Canadian healthcare settings.
  47. The correct answer is A (Select an alternate vein away from the scarred tissue): scarred or burned tissue can have altered blood flow, reduced sensation, and unpredictable vein integrity underneath, which makes both successful venipuncture and patient comfort less reliable at that location. Choosing a healthy, unaffected vein elsewhere on the same or opposite arm avoids these complications. Alcohol application does not meaningfully change the structural properties of scar tissue and should not be relied upon to make the site suitable. Careful visual and tactile assessment of the intended site is a routine part of proper site preparation.
  48. The correct answer is A (Avoid the bruised area and select a different suitable site): extensive unexplained bruising may indicate recent trauma, a bleeding tendency, or an underlying vascular issue, and puncturing through or near it can worsen the bruising and cause additional patient discomfort. A different, unaffected site should be chosen whenever one is reasonably available. If bruising is extensive on both arms, the situation should be discussed with the requesting clinician before proceeding. Documenting the finding is also important, since unexplained bruising can itself be clinically relevant information for the care team.
  49. The correct answer is C (To increase local blood flow and improve sample collection): gentle warming causes local vasodilation, which increases capillary blood flow to the site and makes it easier to obtain an adequate free-flowing sample without excessive squeezing. It does not provide any numbing or sterilizing effect and has no impact on bone density or hardness. This simple, low-risk step is routinely used before infant heel sticks and before difficult capillary collections generally. Adequate warming, followed by proper antiseptic technique, supports both sample quality and infant comfort.
  50. The correct answer is A (It allows fast, one-handed removal once blood flow is confirmed): the tourniquet should be released as soon as adequate blood flow is established, typically within one minute of application, to prevent hemoconcentration and patient discomfort from prolonged constriction. A quick-release buckle lets the phlebotomist free the arm rapidly using only one hand while the other maintains needle position. Leaving the tourniquet on for the full draw is not recommended practice and can affect certain test results. Prompt release also reduces the risk of bruising at the application site.
  51. The correct answer is A (The site must be completely air dry to be fully effective): isopropyl alcohol needs contact time on the skin to disinfect effectively, and puncturing before it has fully evaporated can carry residual alcohol into the tissue, causing a stinging sensation and potentially affecting certain test results. Wiping the antiseptic off would remove its antimicrobial effect entirely rather than complete it. Routine venipuncture uses a single alcohol application rather than the two-step protocol reserved for blood culture collection. Waiting for full air drying is a quick but essential part of correct technique.
  52. The correct answer is B (A false-positive result from inadequate skin antisepsis): when common skin organisms grow in a culture bottle without matching clinical symptoms, contamination introduced during collection is a far more likely explanation than a true bloodstream infection. This is precisely why blood culture collection requires the more rigorous antiseptic protocol with longer contact time compared with routine venipuncture. A contaminated culture can lead to unnecessary antibiotic treatment and repeat testing, so proper technique at the time of collection is the best prevention. The laboratory and clinical team typically correlate the result with the patient's overall presentation before deciding on treatment.
  53. The correct answer is B (The trigger platform must be pressed firmly against the skin): this contact-activated design releases the spring-loaded blade only when the platform is pressed against the puncture site with adequate pressure, which helps ensure consistent puncture depth and reduces the chance of accidental firing away from the patient. There is no manual reset or battery requirement, since the device is intended for a single use and then discarded. The blade also remains housed until activation and retracts automatically afterward. This design supports both patient safety and consistent sample collection.
  54. The correct answer is D (Anchor the vein firmly below the site before inserting the needle): rolling veins, common in older adults due to reduced surrounding tissue support, tend to move away from the needle unless they are stabilized. Using the thumb to gently pull the skin taut and anchor the vein just below the intended puncture point prevents it from shifting during insertion. Skipping palpation removes valuable information about vein depth and stability. Adjusting needle angle or gauge does not address the underlying mobility issue and does not substitute for proper anchoring technique.
  55. The correct answer is B (Choose a different, unpunctured area of the plantar surface): a previously punctured site may still be inflamed, tender, or carry a higher infection risk even if it looks superficially healed, and reusing it can affect sample quality and cause unnecessary discomfort for the infant. Selecting a fresh area on the medial or lateral plantar surface avoids these issues entirely. Puncturing deeper at the old site would also increase the risk of striking the underlying bone. Rotating sites for infants who require frequent capillary testing is standard practice to protect the developing foot.
  56. The correct answer is B (The thumb and the little finger of either hand): the thumb has thicker, more callused skin that can make an adequate puncture more difficult, while the little finger typically has insufficient tissue depth over the underlying bone, increasing the risk of a painful or unsafe puncture. The middle and ring fingers are generally preferred because they provide a better balance of tissue depth and reduced nerve density. Dominant hand use is not itself a contraindication for fingerstick site selection. Following these established site preferences supports both sample quality and patient comfort.
  57. The correct answer is B (Warm the finger first to improve peripheral circulation): a cold, cyanotic finger indicates reduced peripheral blood flow, which can make it difficult to obtain an adequate free-flowing sample and may require excessive squeezing that risks hemolysis or tissue fluid contamination. Gentle warming before the puncture improves local circulation and sample quality. Tourniquets are not used for fingerstick collection and would not appropriately address poor peripheral perfusion in this location. Selecting a better-perfused finger, after warming, generally gives more reliable results.
  58. The correct answer is A (It should be positioned roughly 7 to 10 centimetres above the site): this placement distance allows venous engorgement sufficient for palpation and successful venipuncture while still permitting adequate arterial inflow to the limb. Wrapping it multiple times or applying excessive tightness increases the risk of patient discomfort and can affect certain test results through hemoconcentration. Placement should always occur before the needle is inserted, not afterward, so the vein can be properly located first. Correct positioning of this device is a foundational step in successful site preparation.
  59. The correct answer is C (Check facility policy and consult the care team about port use): implanted ports typically require specialized training and specific facility authorization to access safely, so the decision to use one instead of a peripheral vein depends on staff competency and institutional policy. Consulting the care team ensures the least traumatic and most appropriate method is chosen for this particular patient and specimen type. Automatically defaulting to peripheral draws ignores the benefit of preserving fragile veins in a chemotherapy patient, while automatically using the port without proper authorization would be unsafe. Coordination between disciplines is the correct approach for patients with both vulnerable veins and existing central access.
  60. The correct answer is B (Continue monitoring briefly to confirm full, sustained recovery): symptoms of a vasovagal episode can sometimes return shortly after they initially appear to resolve, so a short additional period of observation helps confirm the patient is genuinely stable before leaving. Discharging immediately once colour returns risks missing a delayed recurrence that could result in a fall outside the supervised area. Deliberately reapplying the tourniquet to test for recurrence is unnecessary and could trigger another episode. A brief period of rest and reassurance, followed by gradual mobilization, is the safer and more appropriate approach.
  61. The correct answer is A (Rely primarily on careful palpation to locate a suitable vein): when veins are not clearly visible due to increased tissue depth, touch becomes the more reliable assessment tool for locating a vein's path, depth, and direction before inserting the needle. General landmarks alone, without palpation, increase the risk of missed attempts and unnecessary probing. While patient-reported prior success can be useful supporting information, it should not replace direct assessment at the current visit. Excessive tourniquet tightness does not substitute for proper palpation technique and can cause unnecessary discomfort.
  62. The correct answer is B (Because the access requires specialized training to use safely): a fistula or graft is a surgically created vascular pathway essential for a patient's ongoing dialysis treatment, and improper needle placement or technique can damage it, cause serious bleeding, or lead to infection or clot formation. Only staff with specific vascular access training and facility authorization should ever access this site, and routine phlebotomists should use an alternate arm or method instead. This is a patient safety and access preservation issue rather than a simple bleeding time or administrative concern. Protecting this access supports the patient's long-term treatment plan.
  63. The correct answer is B (Excess fluid in the tissue may dilute or contaminate the sample): edema reflects an accumulation of fluid in the surrounding tissue, and drawing through or near an edematous area can allow that fluid to mix with the blood sample, affecting the accuracy of test results. An alternate site without visible swelling should be selected whenever one is available. Edema does not automatically indicate infection and may have several unrelated causes such as positioning or fluid overload. Warming the area is not an appropriate solution and does not resolve the underlying dilution risk.
  64. The correct answer is A (Place it directly into an approved sharps container immediately): because the blade retracts automatically after use, the device is designed for a single puncture and immediate disposal, which protects both the technician and future patients from accidental injury or cross-contamination. Reuse or disinfection for future use defeats the purpose of the single-use retractable design and violates standard infection control practice. Leaving a used sharp on a tray, even briefly, creates an unnecessary needlestick risk to staff. Prompt sharps disposal is a routine and mandatory part of safe capillary collection technique.
  65. The correct answer is B (Note on the requisition that the specimen was drawn from the IV-side limb): because collecting near an IV site carries a residual risk of dilution or contamination even after following discard protocol, flagging this on the requisition allows laboratory staff and clinicians to interpret unusual or borderline results with appropriate caution. Routine labelling alone does not convey this important context. Recording only the time or only the fluid type omits the specific detail that most affects result interpretation. Clear documentation supports patient safety and appropriate clinical decision-making downstream.
  66. The correct answer is D (Select a suitable vein in the unaffected right arm instead): a hematoma is a contraindicated site because the collected blood pool can contaminate the specimen and cause an inaccurate result, and puncturing through it can also be painful and cause further tissue damage. When another site is available, it should always be chosen over a hematoma. If no alternative site exists, the phlebotomist would need to draw distal to the hematoma, never proximal or through it.
  67. The correct answer is B (Tissue fluid can dilute and contaminate the collected specimen): oedema means excess interstitial fluid has accumulated in the tissue, and this fluid can mix with the blood sample as it is drawn, skewing analyte concentrations and producing unreliable laboratory results. An alternate site free of swelling should always be located first. If no alternate limb is accessible, the physician should be consulted before proceeding.
  68. The correct answer is D (The right arm since an unaffected alternative site is available): removal of lymph nodes disrupts normal lymphatic drainage on that side permanently, leaving the limb at ongoing elevated risk for lymphedema and infection from a needle puncture. Because an unaffected arm is available, it should be used instead of the mastectomy side, regardless of how long ago the surgery occurred. The affected arm is only used when genuinely no alternative site exists and with physician awareness.
  69. The correct answer is C (Never draw from that arm without specialized authorization): a thrill and bruit confirm an arteriovenous fistula or graft, a surgically created vascular access essential for the patient's dialysis treatment. An untrained phlebotomist puncturing this vessel risks causing clotting, infection, or permanent damage to a lifeline the patient depends on. This limb should be avoided entirely and the opposite arm used for the routine collection.
  70. The correct answer is A (Draw distal to the IV site and document the IV-side collection): when the arm with the running IV is genuinely unavoidable, the puncture must occur below the infusion point, following any facility discard-volume protocol to clear diluted fluid from the vessel before collection. The phlebotomist must also clearly document that the sample was drawn from the IV-side limb so the requesting clinician can interpret results appropriately. Drawing above the IV site would pull directly through infused fluid and is never acceptable.
  71. The correct answer is D (Avoid the burned area entirely and use the unaffected arm): damaged or healing skin is more prone to infection, pain, and poor healing if punctured, and the underlying vasculature may also be compromised or difficult to assess accurately. Since a healthy alternative site exists, it should always be selected over a compromised area such as a burn, extensive scar tissue, or a visible tattoo. If both arms were affected, the least compromised area with the most intact skin would be chosen instead.
  72. The correct answer is A (Apply alcohol in a circular motion outward then let it air dry): a 70 percent isopropyl alcohol swab is applied starting at the intended puncture point and moving outward in expanding concentric circles, which pushes surface debris and skin flora away from the site rather than back across it. The site must then be allowed to air dry completely, since alcohol needs time to fully disinfect and residual moisture can cause a stinging sensation or haemolysis. Puncturing through wet alcohol also risks a false reading on certain analytes.
  73. The correct answer is C (It requires a more rigorous agent with longer contact and dry time): blood culture collection uses a chlorhexidine-based preparation or a two-step iodine and alcohol protocol per facility policy, with a longer scrub and drying time than a routine draw. This heightened protocol exists because normal skin flora introduced into a culture bottle can grow and produce a false-positive contamination result, potentially leading to unnecessary antibiotic treatment for the patient. The site preparation is therefore never shortened for a culture collection regardless of time pressure.
  74. The correct answer is A (The medial or lateral plantar surface of the heel): these areas have adequate soft tissue depth over the underlying bone and a good capillary bed, making them the safest location for an infant heel puncture. The posterior curvature of the heel sits too close to the calcaneus and carries an unacceptable risk of bone injury. A previous puncture site should also be avoided since the healing tissue can affect specimen quality and increase infection risk.
  75. The correct answer is D (It limits penetration depth to avoid striking the calcaneus bone): infant lancets are manufactured with a fixed, shallow puncture depth appropriate for a newborn's heel because puncturing too deeply can strike the calcaneus and lead to osteomyelitis, a serious bone infection. Choosing the correctly rated device for the patient's age and using the plantar heel surface together keep the puncture within safe tissue depth. The retracting blade also protects against an accidental second puncture or needlestick injury after a single use.
  76. The correct answer is B (The lateral palmar surface of the middle or ring finger): this area has fewer nerve endings than the central pad and a good capillary supply, making it both less painful and reliably productive for the patient. The very center of the fingertip has denser nerve endings and thicker skin, so it is avoided. The thumb and pinky are also avoided because of differences in tissue thickness and, in the thumb's case, a pulse that can be mistaken for a vein.
  77. The correct answer is C (Warm the heel gently to improve local blood flow before puncturing): a cool, pale extremity has reduced peripheral circulation, which can make it difficult to obtain an adequate free-flowing sample and may lead to repeated punctures. Gently warming the heel with an approved warming device for a few minutes dilates the local capillaries and improves blood flow before the puncture. Massaging the site itself instead of warming can introduce tissue fluid and affect specimen accuracy.
  78. The correct answer is C (Use gentler tourniquet tension and remove tape slowly and carefully): aged skin loses collagen and elasticity, becoming fragile and prone to tearing or bruising under normal pressure that a younger patient would tolerate without issue. A gentler tourniquet application reduces the risk of skin injury while still allowing adequate vein distension, and slow, careful bandage removal prevents skin tears. Skipping the tourniquet altogether is unnecessary and can make locating a suitable vein more difficult.
  79. The correct answer is A (Anchor the vein firmly below the site before needle insertion): rolling veins are common in older patients because surrounding tissue support has weakened, allowing the vein to shift away from the needle rather than staying in place. Anchoring the vein by pulling the skin taut below the intended site stabilizes it and reduces the chance of a failed or repeated puncture attempt. A steeper insertion angle or excessive tourniquet tightness does not address the underlying mobility problem and can increase discomfort or bruising risk.
  80. The correct answer is B (Reassess technique, try another site, or request assistance): in patients with a higher body mass, veins may sit deeper beneath adipose tissue and be harder to palpate, but this never justifies probing blindly, which risks nerve injury or an arterial puncture. The safer path is to reassess anatomical landmarks, consider a different arm or site, or ask a more experienced colleague for help. A retractable capillary device is one alternative worth considering when venous access genuinely cannot be safely established.
  81. The correct answer is C (Select a smaller gauge needle and use extra care with technique): repeated venipuncture and certain chemotherapy agents can leave veins fragile, sclerosed, or easily bruised, so a smaller gauge needle combined with gentle handling reduces the chance of vein collapse or a failed collection. Rushing the process or using excessive tourniquet pressure increases the likelihood of causing further trauma to already compromised tissue. Careful site selection and a calm, unhurried approach protect the patient's limited remaining venous access.
  82. The correct answer is A (Coordinate timing and site with the care team rather than a separate draw): patients with an implanted port or central line often have the device accessed for other purposes, so coordinating with nursing or the treating team can avoid an unnecessary additional venipuncture and reduce patient discomfort. Accessing the port itself requires specialized training the phlebotomist may not hold. A standard peripheral draw remains appropriate when clinically necessary, but confirming with the care team first respects the patient's overall treatment plan.
  83. The correct answer is D (These are early warning signs of an impending vasovagal episode): pallor, sweating, nausea, and dizziness are classic prodromal symptoms that commonly precede a fainting episode during or after venipuncture. Recognizing these signs early allows the phlebotomist to intervene before the patient actually loses consciousness or falls. Continuing the draw without addressing these warning signs puts the patient at unnecessary risk of injury.
  84. The correct answer is B (Position the patient safely, such as lying down or head lowered): the immediate priority is preventing a fall, so the phlebotomist should stop the procedure and help the patient into a safer position, either lying flat or with the head lowered between the knees if still seated. Standing and walking would increase the risk of a fall during an episode of reduced blood flow to the brain. The phlebotomist should stay with the patient throughout rather than leaving them unattended.
  85. The correct answer is B (They should be monitored until fully recovered before leaving): symptoms can recur if the patient stands or leaves too soon, so continued observation until they are fully alert, stable, and symptom-free is essential before ending the encounter. A partial return of colour or simply finishing the bandaging does not confirm the patient has genuinely recovered. Rushing the patient out to save time places them at real risk of a fall shortly after leaving the collection area.
  86. The correct answer is D (Draw distal to the hematoma if the vessel remains accessible): when no alternate limb exists, the phlebotomist should locate an accessible vein below the affected area rather than drawing above it or through the compromised tissue itself. Drawing proximal to or through the hematoma risks pulling pooled, altered blood into the specimen and causing patient discomfort. Cancelling the order outright is unnecessary once a genuinely usable distal vessel has been identified.
  87. The correct answer is D (Excess interstitial fluid can dilute and contaminate the specimen): oedematous tissue holds surplus fluid that can mix with the sample during collection, altering analyte concentrations and producing results that do not reflect the patient's true blood chemistry. This is true regardless of whether a vein is still visible, since the surrounding fluid is the concern rather than vein visibility itself. An unaffected limb should be sought whenever one is available.
  88. The correct answer is C (Consult with the ordering provider about the safest available option): when both arms carry lymphedema risk, the situation falls outside the usual straightforward alternate-arm solution and benefits from discussion with the care team about the safest realistic option, which may include a specific limb, a different site, or additional precautions. Simply choosing randomly overlooks the elevated risk both sides carry. Outright refusal is not appropriate once a reasonable, informed plan can be made with the provider.
  89. The correct answer is B (This arm must never be used without specialized authorization): a palpable thrill and audible bruit confirm the presence of a dialysis fistula or graft, a critical vascular access the patient relies on for treatment. Puncturing this vessel without specialized training risks serious complications including clotting or damage to the access itself. The opposite arm, or another approved site, should be used for the routine collection instead.
  90. The correct answer is D (Draw distal to the IV following facility protocol and document it): even with an IV present in both arms, the correct venous approach when unavoidable is to select a site below one of the infusion points, follow the facility's discard-volume protocol to clear diluted fluid, and document that the sample was collected from an IV-side limb. Drawing proximal to the IV or through the line itself would introduce infused fluid directly into the sample. Coordinating with nursing staff about temporarily pausing an infusion may also be appropriate before collection.
  91. The correct answer is A (Use the unaffected arm since a clear alternative site is available): heavily tattooed skin can make vein visualization and needle insertion more difficult and may carry a theoretical infection or healing risk depending on how recent the tattoo is. Since an unmarked, clearly accessible arm is available, it is the more straightforward and lower-risk choice for the collection. The tattooed arm would only be used if no reasonable alternative existed.
  92. The correct answer is B (A false-positive result from introduced normal skin flora): if bacteria normally living on the skin surface are not adequately removed before puncture, they can be carried into the culture bottle and grow, mimicking a true bloodstream infection. This false signal can lead a clinician to start unnecessary antibiotics and extend a patient's hospital stay unnecessarily. The extended contact and drying time of the chlorhexidine or two-step protocol is specifically intended to minimize this contamination risk.
  93. The correct answer is A (The alcohol has not been given time to fully air dry): allowing the antiseptic to dry completely gives it time to disinfect the site effectively and prevents residual alcohol from causing a stinging sensation on puncture or interfering with certain test results. Puncturing through wet alcohol also risks carrying the antiseptic into the bloodstream along the needle track. The site should always be visibly dry before the needle is inserted.
  94. The correct answer is B (The curvature sits too close to the calcaneus, risking bone injury): the posterior curved surface of the heel has less soft tissue depth over the underlying bone compared with the plantar surfaces, so a puncture there carries a real risk of striking the calcaneus and causing osteomyelitis. The medial or lateral plantar surface offers adequate tissue depth and should always be selected instead. Accessibility during swaddling does not outweigh this safety concern.
  95. The correct answer is A (The center pad has denser nerve endings and is more painful to puncture): the very center of the fingertip contains a higher concentration of nerve endings than the lateral palmar surface, making a puncture there noticeably more uncomfortable for the patient without any real benefit to sample quality. The lateral aspect of the middle or ring finger provides a comparable blood yield with less discomfort. This is why standard technique directs the puncture slightly off-center.
  96. The correct answer is C (Wipe it away since it is diluted with tissue fluid from puncture): the first drop released after a capillary puncture typically contains interstitial fluid mixed in from the surrounding tissue, which can dilute the true blood composition and skew results. Wiping this drop away with clean gauze before collecting subsequent drops ensures a more accurate specimen. There is no need to repeat the puncture at a new site simply because of this expected first drop.
  97. The correct answer is B (Apply it with gentler, less restrictive tension than usual): thin, fragile skin common in elderly patients bruises more readily under standard pressure, so a lighter, less restrictive tension still achieves adequate vein distension while reducing the risk of skin injury. The correct placement distance above the intended site remains the same as for any patient. Completely omitting the strap is unnecessary and would make locating a suitable vein considerably harder.
  98. The correct answer is D (It risks striking a nerve or artery beneath the surface): blindly advancing a needle without a clearly palpated target increases the chance of injuring a nerve or inadvertently entering an artery, both of which can cause significant harm to the patient. Veins in patients with a higher body mass may simply sit deeper, which calls for reassessing landmarks, trying a different site, or seeking assistance rather than probing further. Patient safety always outweighs the desire to complete the collection quickly.
  99. The correct answer is C (The veins may be fragile and require gentle, careful technique): repeated venipuncture over time, sometimes combined with certain treatment effects, can leave veins scarred, fragile, or prone to collapsing under normal pressure. Recognizing this history allows the phlebotomist to select a smaller gauge needle, apply gentler tourniquet tension, and proceed with extra care rather than assuming a standard approach will work as usual. This patient-specific adjustment helps preserve remaining venous access for future treatment needs.
  100. The correct answer is A (Confirm full recovery and stability before ending the encounter): a brief improvement in appearance does not always mean the episode has fully resolved, so the phlebotomist should confirm the patient feels well, is steady, and shows no lingering symptoms before ending the visit. This final check reduces the risk of a delayed fall after leaving the collection area unsupervised. Documentation of the episode and the recovery period should also be completed as part of standard practice.
